**2026-07-22 — Kael FP-arm visibility blocker: root cause was a character-space-vs-camera-space coordinate mismatch, fixed by recentering around the shoulder midpoint rather than by moving the weapon or disabling validation**

Decision: correct the arm-rig mesh/skeleton's effective origin at MOUNT time (once, not per frame) by measuring a shoulder-midpoint anchor and applying it in two places — the cached IK rest-metrics AND the mesh instance's own position — rather than any of the alternatives considered below.

Reason: manual browser testing reported Kael's arms mounted, IK "running" (debug markers/pole-line/weapon all rendered), but the arm MESH itself invisible in both `/v2/range` and `/v2/range?ik=1`. Diagnosed without a browser via a headless trace of the real `operator-kael-arms.glb` using the exact loader/clone functions the runtime uses (`GLTFLoader.parse` + `SkeletonUtils.clone`, run through `tsx` outside any browser). Found: the asset's skeleton is authored in FULL-BODY character space — hips at world y≈1.06m, shoulders at y≈1.52m, hands at y≈0.85-1.05m, matching the body derivative's own documented "feet at origin, 1.83m tall" convention (`docs/forge/operator-pipeline.md` §3) — NOT recentered to a shoulder-relative origin the way a first-person arms asset conventionally would be. `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` measured rest metrics (`measureArmRestMetrics`) with the freshly-cloned skeleton still unparented (identity transform), so the returned "container-relative" positions equaled these raw character-height world coordinates verbatim. At runtime, `solveSide` reprojects `metrics.shoulderLocalPos` through the CAMERA-FOLLOWING container's `matrixWorld` (`shoulderWorldPos = shoulderLocalPos.applyMatrix4(container.matrixWorld)`) — meaning the "shoulder" ended up computed as roughly `camera.position + [x, +1.5, z]`, about 1.5m above the camera, well outside the normal forward view frustum. The mesh's actual RENDERED geometry (skinning is bone-driven, and IK only ever writes bone ROTATIONS, never positions) inherited the exact same displacement from the untouched rest-pose bone chain above the arm (hips→spine→spine1→spine2→shoulder), since nothing had ever repositioned it either.

Rejected: (a) moving the Vortex weapon or its grip anchors to "meet" the arms — explicitly forbidden by the brief, and wrong regardless: the weapon's own transform/grip targets were never the problem, confirmed by the fact that `?ik=1`'s target/pole markers (computed from the SAME grip-pose bridge) rendered in sensible positions near the weapon; only the ARMS' OWN coordinate frame was offset. (b) Disabling frustum culling as the "fix" — already permanently disabled (`node.frustumCulled = false`, set in the original Step 6 pass) and confirmed via the headless trace to be irrelevant here; the mesh's geometry itself sits ~1.5m outside the visible area regardless of culling settings, so toggling culling would have changed nothing. (c) A material override — ruled out by directly reading the real GLB's material properties in the headless trace (`opacity:1, transparent:false, visible:true, side:DoubleSide, depthTest/depthWrite:true` — nothing wrong). (d) Assuming a `SkeletonUtils.clone` bind-matrix bug (a genuinely common cause of "skeleton moves but mesh doesn't follow") — ruled out by explicitly checking in the headless trace that the cloned `SkinnedMesh.skeleton.bones` are NOT the original un-cloned bone objects (confirmed correctly rebound). (e) Recentering by moving the mesh instance's position ALONE, without also correcting the cached metrics — proven insufficient by construction: `measureArmRestMetrics`'s containerInverse math is self-relative to whatever frame the measured-against object has AT MEASUREMENT TIME, so shifting the mesh's position AFTER measuring (or before, in either order alone) leaves the cached "local" values silently unchanged relative to the new frame, reintroducing the exact same downstream error one level removed. (f) Correcting the cached metrics ALONE, without moving the mesh instance — proven insufficient by the same reasoning in the other direction: the IK solver would then compute the CORRECT rotations for an INCORRECT pivot (the arm's actual rendered base, fixed by the untouched rest-pose bone chain, stays 1.5m too high), producing a fully-extended arm reaching from the wrong height toward the right target, still not resembling a held weapon and still likely out of frame.

Chosen: `recenterArmMetrics(left, right)` (new, `kaelArmRig.ts`) computes the anchor (midpoint of the two sides' rest-pose shoulder/upper-arm positions — a stable, symmetric "upper chest" reference requiring no new bone dependency) and subtracts it from both sides' cached `shoulderLocalPos`/`elbowLocalPos`/`handLocalPos`; `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`'s `runtime` useMemo separately applies the SAME anchor, negated, to the cloned mesh instance's own `.position` — the two corrections must use one shared value and must both exist, per the rejected alternatives above. Verified two independent ways without a browser: 6 new unit tests (`kaelArmRig.test.ts`) against a synthetic character-height skeleton (the existing `buildSyntheticSkeleton()` helper already places its hips at y=1, incidentally already shaped like the real bug), and a standalone end-to-end simulation importing the REAL project modules against the REAL asset, confirming the recentered shoulder sits 0.176m from a simulated camera (not ~1.5m), the mesh's world-space bounding box (y: 1.06–1.81) straddles a simulated camera at y=1.7, and a two-bone IK solve toward a plausible grip target lands on it exactly with reach to spare. This is a mount-time-only correction (computed once per skeleton instance, not per frame) — no change to the per-frame IK solve itself, no change to any GLB, no change to the weapon.

Also decided in this pass: keep the diagnostic Node/`tsx` scripts used to investigate this (headless `GLTFLoader`+`SkeletonUtils.clone` trace, and the real-asset end-to-end simulation) OUT of the repository — both were written to `tools/_diagnose-*`/`tools/_verify-*`, used, and deleted immediately after confirming the root cause and the fix, per this project's "no stray files" convention. The PRODUCTION-facing diagnostics the milestone brief asked for (one-time console logs, the `?ik=1` IK-disabled/bounding-box toggles) are real, shipped, dev-gated code — a different category from the throwaway investigation scripts.

---

**2026-07-22 — "Exploded geometry" investigation: a leading theory was tested and DISPROVEN; exhaustive headless verification found no data corruption; the most likely remaining explanation is extreme camera-to-shoulder proximity, not a bug**

Decision: report the exploded-geometry investigation honestly, including a theory that turned out to be wrong, rather than keep a "fix" whose comments overclaimed what was actually verified.

Reason: manual browser testing (after the recentering fix above landed) reported catastrophic stretched-triangle deformation in `/v2/range`, `/v2/range?ik=1`, and `/v2/play`. First theory: `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`'s `useFrame` called `container.updateWorldMatrix(true, false)` — `updateChildren=false` — meaning `instance`'s subtree (including every bone) could hold a one-frame-stale `matrixWorld` while `solveSide`'s `bones.upperArm.parent!.getWorldQuaternion(...)` read it, baking in a rotation delta between stale and current camera rotation on every frame the camera moved. This was a plausible, well-reasoned theory — but rather than ship it as "the fix" on reasoning alone, a dedicated regression test was built (`kaelArmSolve.test.ts`) to reproduce the exact stale-children scenario and prove the corruption. **The test disproved the theory**: `THREE.Object3D.getWorldQuaternion()`/`getWorldPosition()` call `updateWorldMatrix(true, false)` on themselves internally, before decomposing — they self-correct their own ancestor chain regardless of what the caller already updated. The "fix" (`container.updateWorldMatrix(true, true)`) was kept as harmless, cheap (~65 bones) defensive hardening for any future direct `.matrixWorld` read that wouldn't have this self-correcting property, but its comments were corrected to NOT claim it fixes the reported symptom, since testing proved it doesn't address the actual mechanism.

Went further: extracted `solveSide` (previously private to `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`, untested) into `src/lib/v2/operators/kaelArmSolve.ts` specifically so this class of bug could be tested in isolation — the third time in this milestone a real or suspected bug was found in exactly this kind of previously-untested inline logic (the `Vector3` self-aliasing bug, the per-frame allocation findings, and now this). Then ran exhaustive headless verification against the REAL asset using the REAL project code (not a reimplementation): a single-frame check, a 30-frame simulation with the camera continuously oscillating (mouse-look), using the REAL shipped `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG` pole values, REAL grip-anchor target rotations (not identity), and REAL finger-curl values (`RIGHT_HAND_FINGER_POSE`/`LEFT_HAND_FINGER_POSE`) — reused the SAME per-side state objects across all 30 frames, exactly like the real per-frame loop. Critically, this verification computed the ACTUAL DEFORMED skinned vertex positions via `THREE.SkinnedMesh.applyBoneTransform` (the exact math the GPU vertex shader runs), not just the solver's abstract output — sampling all 10,683 vertices every frame. Result: **no reproducible explosion under any tested scenario** — bounding box stayed within a believable ~0.5–0.7m arm-scale size every frame, no vertex exceeded ~0.6m from the simulated camera position, skinning bind relationships confirmed correctly rebound to cloned (not original) bones, material properties confirmed normal.

One real anomaly WAS found: the closest vertex to the camera across every frame (same vertex index, consistently) sits only ~0.12m away, weighted 92.7% to the upper-arm bone and clustered around the shoulder joint — the exact region where the full-body-to-arms-only extraction (Step 4) would have cut the mesh away from the torso. At a 75° FOV (`PLAYER.FOV_BASE`) and 0.12m distance, `tan(37.5°) × 0.12 ≈ 0.09m` half-frustum-width — meaning even a small, entirely correctly-shaped triangle at that exact distance would project across a large fraction of the screen. This is the LEADING hypothesis for the reported symptom: the recentering anchor (upper-arm midpoint) places the shoulder joint's own geometry unusually close to the camera, and normal perspective projection at that proximity can make undistorted geometry LOOK "exploded"/screen-filling, without any actual data corruption.

Rejected: shipping a `shoulderRootOffset` change to push the shoulder further from the camera as part of this pass — the milestone brief explicitly says "do not tune elbow poles, finger curls, shoulder offsets... until the geometry is structurally valid," and given the near-camera-proximity theory is a hypothesis (not confirmed without an actual render), changing a visual-tuning value based on an unconfirmed guess would violate that instruction's spirit even if its letter might permit it once "structural validity" is established. Also rejected: continuing to guess at further theories without a way to verify them — headless verification has been exhausted for this investigation; every mechanism checkable without a render (data corruption, NaN propagation, bind-matrix validity, quaternion consistency, multi-frame accumulation) has been checked and found clean.

Chosen: report findings exactly as they are — disproven theory documented (not deleted, so the next investigator doesn't re-derive and re-test the same dead end), the harmless defensive change kept with corrected comments, the near-camera-shoulder hypothesis reported as the leading but UNCONFIRMED explanation, and an explicit request for an actual screenshot (or a report using the newly-built `?ik=1` bounding-box helper and IK-disabled toggle) before any further diagnosis or shoulder-offset change is attempted.

---

**2026-07-22 — `shoulderRootOffset` changed on explicit user authorization, verified numerically (not visually) via the same headless harness**

Decision: change `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG.shoulderRootOffset` from `[0.0, -0.05, 0.0]` to `[0.0, -0.15, -0.3]`, after the user explicitly authorized testing this specific fix for the near-camera-shoulder hypothesis above (having reviewed the prior entry and agreeing it "does not look like random skinning corruption anymore").

Reason: the prior entry identified but deliberately did NOT act on a hypothesis — a shoulder-joint vertex sitting ~0.12m from the camera, plausibly explaining the reported "exploded" appearance through ordinary perspective rather than data corruption — specifically because changing a visual-tuning value on an unconfirmed guess, without the user's sign-off, would have violated the milestone brief's "do not tune shoulder offsets" instruction. The user has now reviewed that finding and explicitly asked for this exact change to be tried, with a stated fallback value if it's insufficient.

Rejected: waiting for a screenshot before making ANY change — the user's request was explicit and specific (exact offset values, in a stated priority order), not a general "investigate more" — proceeding is following the instruction, not overriding the earlier caution; that caution was about not acting on an UNAUTHORIZED guess, not about refusing an authorized one.

Chosen: extended the same headless verification harness used throughout this investigation (real asset, real `SkeletonUtils.clone`+`GLTFLoader`, real shipped pole/finger/grip-rotation values, `SkinnedMesh.applyBoneTransform` for GPU-equivalent skinning) to additionally apply `shoulderRootOffset` via `container.translateX/Y/Z(offset)` in the EXACT same sequence `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`'s `useFrame` does (position/quaternion copied from a simulated camera first, then translated) — not a simplified proxy. Measured the worst-case (over 20 oscillating-camera frames) minimum vertex-to-camera distance at three offsets: the OLD value (0.121m — consistent with the earlier investigation's single-frame finding), the user's primary candidate `[0, -0.15, -0.3]` (0.303m), and their stated fallback `[0, -0.18, -0.4]` (0.383m). Shipped the primary candidate, per the user's own "first... starting point" framing — the fallback is recorded in the config's own doc comment as a one-line change if 0.303m still isn't enough once someone can actually look at it. **This numeric distance increase is NOT the same as visual confirmation** — a vertex being farther from the camera doesn't guarantee the composition reads correctly (arms could now look too far away, poorly framed, or the underlying mesh-boundary issue could persist at a smaller but still-noticeable scale) — flagged explicitly in the changelog and to the user as still requiring an actual look before this is considered resolved.

---

**2026-07-22 — `operator-kael-arms.glb` rebuilt: the "exploded geometry" was a Decimate-modifier artifact at the open shoulder cut, confirmed by measurement, not a bind-space or IK-application bug**

Decision: rebuild the FP-arms GLB derivative using a new post-decimation "long-edge scalpel" cleanup step added to the existing `tools/blender/make-kael-fp-arms.py` pipeline, rather than redesigning the extraction method (bisect cut, different bone-weight threshold) or touching any runtime IK/skinning code.

Reason: after the shoulder-offset change (previous entry) still showed giant polygon slabs in a real browser, the user correctly concluded camera-distance tuning couldn't be the fix and asked for the asset itself to be investigated. Used real Blender automation (not guessing) against the accepted rigged source to measure the ACTUAL mechanism: the raw bone-weight-threshold extraction produces tiny boundary edges everywhere tested (max ~0.017m at every threshold from 0.15–0.65) — ruling out the extraction/selection method itself as the cause, contrary to the user's own initial hypothesis in their Step 6C brief ("the current bone-weight-threshold extraction... is not first-person ready"). The actual mechanism is specific to decimation: this pipeline's existing Decimate modifier configuration protects hand/finger vertices from Collapse reduction (an established, working mechanism, unchanged) but has ZERO protection at the open shoulder/torso cut boundary — Collapse decimation next to a topologically OPEN edge (no far-side neighbor to constrain it) can and does stretch individual triangles dramatically. Measured precisely: pre-decimation max boundary edge 0.0168m → post-decimation 0.1208m (7x), max face area 0.000082 → 0.008560 m² (104x) — on the exact threshold/target values that built the previously-shipped, reportedly-broken file (confirmed by near-exact vertex/triangle count match: 10,774v/19,995t reproduced vs. shipped 10,683v/19,996t).

Rejected, each measured not assumed: (a) protecting boundary-adjacent vertex rings (up to 5) from decimation via the same vertex-group mechanism already used for hands — measured to NOT work (edges still reached 0.135m) and to blow the triangle budget; a Collapse decimator forced away from one protected region simply relocates equivalent distortion to whatever remains unprotected at that region's own border — a "waterbed effect" that doesn't reduce total distortion, just moves it. (b) Closing the open boundary with `bmesh.ops.holes_fill` before decimating (tried both on the raw weight-threshold boundary AND on a clean planar bisect cut through the upper-arm bone's own rest-pose axis, specifically to give the auto-fill a single simple loop instead of thousands of irregular edges) — measured to fail: even the clean bisect version only filled 2 of many boundary edges into new faces, left most of the boundary open, and introduced NEW malformed/wire topology that hadn't existed before either extraction method. This mesh's true cut-boundary topology (clothing folds, organic surface detail) is too irregular for automatic hole-filling to handle reliably — confirmed by trying it two different ways, not assumed from one failure. (c) A full geometric bisect-based re-extraction (replacing bone-weight thresholding with a plane cut through the upper-arm axis) as a wholesale alternative to the existing pipeline — the bisect step itself worked correctly (cleanly halved vertex counts per side as expected), but inherited the SAME capping failure as (b), and would have meant maintaining two parallel extraction methods for no measured benefit once the real mechanism (decimation, not selection) was identified.

Chosen: `MAX_EDGE_LENGTH_M = 0.035` scalpel — after decimation completes (protected hand/finger regions unchanged from the existing, working mechanism), delete any face whose longest edge exceeds this length, then clean up the resulting loose vertices. A first version restricted this to "proximal-dominant" faces only (to structurally avoid ever creating a hole in hand/forearm geometry, sidestepping the existing ACCIDENTAL_HOLE validation gate rather than touching it) — measured to under-perform badly (most of the real giant-triangle faces turned out to be mixed/borderline-weighted, not cleanly proximal-dominant; max face area only improved 11%, from 0.008674 to 0.007722, nowhere near what an unrestricted pass achieves). Reverted to an unrestricted scalpel (any long-edge face, any region) plus a size-based exception on the EXISTING accidental-hole classifier: a distal-dominant boundary loop at or below `SMALL_ACCIDENTAL_HOLE_EDGE_LIMIT = 6` edges (a single stray triangle, not a real cut into hand/finger geometry) is recorded and tolerated rather than failing the build; anything larger still fails, preserving the gate's original purpose. On the real asset this produced a clean, full-strength result — max edge 0.1615m → 0.0344m, max face area 0.008674 → 0.000318 m² (27x) — with zero accidental holes triggered at all once the independent `proximal_bones` bug (below) was also fixed.

Also found and fixed, independently of the scalpel work: `proximal_bones` (used by this script's existing boundary-loop classifier to tell "intentional shoulder cut" from "accidental cut into hand/finger geometry") read `resolution.get("shoulder_left")` etc. directly on the outer dict returned by `resolve_arm_bone_set`, but that function's real return shape nests chain names under `resolution["chains"]` — meaning `.get("shoulder_left")` always silently returned `None`, and `proximal_bones` has been just the single boundary bone (e.g. `Spine2`) instead of the intended shoulder+upper-arm set for as long as this script has existed (since Step 4, this milestone's original arms-extraction pass). Never caused a hard failure before because the mis-scoped distal set happened not to trigger the classifier's ACCIDENTAL_HOLE branch on the original (uncut-by-scalpel) boundary — but it meant the classification safety net was never actually checking what it claimed to. Discovered because the scalpel's own (later-reverted) proximal-restriction attempt matched zero faces until this was fixed — a real bug found as a side effect of testing an approach that itself didn't ship. Fixed to `resolution["chains"].get(...)`.

Also added: a permanent edge-length check in `tools/inspect-operator.mjs --mode arms` (reads the raw GLB buffer directly, no rendering) that flags any triangle edge over 0.06m — verified against both the original broken file (fails, 0.1615m) and the rebuilt one (passes, 0.0344m). This specific defect class — clean topology, correct triangle budget, correct bone chains, yet visually catastrophic — passed every EXISTING inspector check on the original file; it took a human looking at a browser to catch it. This addition closes that gap for any future arms rebuild.

---

**2026-07-22 — Kael FP-arm debug bounding box was measuring the wrong thing entirely; built a true rest-pose bypass as a harder diagnostic; no further transform bug found by measurement**

Decision: fix `ArmBoundingBoxHelper` to compute bounds from actual deformed skinned vertices (`computeDeformedSkinnedBounds`, walking every vertex through `SkinnedMesh.applyBoneTransform`) instead of `THREE.Box3.setFromObject()`; separately, build `restoreRestPose()` — a hard, solver-free bypass that overwrites bones with snapshots captured once at mount — as a new "REST MESH DIAGNOSTIC" toggle, distinct from the existing `ikDisabled` (weight=0) toggle.

Reason: manual browser testing with `IK disabled` + `bounding box` enabled showed the arms completely invisible and the bounding-box helper drawing enormous magenta lines across most of the screen. Rather than theorize further, built a headless diagnostic that runs `solveSide` at weight=1 and weight=0 against the real rebuilt asset and directly compares `Box3.setFromObject(container)` against ground-truth deformed vertex bounds. Result: `Box3.setFromObject` returned the EXACT SAME box (size, min, max) regardless of IK weight — proving it never reflected the deformed pose at all, only the mesh's static bind-pose geometry (THREE.js's documented behavior for `Box3.expandByObject`'s non-precise path: it uses `geometry.boundingBox`, computed once at load from the raw, undeformed vertex attributes, since GPU skinning happens entirely in the vertex shader and never touches CPU-side geometry data). The static box's position was also offset by roughly the container's own height from where the TRUE geometry renders. This fully and independently explains the reported symptom — a wireframe box drawn at the wrong place/size will dominate the screen regardless of whether the actual mesh (elsewhere, correctly rendering or not) is visible.

For "arms completely invisible" specifically: rather than accept the bounding-box fix as also explaining invisibility (a leap not warranted by evidence — a wrong debug overlay doesn't hide unrelated geometry), built the requested harder diagnostic. `solveSide` at `weight=0` is mathematically expected to converge to rest pose via `solveTwoBoneIk`'s own weight-blend branch, but that's still a claim ABOUT the solver, not a bypass OF it — a bug anywhere in that blend path could still produce a wrong result while `ikDisabled` is active. `restoreRestPose()` has no such path: it captures `{position, quaternion, scale}` for every relevant bone directly from the freshly-cloned, never-yet-posed skeleton at mount time, and the new diagnostic mode overwrites bones with those exact captured values, calling no IK code at all. Ran this exact path headlessly against the real rebuilt asset: `container.visible: true`; camera-space shoulder/elbow/hand positions for both sides all within 0.5m of the camera, left/right symmetric; true deformed bounds `[1.009, 0.745, 0.195]` m, finite; nearest/farthest vertex 0.356m/0.985m from camera. Every number is sane and arm-scale — no exploded geometry, no missing geometry, no non-finite values, in the hardest-to-fake test constructed so far in this investigation.

Rejected: shipping only a theory-based fix (e.g., guessing the `ikDisabled` weight-blend path has a bug and patching it blind) — the headless evidence doesn't support that theory; `solveSide` at weight=0 was already tested and shown to converge to rest (Step 6C's test suite). Patching code with no reproducing test would be exactly the kind of unverified change this whole investigation has deliberately avoided.

Chosen: ship both the confirmed bounding-box fix (real bug, real fix, real test coverage — 4 new tests using a synthetic 2-bone SkinnedMesh, proving the function responds to actual bone rotation unlike the old method) and the true rest-pose bypass system (5 new tests: exact restoration, finger removal, finiteness, 100-cycle no-drift, position/scale defense-in-depth) as the deliverable for this pass. Report to the user that headless investigation found and fixed one definite bug and built a genuinely harder ground-truth tool, but could not itself reproduce or diagnose "completely invisible" beyond that — the next manual browser check should specifically exercise the NEW "REST MESH DIAGNOSTIC" toggle (a different code path than anything tested before this pass) rather than re-testing the old `ikDisabled` toggle, since that would not distinguish a genuine remaining bug from the now-fixed bounding-box display bug alone.

**2026-07-23 — Kael FP-arm "invisible in REST MESH DIAGNOSTIC" root-caused: a viewing-angle mismatch, not a bug — found by building a true camera-frustum classifier and running the full transform chain through it stage by stage**

Decision: build `classifyVerticesInCameraSpace()` — true camera-space/NDC classification of every deformed vertex (front/behind camera, near/far-plane clipping, in-frustum count via NDC bounds, camera-space center), using the exact view/projection matrices the GPU rasterizer uses — and run it headlessly against the real rebuilt asset at each stage of the transform chain (direct-camera-mount with and without the recenter anchor; the normal camera-following container with and without `shoulderRootOffset`; IK at weight 0/0.25/0.5/1.0), rather than continuing to theorize about the reported "invisible arms, oversized detached bounding box" symptom from Step 6D.

Reason: the Step 6D fix (true deformed bounds instead of static bind-pose bounds) was proven correct by direct measurement, yet manual browser testing still reported the arms invisible and the box still oversized/detached. A finite, correctly-shaped bounding box proves the geometry EXISTS somewhere sane in world space — it does not prove that geometry is inside the camera's actual viewing frustum. That gap is exactly what `classifyVerticesInCameraSpace` closes, and it is the reason this pass finally found the answer where Step 6D could not: Step 6D's headless checks all asked "is this pose finite and arm-scale," never "would a camera at this position actually see it."

Result: at the SHIPPING `shoulderRootOffset = [0, -0.15, -0.3]`, the rest pose (arms hanging naturally at the character's sides, exactly as authored for third-person viewing) places only 0.1% of the mesh's vertices (6 of 10,512) inside the visible frustum — NDC Y spans `[-3.85, -0.15]`, meaning the geometry sits almost entirely below the bottom of the screen, with only a sliver near the wrist poking into view. Running the SAME classifier at IK weight 0.25 → 0.5 → 1.0 (the actual shipping configuration) shows the arm progressively entering frame as the hand is pulled toward the grip target: 0.1% → 36.3% → 66.7% in-frustum. This is the expected, healthy shape of that curve — IK exists precisely to pull the arm from an off-screen rest pose into an on-screen held-weapon pose. The rest pose was never going to look like "two visible arms" at this camera distance, by the nature of a rest pose, not by any code defect.

Also investigated and DISPROVEN, each by direct measurement rather than assumption, since any of these could independently explain "invisible": (1) backface culling from a winding defect introduced by the Step 6C decimation/scalpel rebuild — inspected the real material directly, `side: THREE.DoubleSide`, so winding cannot hide any triangle regardless of decimation artifacts; (2) camera render layers hiding the arms — grepped every file under `src/components/three` for `.layers` usage; none exists anywhere in the V2 three-fiber tree, so camera and mesh both sit on THREE's default layer; (3) a second, dedicated viewmodel camera (the common FPS "avoid near-clipping the gun" technique, which would mean `KaelFirstPersonArms` is reading the wrong camera) — `VortexViewmodel.tsx` has zero references to camera/FOV/near/far/layers/renderOrder/depthTest, confirming the rifle (and by extension anything following the same camera, including the arms) uses the one single main scene camera, no special second pass; (4) the debug bounding-box helper double-applying the camera's transform (the user's own leading hypothesis for this step) — read `Box3Helper`'s three.js source directly (`node_modules/three/src/helpers/Box3Helper.js`): it sets its OWN local `position`/`scale` from the box's already-world-space center/size on every `updateMatrixWorld`, and `KaelArmIkDebug`'s `<primitive object={helper}/>` is mounted as a DIRECT child of `<Canvas>` in `RangeScene.tsx` (same tree level as `KaelFirstPersonArms`, never nested inside any camera-relative group) — with an identity-transform parent, "local == world" here is correct by construction, not a double transform.

Two additional, unrelated-to-the-original-report bugs were found and fixed as a side effect of building this diagnostic honestly rather than superficially:

1. **Bone `matrixWorld` staleness for same-tick debug reads (real, but debug-only — never affected the actual rendered frame).** `restoreRestPose`/`solveSide` mutate bone LOCAL transforms; `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`'s own `container.updateWorldMatrix()` call happens once at the START of its frame, BEFORE those mutations. Any code reading raw `matrixWorld` LATER in the same tick — specifically `KaelArmIkDebug`'s bounding-box helper, which runs in a SEPARATE `useFrame` — would see the PREVIOUS frame's pose, not the one just written. Three.js's own renderer refreshes the entire scene's matrices immediately before rasterizing, AFTER every `useFrame` callback has run for that tick, so the actual GPU-drawn frame was never affected by this — only the debug overlay could have shown a stale, mismatched pose. Fixed with an explicit `updateWorldMatrix(true, true)` call immediately after every bone-mutating call (rest-pose restore, IK solve, and the new direct-camera-mount path). A new regression test in `kaelArmSolve.test.ts` proves the staleness window is real (a raw, non-self-correcting `matrixWorld` read equals the pre-restore value until the explicit refresh runs) and that the fix closes it.
2. **A freshly-built diagnostic would itself have been silently invisible, caught before it ever reached the user.** Implementing "DIRECT CAMERA MOUNT" (parenting the arms clone directly onto the THREE.js camera object, the standard FPS-weapon-attachment technique) surfaced a real three.js behavior: `WebGLRenderer.render()` builds its render list by traversing from `scene`, never from `camera` — confirmed directly in `node_modules/three/src/renderers/WebGLRenderer.js` (`projectObject(scene, camera, ...)`, and `if (camera.parent === null && ...) camera.updateMatrixWorld()`, which explicitly treats an un-parented camera as the normal case). R3F's default camera (as configured via `RangeScene.tsx`'s `camera={{...}}` shorthand prop) is never added to the scene graph. Anything parented directly onto such a camera would therefore never be visited by the render traversal — invisible by omission, regardless of material, `visible`, or `frustumCulled` settings, which would have made a diagnostic meant to prove "the asset itself is fine" instead falsely suggest an asset defect. Fixed by explicitly calling `scene.add(camera)` (idempotent; harmless, since the R3F root scene's own transform is always identity, so this changes nothing about the camera's resulting world position/orientation) before parenting the diagnostic group.

Rejected: treating the Step 6D bounding-box fix as sufficient explanation for "invisible arms" without further evidence — a wrong debug overlay does not, on its own, explain unrelated geometry failing to render; the two are logically independent claims and this pass deliberately did not conflate them. Also rejected: disabling the recenter anchor in the DIRECT CAMERA MOUNT diagnostic per an overly literal reading of the request's "disable recentering" instruction — headless testing showed this reproduces the ALREADY-diagnosed, ALREADY-fixed "mesh ~1.5m above camera" symptom from the Step 6C blocker (asset-space character coordinates, not a runtime feature), which would waste the diagnostic's entire purpose. Documented this deviation prominently in both code comments and the changelog rather than silently overriding the instruction.

Chosen: ship `classifyVerticesInCameraSpace` (6 new tests) as a permanent, reusable diagnostic; ship the "DIRECT CAMERA MOUNT" toggle with its local offset `[0, 0.2, -0.6]` chosen by headlessly sweeping candidates against the real asset (100% of vertices in-frustum at ~0.58m, versus the request's placeholder `[0,-0.2,-0.8]`, which only achieves 4.5%); ship the `matrixWorld` staleness fix with a regression test; ship the `scene.add(camera)` fix inline as part of the new diagnostic (not a separate toggle — it's a precondition for the diagnostic to mean anything). Report to the user that the underlying transform/recenter/skinning/IK chain is confirmed correct by this pass's evidence, that "invisible in REST MESH DIAGNOSTIC" was never a bug to fix, and that the real acceptance check going forward is the IK-enabled (shipping) configuration with the bounding box on — not the rest-pose diagnostic, which by its nature will always show mostly-empty frame at this camera distance.

**2026-07-23 — Kael FP-arm hand-basis axis markers were built circular (always show perfect alignment) and had to be redesigned; headless Stage-B measurement proves the IK solve itself is exact, isolating all remaining work to human-judged calibration**

Decision: derive the new "hand basis axes" debug markers (Step 6F, `palmForwardWorldDir`/`thumbSideWorldDir`/`palmNormalWorldDir` on `KaelArmDebugSide`) from the FIXED, rig-measured `metrics.handBasisCorrection`, composed with the actual solved hand bone orientation — explicitly NOT from `effectiveBasisCorrection` (the version that includes the new tunable `handBasisAdjustQuat` adjustment), even though the latter seems like the more obvious choice at first glance.

Reason: the first implementation used `effectiveBasisCorrection` for both (a) solving what local rotation the hand bone needs, AND (b) reconstructing "where do the canonical grip axes point now" for the debug markers. Algebraically, `side.handWorldQuat` (the value solved for in step a) is defined as `targetWorldQuat * effectiveBasisCorrection^-1` — precisely so that composing it back with the SAME `effectiveBasisCorrection` reproduces `targetWorldQuat` exactly, by construction, REGARDLESS of what `effectiveBasisCorrection` actually is. Using that same value to draw the axis markers is therefore circular: `canonicalAxis.applyQuaternion(effectiveBasisCorrection).applyQuaternion(actualHandWorldQuat)` reduces algebraically to `canonicalAxis.applyQuaternion(targetWorldQuat)` — a result completely independent of any tuning, at `rotationWeight=1`. This was caught by a dedicated test (`handBasisAdjustQuat measurably rotates the published palm-basis world directions`) that applies a 90° test adjustment and asserts the marker actually moves — it failed on the first implementation with a measured 0° change, i.e. the tool would have shown "perfectly correct" no matter what the user did, hiding exactly the mismatch it exists to reveal.

The fix uses the FIXED `metrics.handBasisCorrection` (the value measured once from the real rig's finger-bone geometry at mount, never touched by tuning) for the marker reconstruction instead. Re-deriving algebraically: with `actualHandWorldQuat = targetWorldQuat * (measuredCorrection * adjustQuat)^-1`, composing with the FIXED `measuredCorrection` gives `targetWorldQuat * adjustQuat^-1` — now genuinely dependent on the tuning value. This correctly shows where the mesh's REAL, rigidly-bound geometric axes point given the bone's actual (tuning-affected) orientation, which is the only version that visibly diverges from the target as a WRONG correction is dialed in, and visibly converges as the user corrects it — the entire point of an interactive calibration marker.

Also decided: build a headless "Stage B" verification (per the brief's own calibration order — hands must be proven positionally accurate before any basis/pole/finger tuning is meaningful) against the REAL rebuilt asset, using the production `solveSide` and a plausible hip-fire-region grip target. Result: **0.00cm positional error, 0.00° rotational error, both hands, at full IK weight** — not approximate, exact by construction (the two-bone solver reaches any in-range target exactly, and the hand-basis solve is an algebraic exact-match by design, per the bug above). This is a decisive, evidence-based confirmation of the user's own diagnosis ("this is now a hand-basis and IK calibration problem, not an asset problem") — the solve architecture needs no further changes; 100% of the remaining gap between "structurally correct" and "visually correct" lives in WHAT the solver is told to reach (the measured/tuned basis correction, elbow pole directions, finger curl amounts), which are calibration VALUES, not code.

Rejected: attempting to guess new hand-basis-correction/elbow-pole VALUES that would "look right" without the ability to see a rendered frame — the whole class of error this entire investigation has repeatedly found is exactly "confident guesses about visual correctness that turn out wrong without measurement" (the giant-polygon shoulder-offset guess, the literal DIRECT CAMERA MOUNT offset, the circular axis-marker bug above). Shipping unverified calibration numbers here would repeat that mistake with FAR less of a safety net, since "does the palm look like it's gripping the gun" has no headless proxy at all, unlike every prior bug in this investigation (which all had SOME measurable numeric signature). Also rejected: making the continuous `ikWeight` slider fully replace the binary `ikDisabled` toggle — kept both, since `ikDisabled` is a useful one-click shortcut to the specific value 0, and removing it would be a pure regression for existing muscle memory with no benefit.

Chosen: ship the complete Step 6F calibration toolkit (live per-hand position/rotation-error readout with the brief's own ≤1cm decision rule visible in the panel; per-side hand-basis-adjustment/wrist-rotation-weight/finger-curl-scale controls; a continuous IK-weight slider; corrected hand-basis axis markers with regression-test coverage of the exact bug above) fully wired and defaulting to "no change from pre-6F behavior" (every new field is `null`/optional and falls back to the exact prior computation when untouched) — combined with the headless Stage-B proof that the solve is exact. Report to the user that Stages C through F (hand-basis visual tuning, elbow-pole tuning, finger curls, hip-fire/ADS/motion/`/v2/play` validation) are ready for a human session with the now-live tuner, and were not attempted blind.

**2026-07-23 — Left-hand 7.36cm reach-clamp diagnosed as a real two-handed-rifle-hold geometry mismatch, not a rig bug; fixed with a per-side shoulder assist that must be a REAL bone translation, not a fake solver root**

Decision: add `rightShoulderAssistLocal`/`leftShoulderAssistLocal` to `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG` — a camera-relative offset that rigidly translates ONE SIDE's shoulder/clavicle bone (`bones.shoulder`, a genuine `THREE.Bone.position` write) every frame, ship `leftShoulderAssistLocal: [0.045, -0.007, -0.038]` as a computed (not guessed) starting value, and read the resulting IK-pivot position back from `bones.upperArm` — NOT from the translated `bones.shoulder` bone itself.

Reason: manual browser evidence showed RIGHT at 0.00cm positional error and LEFT reach-clamped at 7.36cm, and that changing the SHARED `shoulderRootOffset` (which moves both shoulders identically) barely moved the LEFT number — correctly ruling out a shared-root fix. Investigated by headlessly reproducing the REAL grip-world-pose math end-to-end (`resolveRuntimeAnchorWorldPose` + the real `VORTEX_RUNTIME_ANCHORS.gripHandLocal`/`gripSupportLocal` + the real `VORTEX_VIEWMODEL_POSES.hip` pose), not an approximation, against the real rebuilt `operator-kael-arms.glb`. Two findings: (1) left/right upper-arm and forearm bone lengths measured to within 0.1mm of each other — this is NOT a bone-resolution bug, NOT a left/right mapping error, NOT an asymmetric extraction; (2) the REAL cause is that the Vortex Rifle's support grip (`gripSupportLocal.position.x = 0.22`, forward of the weapon's pivot) sits much farther from either shoulder than the trigger grip (`gripHandLocal.position.x = -0.25`, behind the pivot) — a real two-handed rifle hold inherently requires the support-side shoulder to extend/reach farther forward than the trigger-side shoulder, which a single SHARED, MIRRORED `shoulderRootOffset` structurally cannot express, no matter its value. Headless reproduction measured a 5.68cm deficit (same mechanism as the browser's reported 7.36cm; the numeric gap is expected since this reproduction cannot replicate the exact live frame's sway/bob/ADS-blend state).

A critical correctness point, derived before writing any code: an EARLIER design considered feeding a shifted "fake root" into the two-bone solver's math without moving anything in the actual rendered scene graph. This does NOT work. IK in this rig only ever writes bone ROTATIONS, never positions (a documented invariant, see `recenterArmMetrics`'s doc comment) — the solver would compute a rotation assuming a false pivot, but the RENDERED chain still visually originates from the bone's TRUE, unmoved position. Working through the vector algebra: if `root_fake = root_real + assist` and the solver aims `hand_computed` at `target`, the REAL rendered hand (which only inherits the computed ROTATION, not the fake root) lands at `root_real + (hand_computed - root_fake) = target - assist` — displaced by the assist vector, in the WRONG direction. A fake-root-only assist would have moved WHERE the error shows up, not reduced it. The fix therefore had to be a genuine rigid translation of a real bone.

A second correctness point, also found before shipping: `metrics.shoulderLocalPos` (the value that becomes `side.shoulderWorldPos`, the actual two-bone IK pivot) is measured in `measureArmRestMetrics` from `side.upperArm.getWorldPosition(...)` — NOT from the separate, optional clavicle/`shoulder` bone, which is resolved (`resolveKaelArmBones`) but was previously unused anywhere in the reach/pivot math (confirmed by grepping every usage across the codebase). Since `upperArm` is a direct scene-graph child of `shoulder` in the real skeleton, translating `shoulder` does correctly carry `upperArm` (and the whole chain below it) along rigidly — but reading the IK pivot position back from `shoulder` itself, rather than from `upperArm`, would be off by the fixed clavicle→upper-arm bone-length offset. The implementation translates `shoulder` and reads back from `upperArm`, deliberately.

Also decided: ship a COMPUTED starting `leftShoulderAssistLocal`, not leave it at `[0,0,0]` pending a live session. Swept assist magnitude along the measured shoulder→target direction headlessly against the real asset until positional error crossed under 1cm (crossed at ~5cm; chose ~6cm for comfortable margin). Final check with the exact shipped config values against the real asset: **RIGHT 0.000cm, LEFT 0.000cm** — both hands now reach their real grip targets with zero measured error. Confirmed via the same headless sweep that the RIGHT arm's error stays at exactly 0.00cm across every tested LEFT assist magnitude, proving the mechanism is side-isolated by construction, not just by convention.

Rejected: reading the repositioned `shoulder` bone's own world position as the IK pivot (the naive first instinct) — would silently introduce a fixed-offset positional bug equal to the clavicle→upper-arm bone length, in the opposite direction of fixing anything. Rejected: a fake-root-only (position-math-but-no-real-translation) assist — proven algebraically incorrect above; would have shipped a change that LOOKS like it should work (the debug numbers might even briefly seem to improve if measured incorrectly) while actually displacing the rendered hand by the assist amount. Rejected: defaulting the tuner's `rightShoulderAssistLocal`/`leftShoulderAssistLocal` fields to a no-op like every other Step 6F/6G override — since the shipped config value here IS itself a computed fix for a measured, real deficit, "untouched" should mean "the current best calibration," not "assist off, error returns."

Chosen: ship the shoulder-assist mechanism (real bone translation, correct pivot read-back), the computed non-zero left default, live reach diagnostics (`shoulderToTargetM`, chain lengths, `maxReachRatio`, `effectiveMaxReachM`, `reachDeficitM`, resolved bone names — all derived from the exact values the solve itself used that frame), the extended tuner (per-side XYZ assist controls, defaulting to shipped-not-zero), and 9 new tests including one that specifically validates the "always take the live bone-read path" implementation choice by confirming a zero-vector assist reproduces byte-identical results to the pre-6G cached-metrics computation path. Report to the user that both hands now measure 0.00cm positional error against the real asset with the shipped values — Stage B (position) is closed; wrist/basis calibration (Stage C onward) can now proceed without a reach deficit contaminating the visual read.

**2026-07-23 — Applied the first human-approved partial IK calibration pose to shipped config, extending the "shipped value is the real default" convention to every tuned field; fixed a genuine tuner-panel viewport-clipping bug found in the same pass**

Decision: promote `rightHandBasisAdjustDeg`/`leftHandBasisAdjustDeg` and `rightFingerCurlScale`/`leftFingerCurlScale` from tuner-session-only overrides (previously backed by nothing in `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG` — an untouched tuner field fell back to a hardcoded `[0,0,0]`/no particular curl scale) to real shipped config fields, update `leftShoulderAssistLocal` from Step 6G's headlessly-computed `[0.045, -0.007, -0.038]` to the human-approved `[0.06, -0.009, -0.05]`, and update every fallback chain (`KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`, `KaelArmIkTunerPanel.tsx`, `ikTunerStore.ts`) so "tuner field untouched" now means "use the shipped, human-approved value" for these fields too — the same convention Step 6G established specifically for shoulder assist, now extended everywhere a real approved value exists.

Reason: the user's browser calibration session approved a partial pose (Stages A/B closed since Step 6G; right hand-basis correction dialed to +30° around the canonical finger-forward axis; left shoulder assist refined slightly from the Step 6G starting point; both wrist-rotation-weights confirmed unchanged at 1.0; finger curls explicitly not yet reached, left at 0) and asked for it to be saved so the next session "starts directly from the approved pose instead of repeating everything." Before this pass, only `shoulderAssistLocal` had a real shipped-config backing — every other Step 6F tuning field (`handBasisAdjustQuat`, `fingerCurlScale`) fell back to a hardcoded neutral value with no way to persist a human's actual approved number outside the ephemeral (in-memory, never-persisted) Zustand tuner store. Saving "the exact block" the user pasted required giving those fields the same shipped-config treatment shoulder assist already had.

Re-verified headlessly against the real rebuilt asset using the exact shipped values (not the tuner overrides, which reset on reload) via the same production `solveSide` call path: **RIGHT 0.000cm, LEFT 0.000cm** positional error — matches the approved session's own reported `RIGHT POSITION ERROR: 0.00cm` / `LEFT POSITION ERROR: 0.00cm` exactly. Rotation-affecting fields (hand-basis adjust) don't change positional error by construction (proven in the Step 6F entry above), so this re-verification specifically confirms the position-affecting field (`leftShoulderAssistLocal`'s small refinement) didn't regress Stage B.

Separately, investigated the "missing buttons" report (the tuner panel's reset/copy-code footer not visible/reachable). Found a genuine CSS bug, not a rendering failure: `KaelArmIkTunerPanel`'s container used `top-72` (intentionally, to avoid overlapping `VortexGripTunerPanel` when both `?ik=1` and `?grips=1` are active) combined with `max-h-[85vh]` — but `85vh` is 85% of the FULL viewport height, not the space remaining below a 288px top offset. On any normal viewport (not just small ones — even 1080p fails this: `288px + 0.85 × 1080px = 1206px`, taller than the 1080px viewport itself), the panel's computed bottom edge extends past `RangeView`'s root container, which is `h-[100dvh] overflow-hidden` — silently clipping whatever falls past that edge, with no way to reach it: the panel's own `overflow-y-auto` only handles content overflowing WITHIN an already-correctly-bounded box, not the box itself extending past its ancestor's clip boundary.

Also found, incidentally, while wiring the new fallback tests: `ikTunerStore.test.ts` (created in the Step 6G pass) was never added to `package.json`'s `test` script's explicit file list, so it silently never ran as part of `npm test` or CI — a real gap in that prior pass's own "run full regression suite" step, caught only because this pass needed to add more tests to that same file and noticed the count didn't include it.

Rejected: reducing `top-72` to fix the clipping — this offset is deliberate (leaves room for `VortexGripTunerPanel` above it), and shrinking it would reintroduce a panel-overlap problem to fix a different problem. Rejected: keeping `max-h-[85vh]` and just accepting the clip — the whole reason a max-height was there in the first place was so the panel doesn't grow unboundedly as more calibration sections get added (which they will, next session per the "elbow poles → finger curls" plan), so removing height-bounding entirely wasn't right either.

Chosen: replace `max-h-[85vh]` with `bottom-4`, letting CSS compute the box's height as the space actually available between the `top-72` and `bottom-4` edges (which by construction can never exceed the parent's own bound), while keeping `overflow-y-auto` so the now-correctly-sized box still scrolls internally as more calibration sections are added in future sessions. Ship the updated `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG` values, the fallback-chain updates across all three consuming files, the CSS fix, the `package.json` test-registration fix, and updated/expanded `ikTunerStore.test.ts` coverage (now asserting the new fields' shipped-value fallback specifically, not just shoulder assist). Report to the user that the approved pose is now the permanent starting point for any future session — not just something they'd have needed to re-paste.
