**2026-07-23 — Extended the approved pose with left hand-basis + first non-zero finger curl; headlessly validated numerical stability through hip-fire/ADS/recoil/transition ahead of the human motion pass, explicit about what that validation can and cannot prove**

Decision: update `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG.leftHandBasisAdjustDeg` from `[0, 0, 0]` to `[30, 30, -10]` and `leftFingerCurlScale` from `0` to `0.30`, matching the newly approved values; re-verify positional accuracy stays at 0.00cm; run a headless motion-stability pass (hip-fire, ADS, a simulated recoil-punch sweep, a hip→ADS blend) using the real production grip-world-pose math, not a synthetic approximation.

Reason: the user approved the next stage of calibration (left hand-basis fully tuned, first real finger-curl value) and explicitly framed the next milestone as motion VALIDATION, not further tuning ("do not re-tune values unless motion exposes a real problem") — meaning the burden is now on finding evidence of a problem, not on continuing to guess new numbers. Before declaring this pose ready for a human motion-validation session, it's worth closing what CAN be checked without a browser: does the solve stay numerically well-behaved (finite, no failures, no discontinuous elbow-pole flips) across the actual motion states the user listed, using the real weapon-pose math this milestone has relied on throughout (`resolveRuntimeAnchorWorldPose` + `VORTEX_VIEWMODEL_POSES.hip`/`.ads`). Results: hip-fire and ADS both solve to exactly 0.0000cm on both hands (only sub-0.04° rotation residual, consistent with float precision, not a defect); a 15-frame simulated recoil-punch sweep (matching `VortexViewmodel.tsx`'s own punch-additive magnitudes) produced finite results every frame; a 10-step hip→ADS blend showed smooth, gradually-varying elbow movement (2-3cm/step, rising then falling) with no outlier spike — the numeric signature an elbow-pole flip would leave.

Also decided: state explicitly, not just imply, what this headless pass does NOT prove. It cannot detect visual clipping (does the hand mesh intersect the weapon geometry), mesh detachment, or how reload/inspect actually look on screen — none of that has a headless proxy; all of it requires a rendered frame and a human's eyes, which is exactly why the user framed this as the next milestone's job rather than something to be closed out now. Being clear about this boundary matters specifically because this investigation has repeatedly found real bugs by refusing to claim more confidence than the available evidence supports (the giant-polygon shoulder-offset guess, the circular axis-marker bug, the fake-root reach-assist trap) — a false "motion validated" claim here would be exactly that same class of mistake.

Rejected: treating the headless stability pass as a substitute for the requested human motion-validation session — it answers a genuinely different question ("does the math stay well-behaved") than the one the user asked ("does it look right, does anything clip"). Rejected: re-tuning any value in response to this pass's results, even though nothing failed — the user's explicit instruction was not to re-tune without motion exposing a real problem, and a clean headless numerical result is not "motion exposing a problem."

Chosen: ship the two approved value updates, the corrected doc comments, updated `ikTunerStore.test.ts` assertions, and report the headless hip-fire/ADS/recoil/transition results plainly as a numerical-stability precheck — not as a claim that visual validation is complete. The human motion-validation session (hip-fire, ADS, recoil, reload, inspect, `/v2/play`) remains the actual next milestone, unstarted by this pass.

---

**2026-07-24 — Kael FP-arm material region strategy: painted from the mesh's REAL geometry distribution (glove-dominant, no forearm shaft), not from an assumed even three-way split**

Decision: classify the arms mesh into glove/sleeve/armor regions using per-vertex dominant-bone (fingers → glove) plus a spatial "t" projection along each side's own wrist→elbow axis for everything else, with thresholds `GLOVE_T_MAX = -0.12` and `ARMOR_T_MIN = 0.5` set from the mesh's actual measured distribution, not the initially-assumed `t∈[0,1]` "even thirds" layout.

Reason: a first attempt at thresholds (`GLOVE_T_MAX=0.22`, `ARMOR_T_MIN=0.85`, chosen assuming t=0 at the wrist and t=1 at the elbow would be roughly evenly populated) produced **zero sleeve vertices** — the pipeline's own regression check caught this immediately rather than shipping a silently-broken region mask. A dedicated diagnostic (`_debug_t_distribution.py`) revealed why: essentially the entire non-finger mesh is hand/palm-dominant geometry sitting at **negative** t (palm/back-of-hand extends "behind" the wrist relative to the elbow direction, t∈[-0.55, 0]), while the only proximal-region geometry is a tiny 3–19-vertex shoulder-cap sliver at t∈[1.0, 2.4]. Nothing exists in the conventional [0,1] band at all — this is a real, load-bearing fact about the accepted source asset (a hand/finger-heavy extraction with almost no forearm-shaft surface, consistent with the Step 6C rebuild's own decimation-protection focus on hands/fingers), not a classification bug.

Rejected: forcing an even three-way split by picking arbitrary thresholds that would technically populate all three regions — this would fabricate a "sleeve" region out of geometry that's actually palm/hand, producing a materially wrong glove/sleeve boundary on the rendered character. Rejected: treating the near-total absence of forearm-shaft geometry as a blocker requiring new geometry authored from scratch (out of scope — Step 7A explicitly forbids replacing arms with procedural runtime geometry or rebuilding the skeleton/mesh topology).

Chosen: set thresholds from the real measured distribution (correctly near-100% glove, confirmed region_counts: glove 10,311 / sleeve 181 / armor 20 of 10,512 total), and deliberately paint the new shoulder-cut concealment cuff geometry (Step 7A §7's small, camera-hidden extrude) as **sleeve** rather than armor specifically to give the sleeve material some real visible surface area, since the base mesh has almost none — exercising the spec's own explicit allowance that the cuff "may use the sleeve or armor material." The practical, honestly-documented outcome is a glove-dominant first-person view, which is both geometrically correct and gameplay-appropriate (a first-person shooter's camera mostly sees hands, not upper arms).

---

**2026-07-24 — Four real bugs found in the new Kael arms materials pipeline before trusting its own "PASSED" report — none were caught by the pipeline's own internal checks, all found by independently inspecting its claimed output**

Decision: treat a script's own "PASSED"/`MATERIALS_OK` self-report as a claim to verify, not a fact to trust — inspect the actual files/bytes/GLB-JSON the pipeline claims to have produced before proceeding to the next stage. Four real bugs were found this way in `tools/blender/make-kael-fp-arms.py`'s new `materials` mode, in order of discovery:

1. **`bpy.types.Image.save()` silently no-ops on a relative `filepath_raw`.** The first full pipeline run reported `PASSED` with a `textures` block listing three PNG paths and byte sizes — but the `textures/` output directory was empty on disk. Confirmed via a minimal, controlled A/B script (`_debug_save.py`/`_debug_save3.py`, both deleted after use) on Blender 5.2.0 LTS: an identical `image.filepath_raw = path; image.save()` sequence succeeds with an absolute path and produces zero file/zero error with a relative one, even though the working directory itself was correct (`os.getcwd()` matched the shell). Fixed by resolving `texture_dir` and `output_glb` to absolute paths (`os.path.abspath`) at the very top of the `materials` mode, before any `os.makedirs` or save call.
2. **A disconnected duplicate `ShaderNodeTexImage` node, plus a custom property, do nothing for glTF export.** The original design for emitting `occlusionTexture` (pointing a second, unlinked Image Texture node at the same packed-ORM image, plus a `mat["_orm_occlusion_node"]` custom property as an "exporter hint") produced no `occlusionTexture` in the exported material at all — direct inspection of the GLB's JSON chunk showed `normalTexture`/`baseColorTexture`/`metallicRoughnessTexture` present but no `occlusionTexture`, despite AO being correctly baked into the ORM texture's R channel. Root cause: Blender's glTF exporter (`io_scene_gltf2`) assigns material roles by tracing which node's output feeds which Principled BSDF input socket — a node with no output link is invisible to that role-assignment logic, and there is no supported custom-property hint for it. Fixed by removing the dead node entirely and instead patching `occlusionTexture: {"index": <same index as metallicRoughnessTexture>}` directly into the exported GLB's binary JSON chunk after export, via a small pure-stdlib (`struct`+`json`) GLB chunk rewrite — deterministic, no dependency on undocumented exporter behavior, matches the standard "packed ORM, occlusion and metallicRoughness share one texture" glTF convention that three.js's `GLTFLoader` already expects.
3. **Hex art-direction colors fed into Blender's linear color sockets without an sRGB→linear decode.** `GLOVE_BASE = (0x15/255, 0x19/255, 0x1D/255)` and seven sibling constants were assigned directly to `default_value` on Principled BSDF color inputs, which are linear — skipping the sRGB EOTF decode a display hex value requires. Visually confirmed on the first bake: "charcoal" #15191D baked and rendered as a medium slate gray, not a dark charcoal, because a linear value of ~0.08 displays (after the normal sRGB encode for viewing) as ~0.32 — noticeably lighter than intended. Fixed with a standard `srgb_to_linear()` piecewise gamma decode applied to all 8 palette constants before use; the re-bake shows genuinely dark glove regions matching intent.
4. **A bmesh round-trip left stray, unintended vertex-color data in the export.** After the shoulder-cut cuff's `bmesh.ops.extrude_edge_only`/`holes_fill`/`to_mesh()` round-trip, the exported mesh carried two extra byte-color CORNER-domain attributes ("Color", "Color.001") never explicitly created by this script — direct inspection (a small Blender headless script loading both the pre- and post-materials GLBs and diffing `mesh.color_attributes`) confirmed the original asset has zero color attributes and the intentional "RegionMask" attribute is separately, correctly excluded from export (it's unused in the final baked-texture-only material graph, per the exporter's own "active Vertex Color... not used in the node tree" warning). Root cause not fully isolated (bmesh's `from_mesh()`/`to_mesh()` round-trip for a custom FLOAT_COLOR POINT-domain attribute is not guaranteed to preserve it under that name/domain in this Blender version) but the fix doesn't require knowing the exact mechanism: strip ALL vertex-color attributes immediately before export, since the final material reads only baked textures and needs none at runtime, regardless of which step introduced them.

Reason: this pipeline has a documented house habit (established across Steps 6E–6G, see the entries above) of never trusting a script's self-reported success without independently re-measuring its claimed output — that habit is exactly what caught all four of these before they could ship silently. A `PASSED`/`0 failures` result only proves the checks the script itself thought to run; it says nothing about a save call that silently no-ops, a material role the exporter silently drops, a color space bug that changes every rendered pixel without erroring, or stray data the script never intended to produce.

Chosen: keep all four fixes in the pipeline script itself (not hand-patched onto one output file) so every future re-run of `materials` mode is correct by construction; re-ran the full pipeline against the real `public/v2-art/operator-kael-arms.glb` after each fix and re-verified (GLB JSON inspection, not just the script's own report) before promoting the result.

---

**2026-07-24 — Marketing "Play Now" CTAs intercept mobile/tablet taps with an explanatory modal instead of navigating to `/v2/play` and letting `MobileNotice` re-block there**

Decision: on coarse-pointer or sub-900px devices, every "Play Now"-style CTA (hero, `CtaSection`, navbar — desktop bar and mobile panel) renders as a `<button>` labeled "Play on Desktop" that opens a glass dialog (`DesktopRequiredModal`) explaining the keyboard/mouse requirement, rather than the real `<a href="/v2/play">`. Desktop is untouched — same `V2Button` gold link it always was.

Reason: `MobileNotice` (Milestone 6, see the `touch controls (deferred...)` decision above) already blocks `/v2/play` itself on the same heuristic, but that means a mobile visitor's first tap on "Play Now" spent a full navigation just to land on a page that immediately tells them to come back on desktop — a worse experience than saying so at the point of the tap. Reusing rather than duplicating the heuristic mattered: a second hand-rolled coarse-pointer/width check on the marketing side could silently drift from `MobileNotice`'s and misclassify a device (e.g. iPad in one state, not the other). Extracted to a single shared hook, `useRequiresDesktopControls` (`src/hooks/useRequiresDesktopControls.ts`), so both consumers read the same coarse-pointer-OR-width<900 test.

Rejected: disabling the CTA outright on mobile (fails silently, gives no explanation) — teaching moment matters more than a hard block; rejected: a toast/inline text swap instead of a modal (spec asked for a floating glass modal or bottom sheet, and a modal is the only one of those that supports the required focus-management guarantees — focus-in on open, focus-return on close, Escape-to-close); rejected: routing to `/v2/play` and relying solely on `MobileNotice` (the whole point is to avoid that wasted navigation).

Chosen: `useRequiresDesktopControls` hook shared by both `MobileNotice` and the new `PlayCta` wrapper; `PlayCta` renders the unchanged desktop `V2Button` link when the hook is false, and a `V2Button`-as-button + `DesktopRequiredModal` pair when true; `V2Button` gained optional `onClick`/`tabIndex` props (mutually exclusive with `href`-navigation) to support the button mode without forking the component. `DesktopRequiredModal` follows the same glass-panel language as `V2Navbar`'s mobile menu (`bg-storm-deep/95`, `border-white/15`, `backdrop-blur-2xl`) for visual consistency, traps focus on its single "Got it" control (no multi-element focus loop needed for a one-button dialog), closes on Escape or backdrop click, and returns focus to whichever CTA instance triggered it via a ref captured at click time (`event.currentTarget`) rather than a single shared ref, since multiple `PlayCta` instances exist on the page at once (hero, CTA section, navbar). Verified with Playwright across real iPhone-13 emulation (touch taps, not just resized viewports) and keyboard-only interaction: desktop CTAs remain real `<a href="/v2/play">` links, mobile CTAs render as "Play on Desktop" buttons with no `/v2/play` link present, the modal opens/closes correctly on tap/Escape/"Got it", and focus moves to "Got it" on open and back to the trigger on close.

---

**2026-07-25 — Kael FP-arm reload/inspect action-pose architecture: blend the LEFT HAND'S IK TARGET outside the solver, never touch `solveSide` itself**

Decision: procedural reload/inspect presentation (Milestone 7, Phase G, Step 7C) is implemented as a pure function of normalized time (`computeActionPose`) whose output — a blend weight, per-hand IK weight, per-hand finger curl, and a weapon-offset pair — is consumed entirely OUTSIDE the existing two-bone IK solver. The left hand's actual IK target position/quaternion is computed by lerping/slerping between the normal grip anchor and a resolved action-target anchor BEFORE calling `solveSide`, using the exact same `targetPosition`/`targetQuaternion` parameters that function already accepted.

Reason: the brief was explicit that the approved IK configuration, grip anchors, and solver must not change, and that "the smallest safe architecture" was required. `solveSide`'s internals (the two-bone solve, hand-basis correction, per-side tuning overrides) are the single most heavily-proven piece of this entire milestone — every earlier step (6E through 6I) treated it as the stable foundation everything else builds on, specifically because it reaches 0.00cm measured accuracy. Changing its signature or internal blend logic to accommodate a THIRD state (grip vs. action-target vs. some blend) would risk that accuracy for a feature that has nothing to do with the solve itself — the solver doesn't need to know an action is happening; it only needs a different (or blended) TARGET to solve toward, which is a pure input-substitution problem solvable entirely at the call site.

Also decided: `rightHandIkWeight` never leaves `1.0` in any phase or at any progress value, and the right hand's target is never blended at all — enforced by having `computeActionPose` simply never vary that field, and by `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` never touching `pose.rightPosition`/`pose.rightQuaternion`. This directly and trivially satisfies "the right hand remains the primary weapon attachment" — there's no code path that could violate it, rather than a runtime check that could fail.

Rejected: extending `solveSide`'s own weight-blend-toward-rest mechanism (the pre-existing Step 11 approach, `computeArmWeightTargets` reducing left weight to 0.15/0.3) to also handle actions — this makes the hand relax toward BIND POSE during a "reach for the mag" gesture, which reads as the hand going loose/floating rather than reaching somewhere deliberate; the brief explicitly asked for "no floating hands" and a hand that visibly "reaches the intended action area." Rejected: computing the action pose independently in both `VortexViewmodel.tsx` and `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` (two separate calls to `computeActionPose` with the same inputs) — technically produces identical results (the function is pure/deterministic — see the 31 unit tests proving exactly that), but violates "one action computation per frame" and creates a structural risk that the two call sites could drift apart later if one gets updated without the other.

Chosen: `VortexViewmodel.tsx` (which already owns `reloadingUntil`/`inspectingUntil` progress calculation and already applies the weapon's own dip/wobble) computes the action pose exactly once per frame, applies the weapon-facing half directly, and publishes the hand-facing half through a new bridge (`actionPoseState.ts`) in the same singleton-bridge family as `muzzleWorldPose.ts`/`gripWorldPose.ts` — `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` reads it imperatively (`.property` access, no subscription), matching the zero-React-per-frame-update requirement.

---

**2026-07-25 — Action-target anchors are small, honest offsets from the calibrated grip anchor, not a fabricated magazine-well location**

Decision: `RELOAD_LEFT_HAND_LOCAL` and `INSPECT_LEFT_HAND_LOCAL` (`lib/v2/weapons/actionTargets.ts`) are defined as small (6–8cm) offsets from the already-visually-calibrated `gripSupportLocal` anchor, not as a distinct "magazine well" or "bolt" location.

Reason: the real Vortex Rifle GLB (`vortex-rifle.lod1.glb`) is a single mesh with a single material and no separate magazine, bolt, or charging-handle geometry — confirmed by the existing, already-documented asset inspection from the Step 5 grip-anchor work (`docs/forge/vortex-rifle-v0.2.md`). The brief explicitly required "use only interactions supported by the current Vortex geometry" and forbade "a fake detached magazine unless a real magazine prop exists" or claiming a physical component moved when it did not. Placing the left hand at a plausible-sounding but entirely fictional "magwell" coordinate would be exactly that kind of unsupported claim — there is no magwell in the geometry for the hand to visually align with.

Rejected: inventing a precise-sounding magwell coordinate via guesswork (would look arbitrary/wrong once a human actually views it, and misrepresents what the asset contains). Rejected: leaving the left hand at the unmodified grip anchor during actions (defeats the entire point of the pass — "the left hand releases and returns believably" requires SOME visible repositioning).

Chosen: treat the action target honestly as "the support hand shifts to a different position on the same general area of the gun" — geometrically modest, visually plausible against the real mesh, and explicitly flagged in both the source file's doc comment and this entry as NOT visually calibrated (same "temporary runtime proxy, needs a human pass" status every other hand-measured anchor in this codebase already carries, e.g. the original grip anchors before their Step 5 calibration). A `?anim=1` debug marker ("show action target") exists specifically so a human can calibrate these values against the real geometry in a future pass, the same tool-first workflow the grip anchors themselves went through.

---

**2026-07-25 — Inspect's weapon motion was redeveloped (a held presentation angle, not a wobble); reload's weapon motion was left untouched**

Decision: the reload dip curve (`reloadDipOffset`, now living in `actionPose.ts`) is ported byte-for-byte from the pre-existing implementation — same breakpoints (0.35/0.55/0.85), same magnitudes, same settle-wobble tail. The inspect weapon motion was NOT ported as-is; it was redesigned from a small back-and-forth Z-tilt oscillation (no Y-yaw at all) into a held Y-yaw (~26°) + Z-roll (~7°) + small Y-lift (0.05m) presentation that rises during a short anticipation window, HOLDS at the presentation angle, then returns — using the same trapezoid shape function that also drives the hand/finger blend weights, so the whole gesture reads as one coherent motion rather than independently-tuned parts.

Reason: the brief's requirements differ meaningfully between the two actions. Reload already had a proven, good-looking dip (nothing in the brief asked for a different reload weapon motion — only the HAND behavior around it needed work), so re-deriving it from scratch would be needless risk for zero requested benefit. Inspect's brief was explicit and specific in a way the old implementation didn't satisfy: "the rifle rotates and lifts enough to expose its side/profile," "main inspection HOLD" (implying a held angle, not a continuous oscillation), and "avoid excessive spinning or arcade-style flourish." The old ±0.08 rad Z-only tilt, oscillating twice within the 1.5s duration, does not reveal a side profile at all (a pure Z-roll around the viewing axis doesn't rotate the object AWAY from the camera the way a Y-yaw does) and has no distinct hold phase — it's continuously moving throughout.

Rejected: keeping the old wobble and only changing the hand/finger side of inspect (would ship a pass that doesn't satisfy the brief's own explicit visual requirement for the weapon's motion). Rejected: a full free-spinning presentation turn (explicitly the "arcade-style flourish" the brief said to avoid).

Chosen: redesign the WEAPON motion for inspect specifically (duration unchanged, 1.5s), reuse the reload dip verbatim, and drive both the hand-blend weight and the new weapon-rotation amount from the same underlying trapezoid(anticipate-in, hold, return-out) shape per action, so a single, legible "how deep into the gesture" curve governs everything that moves.

---

**2026-07-25 — Found (not fixed): a real, pre-existing `VortexFireSystem.tsx` bug where a queued trigger press during reload fires a phantom shot the instant the reload completes**

Decision: report this bug in the changelog and here, with full root cause and the exact one-line fix, but do not touch `VortexFireSystem.tsx` in this pass.

Reason: Step 7C's interruption-matrix testing (`fireDuringReload`) repeatedly showed ammo landing at 29 instead of 30 after an otherwise-normal, uninterrupted reload — reproduced twice independently (once holding the fire button, once with a single quick tap-and-release logged to have happened while the phase was still `reloadSettle`, nowhere near reload completion). Direct code inspection of `VortexFireSystem.tsx`'s fire-gating block found the exact mechanism: `mousedown` unconditionally sets `triggerQueued.current = true`; of the THREE exit paths from the fire-gating function, two (`store.ammo <= 0` and a successful fire) explicitly reset `triggerQueued.current = false`, but the THIRD — `if (store.reloadingUntil !== 0) return;`, the path taken when a fire attempt is correctly blocked because a reload is in progress — does not. A single click during an active reload therefore leaves `triggerQueued` permanently true until SOME later frame where `store.reloadingUntil` happens to be `0`; the instant the reload naturally finishes, the fire-gating check re-evaluates, sees `wantsFire = triggerQueued.current` still true, and fires one round — even though the player released the mouse button long before the reload ended. This is completely unrelated to anything Step 7C's action-pose system touches (that system only reads `reloadingUntil`/`inspectingUntil`, never writes them, and `VortexFireSystem.tsx` was not modified by this pass at all) — it is a genuine, independent, pre-existing defect this pass's testing happened to surface.

Rejected: fixing it as part of this pass. The brief's hard constraints for Step 7C are explicit and repeated: "do not change gameplay or asset files," and `VortexFireSystem.tsx` owns fire rate, damage, and ammo consumption — exactly the category of file the brief said not to touch, even for a one-line, obviously-correct-looking fix. A found bug is not the same as permission to fix it outside the stated scope.

Chosen: document the exact root cause and fix here and in the changelog so a future, explicitly-scoped pass can apply it with full context, rather than silently working around it or silently shipping it unmentioned. The fix, when authorized: add `triggerQueued.current = false;` immediately before or after the `if (store.reloadingUntil !== 0) return;` line in `VortexFireSystem.tsx`'s fire-gating block.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 7D action-target calibration: judge by marker separation and geometric context, not by trying to see the glove mesh directly, in `/v2/range`'s intentionally dark environment**

Decision: calibrate `RELOAD_LEFT_HAND_LOCAL`/`INSPECT_LEFT_HAND_LOCAL` primarily by observing the `?anim=1` tool's action-target/grip-target debug markers (bright, unambiguous colors) relative to the visible weapon silhouette, rather than by trying to judge the actual glove mesh's position directly.

Reason: `/v2/range`'s environment is deliberately dark (matches its established art direction), and at that exposure the dark-charcoal glove material (itself a deliberate Step 7A choice) is very difficult to distinguish from the black background — confirmed by direct comparison screenshots. The debug markers exist specifically so a developer can calibrate a target's WORLD POSITION without needing perfect glove visibility, since the IK solver places the hand at (or very near) the target by construction — if the marker sits in a plausible, safe location, the hand will too.

Found via this method: the Step 7C first-pass reload offset (6cm back / 5cm down from the grip anchor) was too small — the action-target marker sat close enough to the grip-anchor marker that the two nearly overlapped on screen, which would read as an imperceptible nudge rather than a believable "released the grip" gesture. Separately, the inspect target's first attempted offset (matching reload's magnitude, 11cm back) placed the marker on top of the scope/optic bump visible on the receiver at `inspectHold`'s presentation angle — a real, visually-confirmable problem the brief's own "scope and receiver remain readable" requirement flags.

Rejected: leaving the values as shipped from Step 7C without visual verification (the whole point of this pass). Rejected: attempting pixel-level glove-contact judgment in the dark range environment, which the evidence showed wasn't reliably possible — the marker-relative-to-silhouette method is not a compromise, it's the correct tool for this specific asset/environment combination.

Chosen: increase the reload offset to 9cm back / 8cm down (clear separation from the grip marker, confirmed via screenshot, still conservative relative to the muzzle and within the receiver-body silhouette); reduce the inspect offset's backward component to 5cm (clears the scope, confirmed via screenshot) while keeping its vertical/rotation values. Both remain flagged as anchors a future pass could refine further with a brighter test environment (e.g. `/v2/play`) if higher precision is ever needed.

---

**2026-07-26 — Playwright's coordinate-based `.click()` breaks after Pointer Lock engages; use `element.click()` method-call dispatch instead**

Decision: for any Playwright test script that needs BOTH real pointer-lock-gated gameplay input (firing, ADS — which require `document.pointerLockElement !== null`) AND DOM panel interaction (buttons, checkboxes) in the same session, engage pointer lock first, then interact with panel elements via `locator.evaluate(el => el.click())` (a direct method call on the target element) rather than `locator.click()` (which computes a screen coordinate and dispatches a synthetic mouse event there).

Reason: empirically confirmed (a controlled reproduction) that once `canvas.requestPointerLock()` succeeds, `locator.click()` calls on other DOM elements time out with "canvas intercepts pointer events" — consistent with the Pointer Lock API's documented behavior of capturing mouse input for the locked element once engaged, which breaks Playwright's coordinate-based hit-testing for synthetic clicks elsewhere on the page. This was initially worked around (Step 7C) by simply never engaging pointer lock during panel-interaction tests, but that also means firing/ADS never work in that same test, forcing a compromise where reload could never be triggered by real ammo consumption in the same script.

Rejected: keeping pointer lock disengaged for all tests (blocks any test that needs real fire/ADS input alongside panel interaction — exactly the combination Step 7D's validation suite needed for realistic ammo/reload testing). Rejected: re-clicking "Click to enter" between every panel interaction (would repeatedly toggle lock state, adding flakiness and slowing every test).

Chosen: `element.click()` dispatch for buttons/checkboxes, and the pre-existing native-value-setter-plus-dispatched-event approach (already used for the range slider) for anything else — both bypass the OS-level pointer-position semantics that break coordinate-based clicks under lock, letting one script exercise real gameplay input and dev-panel controls together reliably.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 7F: fixed the Vortex fire/reload audio 404s by removing the real-file probe entirely, confirming `docs/design/audio.md`'s "reuse v1 synthesis" inference as settled rather than shipping placeholder audio files**

Decision: `vortexAudio.ts` no longer calls `playAudioEvent`/`resolveAudio` (the real-audio-file-first resolver that HEAD-probes `/v2-art/vortex-rifle.sfx-{fire,reload}.{mp3,ogg,wav}`) before falling back to procedural synthesis. `playVortexShot`/`playVortexReload` now call the existing `VortexSynth` directly and unconditionally, matching v1's `AudioEngine` exactly. This also resolves `docs/design/audio.md`'s open item: "When v2 audio implementation starts, the default assumption should be: reuse the v1 synthesis approach... This is a reasonable inference, not a settled decision — confirm before committing real implementation work and log the outcome here." That confirmation is this entry.

Reason: the 404s the user reported (`/v2-art/vortex-rifle.sfx-fire.{mp3,ogg,wav}`, `.sfx-reload.{mp3,ogg,wav}`) were real, traced to `resolveAsset`'s HEAD-request probing finding nothing at any of the 6 candidate paths (since none were ever placed there, and no manifest entry exists). Two fixes were possible: (a) manufacture and commit placeholder audio files so the probe succeeds, or (b) stop probing for files the project's own docs say were never meant to exist. `docs/design/weapons/vortex-rifle.md` §15 states plainly: "Following v1's proven technical approach (100% procedural Web Audio synthesis...) is the recommended implementation path, not sourced/recorded audio." Given that, the real-file-probing path wasn't "awaiting a future asset drop" the way `assetResolver.ts`'s model/image probing genuinely is elsewhere in this codebase (e.g. `aeolus.glb`) — it was probing for something the design docs already say shouldn't exist, making the fallback synth (already fully implemented, already correctly gated to accepted-shot/accepted-reload call sites) the actual permanent solution, not a placeholder.

Rejected: generating and committing placeholder WAV/MP3 files to make the probe succeed. This would add binary asset payload and provenance/licensing documentation burden for something explicitly NOT the documented direction, and would need to be redone or deleted whenever real authored audio (if ever pursued) arrives — solving a problem (missing real audio) the project's own docs say isn't being solved this way. Rejected: keeping the probe but reducing it to a single extension — still generates one real HEAD request (and a 404, if the single guessed extension is wrong) for a resolution path whose target the docs say won't exist; removing the call is strictly simpler and has zero behavioral downside given synthesis is permanent, not provisional.

Chosen: remove the file-probing call sites from `vortexAudio.ts` entirely; call the synth directly. Added, in the same pass: live integration with the existing `useSettingsStore().masterVolume` (previously hardcoded to 0.5, never reading the real player-facing volume slider — matches v1's `audioEngine.ts` pattern exactly, no competing settings store introduced), and a small pure `VoiceBudget`/`SingleVoiceGuard` (`src/lib/v2/weapons/audioVoiceBudget.ts`) bounding concurrent shot voices and preventing the reload jingle from ever overlapping itself — both directly requested by the Step 7F brief and cheap, low-risk additions given the rewrite was already touching this file. The generic real-file pipeline primitive (`src/lib/v2/pipeline/audio.ts`/`modelResolver.ts`) is untouched — it remains valid for any future v2 slot whose own docs actually call for real-file-first resolution; this decision is scoped to Vortex fire/reload audio specifically, not a statement that the primitive itself is wrong.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 7G: the sustained-fire turbine layer reuses `VortexFireSystem.tsx`'s existing `spinUpT` signal rather than inventing a parallel shot-streak tracker**

Decision: `computeVortexTurbineTarget`/the persistent turbine node chain in `vortexAudio.ts` are driven entirely by the `spinUp` parameter already passed into `playVortexShot(spinUpT)` — no new state, ref, or counter was added to `VortexFireSystem.tsx`, and no new call site was introduced there.

Reason: `VortexFireSystem.tsx` already computes `spinUpT` (0..1, ramping over `rpmSpinUpTimeS` ≈0.7s of continuously-held-and-firing trigger, reset on release/reload via `store.triggerHeldSince`) to drive the turbine's RPM ramp and recoil feel — this is EXACTLY "how far into sustained fire" the brief's `VortexShotContext.sustainedFireAmount` asks for. Building a second, independent tracker in the audio layer would either drift from the real gameplay signal (two sources of truth for the same concept) or require a NEW call site/prop threading into `VortexFireSystem.tsx`, directly risking the brief's hard constraint against touching gameplay/IK/animation files. Reusing the existing parameter keeps audio a strictly passive consumer of an already-exposed signal.

Rejected: a shot-counter/streak tracker owned by `vortexAudio.ts` itself, incremented on each `shot()` call and decayed on a timer. This would have been a plausible-looking alternative, but it can't naturally distinguish "held through an RPM-gate-blocked frame" from "genuinely stopped firing" the way the existing `triggerHeldSince`-derived `spinUpT` already does correctly, and building it would duplicate logic `VortexFireSystem.tsx` has already solved.

Chosen: `shot(spinUp: number)` (unchanged public signature) both drives per-shot layer pitch variation AND calls a new private `driveTurbine(spinUp)`. The turbine's own RELEASE (when to ramp back to silence) does NOT rely solely on gameplay calling `playVortexSpinDown()` correctly in every scenario — a 220ms self-release timeout, rearmed on every `shot()` call, provides a passive fallback that self-heals from death/pause/route-unmount without any of those needing a dedicated audio hook. `spinDown()` and `reload()` additionally trigger an immediate release for snappier response than waiting the full timeout.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 7G: caught and fixed a real double-volume-scaling bug in `computeVortexTurbineTarget` before it shipped**

Decision: `computeVortexTurbineTarget` takes only `sustainedFireAmount` — an earlier draft also accepted `masterVolume` and scaled the returned gain by it directly (`0.4 + 0.6*volume`).

Reason: the turbine's gain node connects through the SAME shared `master` GainNode every other layer (shot, reload, dry-fire) connects through, and `master.gain.value` is already set from `useSettingsStore().masterVolume` in `ensure()`. Baking a SECOND volume multiplication into the turbine's own target gain meant the turbine specifically would end up quieter than intended at any volume below 1.0, non-linearly (e.g. at masterVolume=0.5, effective gain would be `target*0.7*0.5` instead of the intended `target*0.5`) — an inconsistency that would have been very hard to notice by ear (it doesn't clip or silence anything, just subtly under-mixes the turbine layer at partial volume) but would show up immediately in a careful gain-staging review or an offline measurement comparing turbine output across volume settings.

Rejected: leaving the parameter in "for future flexibility." An unused-but-present volume parameter that LOOKS like it should matter is worse than not having it — the double-scaling bug existed specifically because the parameter's presence suggested it needed to be applied, and it was, incorrectly. Muting is already fully guaranteed by the shared master node alone (multiplying by 0 zeroes everything downstream regardless of any per-layer factor).

Chosen: removed the parameter entirely; `computeVortexTurbineTarget(sustainedFireAmount)` now has a single responsibility (map sustain 0..1 to gain/filter/pitch targets), and volume scaling happens exactly once, at the shared master gain node, for every layer uniformly. Caught during self-review while writing this decision entry, not by a test failure — a reminder that "does this parameter's effect compose correctly with the rest of the graph" is worth checking explicitly for any new AudioParam-driving function, not just whether its own output is clamped/finite.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 7G: `OfflineAudioContext`-based peak/RMS measurement lives in the browser-only path, not `node:test`**

Decision: `renderVortexShotOffline`/`renderVortexBurstOffline`/`renderVortexReloadOffline`/`renderVortexDryFireOffline` (in `vortexAudio.ts`) are exercised by the `?audio=1` debug panel and by Playwright browser validation — not by an automated `node:test` suite.

Reason: `OfflineAudioContext` (like `AudioContext`) is a browser Web Audio API with no Node.js equivalent; installing a native/WASM polyfill (e.g. `node-web-audio-api`) to make it available under `node:test` would be a new devDependency with real platform-build risk (native addon on Windows) for a "focused on audio content, not infrastructure" milestone that explicitly asked to keep the patch scoped. The brief's own wording — "Use OfflineAudioContext where practical" — is satisfied by using it where it actually exists.

Rejected: adding a Web Audio Node polyfill devDependency. Rejected: skipping offline measurement entirely and only sanity-checking via ear — the brief explicitly wants peak/RMS/crest-factor numbers, not just a subjective judgment call, and those numbers ARE obtainable, just not from `node:test`.

Chosen: the pure PARAMETER layer (`vortexSoundRecipe.ts` — gains, frequencies, envelope durations, hierarchy relationships like "shot louder than reload louder than dry-fire") is fully covered by deterministic `node:test` cases (25 of them). The actual RENDERED-AUDIO measurement (peak amplitude, RMS, worst-case burst-overlap accumulation) is real, but lives in the browser-only validation path and is recorded as point-in-time measured results in `changelog.md`/this file rather than as an automated regression gate — a future pass could add browser-based (Playwright) measurement assertions as a proper CI gate if that becomes valuable.

**2026-07-26 — Step 8B: Blender unavailable in this session's environment — prepared scaffolding only, did not fake or simulate a lower-body GLB**

Decision: do not write a fabricated report claiming a `operator-kael-lowerbody.glb` was extracted, textured, rendered, or deformation-tested. Instead: (1) confirm and report the Blender-unavailability blocker immediately, (2) ask the user how to proceed rather than guessing, (3) once directed, do everything genuinely possible without Blender — author `tools/blender/make-kael-fp-lowerbody.py` as real, structurally sound, ready-to-run code; extend `tools/inspect-operator.mjs` with a working, smoke-tested `--mode lowerbody` gate; register the manifest entry; update docs — while being explicit everywhere that no actual derivative exists yet.

Reason: a PATH search plus checks of `Program Files`, `Program Files (x86)`, and the user's `AppData\Local\Programs` found no `blender.exe` anywhere on this machine. `docs/forge/kael-v0.1-inspection.md` records the historical Kael pipeline work running via `E:\Program Files\Blender Foundation\Blender 5.2\blender.exe` — a path that doesn't exist in this environment, confirming that work was done in a different session/machine, not one available here. Every acceptance-gate item in Step 8B's brief (an exported GLB, deformation-test results, Blender screenshots, baked textures) depends on actually executing Blender, which is impossible here. Writing the extraction/materials script BLIND, with no way to run or verify a single line of `bpy`/`bmesh` code against real geometry, carries a specific, previously-demonstrated risk on this exact rig: the arms pipeline's own history includes a shoulder-boundary decimation defect that shipped invisibly once, tested by numeric gates that all passed, only caught later by an actual render. Claiming a lower-body derivative was "built" without ever rendering it would repeat exactly that failure mode, deliberately.

Rejected: silently attempting the full pipeline and reporting confident-sounding but fabricated numbers (obviously wrong — would actively mislead whoever reads the final report). Rejected: stopping entirely with no file changes (the user's chosen path, "I'll run Blender myself," explicitly asked for the ready-to-run scaffolding instead — doing nothing would have under-delivered against that direction). Rejected: attempting to install or locate a portable/alternative Blender build to force execution — outside this task's scope and risks introducing an unreviewed binary dependency into the project's tooling without the user's explicit request.

Chosen: `make-kael-fp-lowerbody.py` mirrors `make-kael-fp-arms.py`'s proven structure closely (same bone-resolution pattern, same connected-component/boundary-loop/scalpel-cleanup mechanisms, same deformation-test methodology, same export settings) specifically so the parts of the design that ARE proven (on this exact rig) carry over with justified confidence, while everything lower-body-specific (bone sets, waist boundary, contamination check, region-classification thresholds for materials) is clearly flagged in comments as "measure and verify when actually run, do not trust the seed values as final" — matching the arms script's own "measure, don't guess" discipline. The JS-side inspector extension (`--mode lowerbody`) IS fully real and IS verified: smoke-tested against both existing GLBs, correctly identifying `operator-kael-arms.glb` as 100% upper-body-contaminated and `operator-kael.lod1.glb` as textureless with real upper-body contamination — proving the contamination-detection logic works correctly against known-answer test cases, not just that it runs without crashing.

**Next step**: whoever has Blender access runs `measure` first against the real source (`WindArms Assets/Characters/Operator01_Kael/kael_v0.1_source.glb`, confirmed present in this checkout despite being git-ignored), reads the resulting weight-distribution histogram, picks a genuine threshold from that data (not from a guessed number in this script), runs `candidates` to compare a few threshold values' actual structural quality, then `final` and `materials`. The output GLB should be validated with `node tools/inspect-operator.mjs public/v2-art/operator-kael-lowerbody.glb --mode lowerbody` before it's treated as accepted.
