**2026-07-26 — Step 8B live Blender session: the triangle budget was raised from 15,000-17,000 to 60,000 after a measured quality curve proved the original budget could not produce clean topology on this mesh**

Decision: `LOWERBODY_BUDGET_TRIS`/`LOWERBODY_TARGET_TRIS` (in `make-kael-fp-lowerbody.py`, mirrored in `tools/inspect-operator.mjs` and `manifest.ts`) are 62,000/60,000, not the brief's original 17,000/15,000.

Reason: with real Blender access (the user's own machine, `E:\Program Files\Blender Foundation\Blender 5.2\blender.exe`), `measure` and `candidates` both ran cleanly and confirmed threshold 0.15 as the best extraction (15 connected components, 6 loose vertices, zero upper-body contamination, most waist coverage). But `final` at the original 15,000-17,000 budget (an ~87-88% reduction from the raw 127,725-tri selection) failed its own safety gates every way it was tried — narrower decimation protection, wider protection, a larger pre-scalpel target within the same ceiling — and a render of each failing attempt showed why: the mesh was visibly riddled with holes scattered across the thighs and shins, not just at the waist-cut boundary. A disciplined diagnostic ladder (four independent measurements: 60,000/40,000/26,000/17,000 tris, same threshold, same source) established a clean, monotonic relationship between reduction aggressiveness and defect rate: 60,000 tris (~53% reduction) rendered completely clean (0.3% scalpel-removal, sharp panel-line detail, zero visible holes); 40,000 (~69% reduction, 1.37% scalpel removal — nearly identical to the arms derivative's own healthy 1.4%) still showed small visible blemishes; 26,000 and 17,000 were both clearly broken. This mesh's raw selection evidently carries denser small-scale panel/plating detail than the arms' shoulder/hand region did, so the arms derivative's own successful ~78% reduction ratio (95,700→21,000) did not transfer to a ~87-88% ask here.

Rejected: shipping the 15,000-17,000 result anyway with the safety gates loosened to let it through — the render evidence made this an easy rejection; a visibly perforated leg is not a shippable asset regardless of what the numeric gates say. Rejected: settling for 40,000 tris despite its still-visible minor blemishes, without checking whether something between 40k and the confirmed-clean 60k might also be clean — raised to the user as an explicit option (the "probe 45k-50k first" choice) rather than assumed away. Rejected (implicitly, by not attempting it): reducing the raw pre-decimation selection itself via a much higher extraction threshold (e.g. 0.70) to lower the overall reduction burden — not explored because the 0.15 threshold's much better waist coverage and cleaner candidate-stage structure were already established, and the diagnostic ladder found a working answer within the 0.15 selection without needing to trade that away.

Chosen: present the full measured curve to the user, including the one number PROVEN clean by an actual render (60,000) rather than an interpolated guess, and let them make the budget call rather than unilaterally deciding to blow past a brief-stated hard ceiling by more than 3x. User approved 60,000. A related, second recalibration was needed once extraction succeeded at that budget: the arms script's borrowed `SMALL_ACCIDENTAL_HOLE_EDGE_LIMIT` (6 edges) was still flagging the confirmed-clean 60,000-tri result as containing "accidental holes" (up to 20 edges) — but a render of that EXACT geometry had already been visually confirmed clean before this limit was touched, proving the limit itself, not the mesh, was miscalibrated for this boundary's naturally more complex multi-panel structure. Raised to 25 (real margin above the observed max of 20, backed by the already-collected visual evidence, not a blind guess) — this, plus the two real script bugs found and fixed while iterating live (a BMesh-use-after-free `ReferenceError`, and a Blender API misuse assigning a socket directly to `inputs[]` instead of using `links.new`), got `final` and `materials` both to PASSED, and the resulting asset passes `--mode lowerbody` clean (0 errors, 0 warnings).

Known limitation carried forward: the render camera framing for the "boots" close-up view cuts off part of the boots at frame edges — a render-camera-parameter issue, not a mesh defect (confirmed by the front/waist-closeup renders showing the same boot geometry fully and cleanly). Worth a small camera-framing fix in a future pass; does not affect the asset itself.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 8B.1: thigh/shin decimation artifacts required a targeted cleanup pass, and two repair techniques were tried and rejected before one actually worked**

Decision: added `tools/blender/fix-kael-fp-lowerbody-topology.py`, a post-process-only pass on the already-exported `operator-kael-lowerbody.glb`, and used it to repair isolated thigh/shin triangulation defects rather than re-running extraction/decimation. Re-shipped the GLB (58,457 tris, was 59,809; 34,689 verts, was 35,591).

Reason: the round-2 visual gate (boots-framing fix + brightened FP look-down) surfaced fine speckled shading on the thighs/shins. A wireframe render on a completely textureless flat-grey material (ruling out texture bake or lighting as the cause) confirmed it was real topology — small, localized patches of noticeably denser, less orderly triangulation than the clean, flowing edge loops everywhere else on the same leg. The user's own framing was exactly right: acceptable for a background NPC, not for a first-person body sitting close to camera where PBR normals, knee-bend deformation, and brighter maps would make those patches shimmer or shade badly. The user set the visual gate to FAIL and asked for a small, targeted fix — not a re-decimation, not a new milestone.

Rejected (detection): a local-vs-regional face-area density ratio (compare each face's immediate-neighborhood mean area against a broader regional mean, flag large drops). This over-triggered badly on the first run — 398 clusters, roughly a third of the entire mesh, including large swaths of the boot and hip/pelvis regions. Those regions are *deliberately* denser than mid-leg fabric (the original extraction protects foot/toe/leg/upleg/hips/pelvis fragments from aggressive decimation), and the user had already approved the boots/waist renders as clean — density alone can't distinguish "denser by design" from "actually disordered," so this signal would have "fixed" geometry that was never broken and been rejected on a second review for exactly that reason.

Rejected (detection, accepted instead): per-triangle shape quality `Q = (4√3·area)/(e1²+e2²+e3²)` (1.0 for equilateral, →0 for a degenerate sliver), with candidate faces restricted up front to the thigh/shin height band (16%-70% of derivative height) so boots and hip/pelvis structurally can never be flagged regardless of score. This is the signal that actually correlates with "looks disordered under shading" rather than "is merely smaller." Verified BEFORE any repair by rendering the flagged faces highlighted red on a disposable duplicate mesh and comparing directly against the round-2 wireframe screenshots — the flagged patches matched the visible speckling closely, while the deliberate rectangular accent-panel details were correctly left unflagged.

Rejected (repair, attempt 1): `bmesh.ops.dissolve_limit` at a conservative 4° angle limit. Produced a real but tiny ~0.7% global vertex/face reduction, and a re-render showed a result visually indistinguishable from the un-repaired mesh — not shipped as a fix on the strength of a passing number alone.

Rejected (repair, attempt 2): the same operator raised to 15°. A meaningfully larger global reduction (~4.6%), but the re-render *still* looked the same as the original defect by eye. Root cause understood only after this second failure: `dissolve_limit` is an angle/coplanarity-based tool, built for removing unnecessary subdivision on surfaces that are locally flat but over-tessellated — these patches are genuinely *chaotic* (erratic, not smoothly-varying, face orientations), which is the wrong problem for that tool regardless of the angle threshold chosen.

Chosen (repair): `bmesh.ops.dissolve_faces(faces=<flagged>, use_verts=True)` — merges the flagged faces into one opening regardless of angle and drops the now-redundant interior vertices, applied across all flagged clusters in one combined call (a sequential per-cluster version crashed with a BMesh reference error once one cluster's operation touched a vertex shared with another) — followed by `bmesh.ops.triangulate(quad_method="BEAUTY", ngon_method="BEAUTY")` and `bmesh.ops.beautify_fill` to re-fill the same opening cleanly using only its existing boundary vertices. No new vertices are ever created, so no skin-weight or UV interpolation was needed — the boundary loop (the seam with untouched, good mesh) is never touched since it isn't part of the flagged face set.

Chosen (validation methodology): trusted the detector's own re-measurement over a second eyeball pass, after the eyeball read of the FIRST successful repair wrongly concluded "no visible difference." Re-running detection against the repair's own output is what actually proved the fix worked: pass 1 took 265 clusters (5,416 flagged faces) down to 25 clusters (242 faces) — a ~95% reduction; pass 2 took that down to 5 clusters (51 faces) — ~99% total reduction; pass 3 produced zero further change, a stable fixed point, accepted as the final state rather than chasing a diminishing-returns tail past a genuine plateau. This is the same "measure, don't eyeball" discipline as the Step 8B triangle-budget curve — a render at normal zoom is not always sufficient to judge a small, localized topology change, but a deterministic recount is.

Known limitation: 51 residual flagged faces (0.09% of the final mesh) remain in a handful of tiny, isolated spots after convergence — visually negligible in the final render set, but not literally zero. Not chased further since three consecutive passes agreed this was the operator's genuine limit for these specific boundary shapes, not a bug.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 8C: publish the player's own already-computed kinematic-body pose, never re-derive it from `camera.position`**

Decision: `firstPersonBodyPose.ts` is written every frame by `PlayerController.tsx`/`RangeController.tsx` from the SAME `nextX/nextY/nextZ`/`yaw.current` values those controllers already compute for the Rapier kinematic body and the camera — never from `camera.position`/`camera.rotation` after the fact.

Reason: the Step 8C brief itself flagged this as an audit question ("would deriving body position from camera position accidentally include camera bob, recoil, or future view offsets"). The audit found the answer today is "no" (recoil/`viewKick` only ever touches pitch/yaw, never position) — but "no today" isn't the same guarantee as "structurally can't happen," and a future camera-only positional effect (landing dip, ADS lean, a screen-shake system) would silently leak into the visible body's world position if the body read `camera.position` directly. Reading the controllers' own pre-camera translation is immune to that entire class of future bug by construction, not by convention.

Rejected: deriving body position from `camera.position - [0, EYE_STAND, 0]`. Simpler to write, but couples the body to every future thing the camera does, and there is no way to test "the body position is a pure function of the physics translation" if it never actually reads that translation directly. Rejected: growing `rangeLocalPose.ts` to also carry world position — that bus already has an established shape/consumer (the weapon/viewmodel systems reading yaw/pitch/speed); mixing in a second consumer's needs risks a future change to one breaking the other, the same reasoning `gripWorldPose.ts`'s own doc comment gives for staying separate from `muzzleWorldPose.ts`.

Chosen: a dedicated bridge, `firstPersonBodyPose.ts`, reusing `gripWorldPose.ts`'s proven generation-counter contract verbatim (a fresh mount bumps a counter; publish/invalidate calls are rejected unless they carry the CURRENT counter — the same protection against a route-remount's straggler frame or a just-unmounted instance's in-flight `useFrame`). `/v2/range`'s `RangeController.tsx` has no match store/respawn-nonce concept, so it grew a small local counter bumped on its own kill-Y recovery-volume branch, giving the bridge a real teleport signal on both routes without inventing match-store concepts where none exist.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 8C: the lower-body's world-position/yaw reference points differ from the physics capsule's by a measurable, derivable amount — found and confirmed via real-browser calibration, not guessed**

Decision: `LOWERBODY_CANONICAL_LOCAL_OFFSET` (`lib/v2/operators/lowerBodyTransform.ts`) is `[0, -(PLAYER.HALF_HEIGHT + PLAYER.RADIUS), -0.1]` — always applied, on top of which the `?body=1` debug panel's own offset (default `[0,0,0]`) is an ADDITIONAL fine-tune.

Reason: the published `firstPersonBodyPose` world position is `RigidBody.translation()` — for a `CapsuleCollider`, the CAPSULE'S CENTER (a physics reference point). The lower-body GLB's own local origin is its FEET (bounds min y ≈ 0, per `tools/inspect-operator.mjs --mode lowerbody`). With zero Y offset, the mesh's feet land at the capsule's CENTER height (~1.01m) instead of the ground (~0.01m) — the body renders roughly 1m too high. This was found via a real, live debugging session: the first browser render showed literally nothing where the body should be; a temporary diagnostic (logging `container.position`/`instance.visible`/a world-space `Box3` every ~1.5s) proved the geometry WAS correctly positioned in world space and WAS visible — the mesh was simply positioned too high to be in the ordinary forward-looking frustum. Applying `-(PLAYER.HALF_HEIGHT + PLAYER.RADIUS)` = -1.0m (the exact vertical distance from the capsule's center to its ground-contact bottom) is not a calibration fudge factor, it's a derived coordinate-frame reconciliation between two different, both-correct reference points.

A genuine, separate mystery followed: even with the Y correction applied, steep-downward-pitch screenshots showed a rounded, bilaterally-symmetric double-lobed shape that was initially — and wrongly — assumed to be an unrelated scene prop (a "range target"). Confirmed via a `Box3Helper` literally wrapping that exact shape (a helper built from `setFromObject(instance)` can only ever enclose the real mesh) that this WAS the lower body — specifically the character's own hips/pelvis seen from almost directly above, an angle nobody normally sees their own body from and so didn't immediately recognize. This resolved the "why isn't the body visible" question as "it always was — the confusion was a misidentified silhouette from an unfamiliar top-down angle," not a positioning bug.

A THIRD, separate and real finding: even after both of the above were resolved, a natural 30-45° downward glance (the brief's own hoped-for acceptance angle) showed almost nothing, because with zero forward offset the body's vertical centerline sits EXACTLY under the eye — at a moderate pitch, the look-ray reaches the ground meters ahead of the player before it would ever cross the body's own ~0.3m-deep column (this is normal FPS camera geometry: a 45°-down look-ray meets the ground ~1.7m forward of a 1.71m-high eye, not directly beneath it). A small forward (-Z, "yaw 0 looks down -Z" per `PlayerController.tsx`'s own established convention) nudge — empirically tuned via real-browser screenshots at several values, landing on -0.1m — brings the waist into a more natural downward-glance angle. This term is NOT derived like the Y term; it's a genuine, if modest, tuned calibration value, honestly distinguished as such in the code comment.

Rejected: touching `PLAYER.EYE_STAND` or the collision capsule to fully fix the remaining proportion mismatch (the character's own eye-to-waist gap, ~0.5m, reads shorter than a real human's ~0.9-1.1m, since `EYE_STAND` was tuned purely for FPS movement feel, years before any visible-body system existed) — both are explicitly protected in the Step 8C brief's hard-constraints list ("player collision capsule," implicitly "camera near/far planes unless a proven blocker is found" extends to the closely-coupled eye-height convention). Rejected: masking the mismatch by exaggerating the forward offset further — tested at -0.2m, which did make the waist appear sooner, but at a distance close enough to risk reading as an extreme fisheye close-up rather than a natural body view; -0.1m was chosen as a smaller, safer compromise given the underlying proportion gap can't be fully closed within this pass's constraints anyway.

Chosen: ship both terms as one canonical, always-applied constant, with the proportion-gap limitation documented plainly (`changelog.md`, `docs/gameplay/skyfront-trial.md`) rather than implied to be fully solved. Numerically confirmed via direct measurement (not just eyeballing): a real-browser pitch sweep recorded the exact `camera pitch` value at each screenshot via the debug panel's own live readout, so the reported "waist visible starting ~40-50°" claim is backed by logged numbers, not an approximate guess from looking at images.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 8C: yaw-tracking correctness was verified by reading the published numbers directly, not by eyeballing screenshots**

Decision: the claim "the lower body's effective yaw exactly tracks the player's own look-yaw, with no lag/reversal/mismatch" is backed by a real-browser test that read `player yaw`/`effective yaw` from the `?body=1` debug panel's own live readout at three successive turns (0.000 → -0.828 → 1.012), not by visually judging whether a rendered silhouette "looked" correctly rotated.

Reason: at the pitch levels where this test was run, the body wasn't necessarily even in frame (a screenshot-only check would have shown an empty background and proven nothing) — the debug panel's readout reads the SAME `getFirstPersonBodyWorldPose()`/yaw-offset values `KaelFirstPersonLowerBody.tsx`'s own per-frame transform consumes, so confirming the NUMBER is correct is a direct, stronger proof than confirming a screenshot LOOKS correct. This complements, not replaces, the earlier `lowerBodyTransform.test.ts` unit tests (which prove the pure math is correct in isolation) — this real-browser check additionally proves the live gameplay controller's yaw value actually reaches that math unmodified, end to end.

Chosen: recorded as an explicit real-browser validation step in the final report, distinguished from unit-test coverage (which proves the function is correct) and from screenshot review (which proves the RENDER looks correct) — three different, complementary kinds of evidence for the same "yaw is followed, pitch is not" requirement.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 8C: performance numbers from this session's headless-Chromium environment are reported as directionally-only, not as real hardware data**

Decision: a frame-timing A/B (lower body visible vs hidden, via the debug panel's own visibility toggle, measured with `requestAnimationFrame` deltas over several seconds) was run and its raw numbers are recorded in `changelog.md`, but explicitly labeled as NOT representative of real hardware performance.

Reason: the measurement environment (headless Chromium via Playwright, no real GPU, SwiftShader software rasterization) rendered the ENTIRE existing scene at ~3-4fps baseline even WITHOUT this pass's changes — a rate so far below real-time that it reflects software-rasterization overhead dominating everything on screen, not a usable signal for "how much does this one 58,457-triangle mesh cost on real hardware." Presenting the measured delta (WITH ≈330ms/frame vs WITHOUT ≈258ms/frame) as if it estimated real in-game FPS impact would be actively misleading — a real GPU handles a single additional 58K-tri, one-draw-call skinned mesh at a small fraction of a millisecond, nothing like this ratio.

Rejected: omitting a performance section entirely (the brief explicitly asks for one). Rejected: reporting the raw numbers without the caveat (would read as real performance data to a future reader who didn't see this reasoning).

Chosen: report the structural/analytical facts that ARE reliable regardless of environment (58,457 tris / 1 draw call / 1 skinned mesh / 3 embedded 1024px textures / 3.51MB, on-demand-loaded not bundled — `/v2/range`'s JS bundle grew only 179kB→181kB), note that V2 has no live quality-preset switch today (unlike v1's auto-adjusting `GameCanvas.tsx` — `useGraphicsStore.quality` never changes from its 'high' default on `/v2/range`/`/v2/play`, so "test Low/Medium/Max" doesn't correspond to a real switchable axis yet), and flag real-hardware GPU measurement as an explicit, open validation gap for whoever reviews this pass with real hardware access — not fabricated, not silently skipped.

---

**2026-07-26 — Step 8C.1: the human visual gate on Step 8C correctly failed the result, and a rigorous measure-diagnose-test loop (not another guess) found a visual-only fix**

Decision: `LOWERBODY_CANONICAL_LOCAL_OFFSET`'s Y term becomes the Step 8C derived capsule-to-feet term (-1.0m) plus a further empirical -0.2m (-1.2m total); the Z (forward) term increases from Step 8C's -0.1m to -0.5m. Both changes are visual-only — `PLAYER.EYE_STAND` and the collision capsule are untouched.

Reason (measurement first): a Blender script measured the asset's real rest-pose bone world heights directly (not inferred from screenshots) — feet≈0.01m, ankle≈0.11m, knee≈0.54m, hip≈1.01m, waist-cut≈1.16m (matching the mesh's own recorded bounds exactly, confirming the measurement was sound). Combined with the real eye height (1.71m = capsule half-extent 1.0m + `PLAYER.EYE_STAND` 0.7m), eye-to-knee (1.16m) and eye-to-ankle (1.58m) were already in a plausible human range, but eye-to-waist (0.54m) was notably short. This is the answer to the brief's own question ("does the eye-to-waist gap come from body placement, source skeleton proportions, camera eye height, or a combination") as precisely as it can be answered: it's concentrated in the upper body specifically, not a uniform whole-body mismatch — which matters mechanically, because it means no single RIGID translation (which shifts every landmark by an identical amount) can fully close the waist gap without pushing the already-reasonable knee/ankle past their own correct range.

Reason (diagnosis second, not assumed): added a real diagnostic, `showSkeletonLandmarks` (`bodyDebugStore.ts`/`KaelFirstPersonLowerBody.tsx`) — colored markers at the actual hip/knee/ankle/waist bone world positions, resolved once via `skeleton.getBoneByName(...)` and updated every frame via `bone.getWorldPosition()`. Combined with the existing neutral-material toggle, confirmed via direct screenshot (a `Box3Helper`-style proof, not an assumption) that the dominant rounded silhouette genuinely was the character's own pelvis/upper-thigh region — ruling out duplicated geometry, an orientation bug, and a detached-object misread, per the brief's own required checklist. The deeper mechanism: at near-vertical pitch with a small or zero forward offset, the camera looks nearly straight down the LENGTH of the standing leg — an axial/end-on view. A cylinder viewed end-on shows only a small circular cross-section regardless of its length; this is why the pelvis "dominated" — not because it was mispositioned, but because the viewing angle made every lower landmark foreshorten into an indistinguishable stack directly beneath it.

Rejected: pure vertical (Option A) offsets, tested up to an additional -0.5m beyond the Step 8C canonical term (total -1.5m). Real screenshots at every additional increment (-0.3m, -0.5m) showed the SAME dominant pelvis dome, just smaller/more distant (camera-to-body-root distance grew from 1.703m to 2.202m) — confirms the axial-viewing-angle theory: a uniform shift can't change the VIEWING ANGLE, only the distance, so it can shrink the dominant near-object but never reveal the leg's side profile. This was the correct, disciplined thing to test and rule out BEFORE reaching for a forward offset, per the brief's own required Option A/B/C sequence — not skipped.

Chosen (what worked): a substantially larger forward offset. Real matrix tested: Z=-0.15/-0.3/-0.35/-0.4/-0.45 paired with several Y values (-0.15 to -0.3 additional). All of the Z≥0.35-in-magnitude candidates produced a genuine, recognizable pelvis→thigh→knee(visibly bent)→shin→boot silhouette — confirmed at 45°, 60°, 70°, and near-maximum pitch, not just one angle. Z=-0.5m paired with Y=-0.2m (additional) was selected as the final value: consistently the clearest silhouette across the full tested pitch range in both `/v2/range` and `/v2/play`, without the body reading as detached from the player (camera-to-body-root distance 1.965m, still close enough to read as "attached," unlike the much-larger distances the pure-vertical attempts required for a lesser result).

Grounding, checked not assumed: the additional -0.2m Y means the feet sit ~0.2m below the capsule's literal ground-contact point (a real, quantified trade-off, not hand-waved). Verified in the browser — bright neutral-material steep-down shots (where a floor/geometry boundary would be most visible if present), the existing `/v2/play` arena's clearly-lit floor at the Step 8C baseline offset, normal walking, jump, and landing — no visible floor-clipping artifact was observed in any of these at any tested angle in either scene. Documented as a real, deliberately-accepted, small trade-off per the brief's own instruction ("when a small amount of hidden floor penetration is necessary and not visible to the player, document it honestly") — not silently absorbed.

Classification (per the brief's required Section 6 decision): **PASS WITHOUT EYE-HEIGHT CHANGE.** A visual-only transform correction produced a convincing, coherent body silhouette across the full required pitch range with no visible grounding defect — `PLAYER.EYE_STAND` and the collision capsule were never touched, exhausting the visual-only option space first exactly as instructed, rather than reaching for the eye-height convention as a first resort.

Known limitation carried forward unchanged from Step 8C: this character's own eye-to-waist proportion is still shorter than a generic real human's even after this fix (the fix corrects the SILHOUETTE/viewing-angle problem, not the underlying proportion gap itself, which remains out of scope) — the waist reads a little closer than a textbook-perfect result would, but no longer as an isolated, disconnected blob.

---

**2026-07-27 — Step 8D: bone posing is expressed in world-relative swing axes, never guessed bone-local Euler angles**

Decision: `lowerBodyRig.ts`'s `applyLowerBodyLocomotionPose` poses every leg/pelvis bone by computing its desired WORLD orientation (rest orientation, re-expressed at the container's current yaw, further rotated by a small angle around the CHARACTER's world-space local-right/local-forward axes, computed fresh each frame from the container's live world quaternion) and only converts to a bone-local quaternion as the final step.

Reason: this asset's Mixamo/Blender-exported rig gives no guarantee that a bone's own local X/Y/Z axes correspond to any particular anatomical direction (hip flexion, knee bend) — a naive `bone.rotation.x += swingAngle` approach would require correctly guessing that axis for 7 different bones, with a wrong guess manifesting as exactly the failure modes the Step 8D brief explicitly forbids ("knees bending sideways," "legs crossing," "feet rotating backward"). The arm IK system already in this codebase (`kaelArmRig.ts`'s `ArmRestMetrics`) solves an analogous problem by measuring each bone's rest orientation CONTAINER-RELATIVE once, then recomposing with the container's live transform every frame — reusing that same technique for a much simpler additive-swing (not full IK) case sidesteps the axis-guessing problem entirely, at the cost of one extra `getWorldQuaternion` read per bone per frame (cheap; bones are shallow, unchanging-topology chains).

Rejected: guessing a per-bone local axis convention (would require empirically discovering it separately for hip/knee/ankle/pelvis, then hoping the same axis holds under yaw); building a from-scratch parent-to-child manual quaternion-composition chain to avoid the extra scene-graph reads (unnecessary — `Object3D.getWorldQuaternion` self-corrects its own ancestor chain regardless of write order within the same frame, already verified and documented for this exact rig family in `kaelArmSolve.ts`'s doc comment, so there is no real staleness risk to engineer around).

Chosen: measure each bone's rest quaternion relative to the container once at mount (`buildLowerBodyRigRuntime`), then every frame compute `desiredWorldQuat = swingOffsetQuat * containerWorldQuat * restQuatContainerRelative` and convert to local via the bone's actual (freshly-read) parent world quaternion. The only axes ever used for legs are the container's own live local-right (pitch/swing) — never yaw or roll — which structurally prevents sideways knee bends and leg-crossing by construction, not by convention alone. Verified by a dedicated test (`lowerBodyRig.test.ts`) that applies a pose at two different container yaws and confirms the resulting WORLD rotation actually differs (proving the swing follows live yaw, not a frozen axis), and by a left/right symmetry test (equal-magnitude opposite-sign input produces equal-magnitude rotation away from rest on both sides).

---

**2026-07-27 — Step 8D: takeoff detection reads THIS frame's vertical velocity; landing detection reads the PREVIOUS frame's — a real, test-caught asymmetry, not an oversight**

Decision: `lowerBodyLocomotionPose.ts`'s takeoff-envelope trigger checks `verticalVelocity` (the value published THIS call); the landing-envelope trigger's strength calculation uses `state.prevVerticalVelocity` (the value from the PREVIOUS call, before this one).

Reason: `PlayerController.tsx`/`RangeController.tsx` set `vel.y = PLAYER.JUMP_VELOCITY` and flip `grounded` to false in the SAME frame a jump starts — so the true takeoff impulse is present in the very frame that publishes `grounded: false` for the first time, and reading it there is correct. Landing is the opposite: the controller's own grounded-clamp (`if (vel.y < 0) vel.y = -0.6`) runs in the SAME frame `grounded` flips to true, AFTER the real impact velocity has already been used to move the character — meaning by publish time, every landing's true impact speed has already been overwritten with a small fixed constant, regardless of how far the character actually fell. The only frame where the real pre-impact speed was ever visible is the previous one, while still airborne.

Caught by: an automated test (`lowerBodyLocomotionPose.test.ts`, "a real jump... triggers the takeoff envelope") that initially failed — the first implementation used `prevVerticalVelocity` for BOTH triggers (copy-pasted reasoning from the landing case without re-deriving it for takeoff), and the test's synthetic input (jump velocity published in the SAME call as `grounded: false`, matching the real controllers' actual behavior) caught the mismatch immediately. Not caught by inspection alone — this is exactly the kind of subtle, easy-to-miss timing asymmetry a same-frame-vs-previous-frame test forces out into the open.

Chosen: two different, individually-justified reads (documented inline at both call sites) rather than a single "always use the previous frame" rule that would silently under-detect every real jump (since `prevVerticalVelocity` on the leaving-ground frame still reflects whatever the character was doing WHILE grounded — near zero — not the jump impulse that only exists starting that same frame).

---

**2026-07-27 — Step 8D: dev-only preview/debug controls extend `bodyDebugStore`/`KaelBodyDebugPanel` rather than a new parallel tool**

Decision: locomotion preview mode, freeze-stride, stride-phase scrub, and locomotion-enabled all live in the existing Step 8C `bodyDebugStore.ts`/`KaelBodyDebugPanel.tsx`, gated the same `?body=1` way, rather than a new `?locomotion=1`-style tool.

Reason: every Step 8D control needs to interact with the Step 8C static-offset controls in the SAME frame (e.g. "preview the sprint gait at a specific pitch with a specific position offset") — splitting them into two separately-gated panels would force a developer to enable two flags and cross-reference two floating panels for what is conceptually one calibration session for one feature (the lower body). `resetLocomotion()` is a NEW, separate store action from the existing `reset()` (which still only touches the Step 8C position/yaw/marker fields) specifically so a developer can reset "just the animation controls" without losing a carefully-tuned static offset mid-session — verified by a dedicated test that resets locomotion and asserts the position offset survives untouched.

Chosen: one store, one panel, two independently-resettable groups of fields.

---

**2026-07-27 — Step 8D real-browser validation: headless SwiftShader rendering makes wall-clock-timed screenshots of short envelopes (takeoff/landing) unreliable; a state-polling capture technique was used instead**

Decision: the takeoff/landing screenshots for human review were captured by polling the debug panel's own live "locomotion state" readout text every ~8ms and screenshotting the instant it actually read `"takeoff"`/`"landing"`, not by guessing a wall-clock delay after switching preview mode.

Reason: this session's headless Chromium has no real GPU (SwiftShader software rasterization, consistent with the Step 8C/8C.1 performance-caveat precedent) and renders at roughly 3-4fps for this scene. The takeoff/landing envelopes are deliberately short (~160-280ms) to read as a "quick, believable" transient rather than a slow, obvious animation — but `deltaSeconds` is fed from each rendered frame's REAL elapsed wall-clock time (clamped at 100ms), so in this slow environment a single simulated frame can advance the timer by up to 100ms, and two slow frames can consume an entire 160ms envelope. A first attempt using a fixed `waitForTimeout(60)` after switching preview mode landed on `airRise`/`idle` (envelope already finished) rather than `takeoff`/`landing`, discovered by reading the panel's own state text rather than trusting the screenshot alone.

Rejected: lengthening the envelope durations to make them easier to screenshot in a slow environment (would mean shipping a WORSE, slower-reading transient for real players just to make automated capture easier — backwards priority). Rejected: presenting the mis-timed `airRise`/settled screenshots as "jump take-off"/"landing" evidence (would misrepresent what those states actually show).

Chosen: poll the already-existing live readout and capture reactively. This is a validation-tooling technique only — no production code changed to accommodate it. Also reconfirmed two Step 8C.1-established Playwright findings and found one NEW one this session: (1) DOM checkbox clicks remain unreliable once pointer lock is engaged (still true, re-verified — the neutral-material toggle silently failed to apply when clicked post-lock); (2) `<select>` `.selectOption()` and range-input `.fill()` remain reliable post-lock (used throughout for preview-mode switching and the stride-phase scrub slider); (3) NEW — a Playwright-synthetic `Escape` keypress does not reliably trigger Chromium's native pointer-lock-exit in headless mode, so `/v2/play`'s real match-pause (which depends on the `pointerlockchange` event) never fired when tested via `page.keyboard.press('Escape')`, even though `document.pointerLockElement` visibly stayed set — worked around by calling `document.exitPointerLock()` directly via `page.evaluate`, which fires the same underlying event a real Escape press does and correctly triggered `match.pause()`.

---

**2026-07-27 — Step 8D pause-freeze validated with a byte-level screenshot diff, not a visual approximation**

Decision: the claim "pause freezes the lower body's locomotion pose exactly" is backed by two `/v2/play` screenshots taken 900ms apart while genuinely paused (see the `exitPointerLock` finding above) being reported as **byte-identical** (`Buffer.equals()`, not a perceptual/threshold diff) — same file size, same bytes.

Reason: this is the strongest available proof that literally nothing in the render — camera, weapon, HUD, AND the lower body's bone poses/position — changed across a real elapsed-time gap while paused, which is exactly what "pause cannot advance the landing envelope"/"pause freezes the last valid pose" require. This works because `PlayerController.tsx` returns from its `useFrame` callback before ever calling `publishBodyWorldPose` while `match.phase === 'paused'` — `firstPersonBodyPose.ts`'s `updateTick` therefore never advances, `KaelFirstPersonLowerBody.tsx` reads `liveDelta = 0` for that stalled tick, and `computeLowerBodyLocomotionPose` with `deltaSeconds: 0` is provably a no-op (also covered by a dedicated unit test in `lowerBodyLocomotionPose.test.ts`).

Reason (a related, deliberately NOT re-tested claim): `/v2/range` has no equivalent real pause at all (`RangeController.tsx` never gates on any phase concept — Escape there only releases the pointer/input, physics and publishing continue) — this was already true before Step 8D and remains true; Step 8D's `updateTick`-based freeze mechanism is a general "did the authoritative writer stall" detector that would ALSO correctly freeze locomotion if `/v2/range` ever gained a real pause concept in the future, but there is nothing to prove today on that route specifically.

Chosen: report the `/v2/play` byte-identical result as the authoritative pause-freeze proof; note the `/v2/range` non-finding honestly rather than silently reusing the wrong route's screenshot as if it proved the same thing.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-A audit: Option B (full-body shadow-only rig, legs locomotion-synced, arms IK-solved to the real grip target) approved as the final architecture, but its own proposed Step 8E-B literal plan rejected**

Decision: the read-only Step 8E-A audit's architecture comparison (visible-body-reuse vs. full-body-shadow-only-with-pose-sync vs. simplified-proxy vs. composite) is settled on Option B. Its own proposed next step — mounting the relaxed-A-pose full body, disabling the existing FP-arm shadow, and requesting human shadow approval in the same pass — is explicitly rejected as the literal Step 8E-B.

Reason: the full-body GLB (`operator-kael.lod1.glb`) has zero authored animation clips (confirmed by direct GLB inspection, `docs/forge/kael-v0.1-inspection.md`) — its arms/spine render only the source's static bind-pose "relaxed A-pose," unrelated to the actually-held rifle. Following the audit's own proposed sequence would mean replacing a partial-but-plausible existing shadow (FP-arms casting, even if previously invisible near the player due to the frustum bug fixed in this same step) with a complete, but obviously WRONG, rifle-user silhouette, and then asking a human to sign off on it as if it were a real improvement. The audit itself flags this exact risk in its own risk section — proceeding anyway would be building something explicitly foreseen as regressive.

Rejected: the audit's literal 8E-B (full swap + disable old shadow + request final approval this pass). Rejected: the audit's proposed "Medium = relaxed static arms" quality tier — a coherent shadow is optional to skip, but a visibly wrong one is not an acceptable shipped tier at any quality level.

Chosen: a revised four-step sequence — 8E-B (dev-only shadow foundation + leg synchronisation ONLY, gated behind `/v2/range?shadow=1`, never touching the existing FP-arms shadow's normal-session behavior), 8E-C (world-space arm IK + exact weapon shadow — the part that actually makes the full body's pose correct), 8E-D (visual/light calibration), 8E-E (lifecycle/performance/quality gating and the final caster-ownership switch). The full-body shadow only ever becomes the SOLE caster once arms and weapon agree with the real held pose, not before.

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-B: the shadow body's root offset is physically derived, not the visible body's silhouette-tuned offset**

Decision: `KaelFirstPersonShadowBody.tsx` positions its root using a new constant, `SHADOW_BODY_PHYSICAL_LOCAL_OFFSET` (`shadowBodyTransform.ts`) — `[0, -(PLAYER.HALF_HEIGHT + PLAYER.RADIUS), 0]`, i.e. Y=-1.0m only, no X, no Z — deliberately NOT `LOWERBODY_CANONICAL_LOCAL_OFFSET` (the visible lower body's `Y=-1.2m/Z=-0.5m`, Step 8C/8C.1).

Reason: the visible body's offset is a two-part value — Step 8C's derived -1.0m capsule-center-to-feet reconciliation, PLUS Step 8C.1's empirical -0.2m/-0.5m(Z) silhouette nudge that exists SPECIFICALLY to fix an axial/end-on viewing-angle problem unique to a camera positioned at the player's own eye, looking down the length of its own standing leg (see the 2026-07-26 Step 8C.1 entry above). An externally-projected shadow is never viewed from that axial first-person angle — it's viewed from the light's direction, projected onto the floor — so importing the silhouette nudge would reproduce a correction for a viewing problem the shadow doesn't have, at the cost of a real, unexplained 0.2m vertical / 0.5m forward positional error versus the player's true physical location. Only the derived, physically-real capsule-to-feet term applies to the shadow.

Chosen: a dedicated constant with its own doc comment stating explicitly why it must never be unified with the visible body's offset, backed by a pure-function test (`shadowBodyTransform.test.ts`) proving capsule-center-to-feet placement, no camera-forward offset, yaw-only orientation (no pitch input), respawn snap, and finite output for any input. Live-verified: shadow root world Y (0.01) exactly equals the visible body's world Y (1.01) minus 1.0m, at every tested movement state.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-B: one authoritative locomotion computation, distributed via a generation-safe bridge, not two independent computations kept in sync by convention**

Decision: `KaelFirstPersonLowerBody.tsx` is the SOLE caller of `computeLowerBodyLocomotionPose()` on `/v2/range`. A new module, `lowerBodyLocomotionPoseBridge.ts`, publishes that already-computed result once per frame; `KaelFirstPersonShadowBody.tsx` only ever reads the published snapshot and applies it to its own separate rig runtime — it never computes a pose itself.

Reason: the brief's own hard requirement is that the visible and shadow legs can structurally never read as two different bodies walking slightly out of phase with each other — the audit flagged this as a real risk of any two-skeleton shadow design. Two independent calls to the same pure function, even with byte-identical inputs, is a coincidence of correct inputs, not a structural guarantee — a future edit to either call site (different rounding, a stray extra frame of latency, a different `deltaSeconds` source) could silently desync them with no compiler or type error to catch it. Reusing the exact generation-safe publish/read idiom already established for `firstPersonBodyPose.ts`/`gripWorldPose.ts` (stale-generation publishes/invalidates silently rejected, a route remount starts clean) means there is exactly one pose value in existence per frame, referenced by both consumers — not two values that happen to agree.

Chosen: `beginLowerBodyLocomotionPoseGeneration()`/`publishLowerBodyLocomotionPose()`/`invalidateLowerBodyLocomotionPose()`/`getSharedLowerBodyLocomotionPose()`, with the writer-side calls added immediately after `KaelFirstPersonLowerBody.tsx`'s own existing `computeLowerBodyLocomotionPose`/`applyLowerBodyLocomotionPose` calls (Step 8D code untouched, only extended). Verified by a 180-frame deterministic walk→sprint→jump→landing unit test asserting reference equality (`shared.pose === pose`) every single frame, and live-verified via the debug panel's own "visible state: X · shadow state: X (match)" readout holding true across idle/walk/sprint/jump-takeoff/airFall/landing and through a "freeze shared locomotion" test where both readouts stayed byte-identical 700ms apart while the player kept moving.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-B: the debug full-body shadow keeps the existing FP-arms shadow on everywhere except its own opt-in dev session**

Decision: while `/v2/range?shadow=1` is active, the visible FP-arms' `castShadow` is disabled ONLY for that debug session (so the new full-body clone is the sole visible caster while inspecting it); the moment the flag is absent, FP-arms cast exactly as before, and no full-body caster exists at all. This is NOT the final one-authoritative-caster ownership change described in the Step 8E-A audit.

Reason: the audit-approved end state (Option B, fully realized) is a single coherent full-body shadow that PERMANENTLY replaces the current partial FP-arms shadow for every player, every session. That switch is only correct once the full body's upper half actually matches the held weapon — i.e. after Step 8E-C's arm IK and weapon shadow land. Flipping real ownership now, before arms/weapon are solved, would make the ALWAYS-ON player-facing shadow worse (a relaxed-A-pose body with a floating invisible rifle) in exchange for legs that are honestly no more correct than the current partial shadow already provides. The scope correction from the user's brief is explicit on this point: "do NOT globally disable existing FP-arm shadows yet."

Chosen: ownership stays exactly as it was pre-8E-B for every normal session (with or without `?shadow=1` present in the URL matters not at all for a normal player — this flag is dev-only and stripped from production builds by `useShadowDebugEnabled.ts`'s `NODE_ENV` gate); only the debug session itself temporarily reassigns casting so the new prototype can be inspected in isolation without two overlapping, competing shadows confusing the reading. The debug panel carries a persistent amber warning ("UPPER-BODY SHADOW UNSYNCHRONISED — STEP 8E-C REQUIRED") specifically so this in-progress state is never mistaken for a finished feature by a future reader of a screenshot.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-B: the pre-existing `/v2/range` shadow-camera frustum bug was a real, already-shipped defect, fixed with a computed (not copied) bound**

Decision: `RangeScene.tsx`'s directional light now sets explicit `shadow-camera-{left,right,top,bottom,near,far}` props sourced from a new `RANGE_SHADOW_CAMERA_BOUNDS` constant (`±65/near1/far100`), replacing THREE.js's implicit default (an origin-centered `±5` orthographic frustum, since no `target` prop ever repositioned the light).

Reason: this is a real, already-shipped bug independent of anything in Step 8E-B's own new work — `/v2/range`'s own spawn point (`RangeController.tsx`: `[0, 3, 10]`) already sits outside a `±5` frustum, and the existing (already-`castShadow`-enabled) FP-arms shadow was very likely never visible anywhere near the player before this fix, on any prior milestone. `/v2/play`'s own light uses `±30`, which was tested and found to still clip most of this range's actual floor (`RANGE_FLOOR_SIZE`/`RANGE_FLOOR_CENTER`, x:[-18,18]/z:[-60,20]) — so `±30` was rejected as a copy-paste fix and a value computed from the real floor geometry was used instead.

Chosen: a single source-of-truth module, `rangeEnvironmentBounds.ts`, exporting both the floor's real size/center (now also consumed by `RangeEnvironment.tsx`, replacing inline literals) and the shadow bounds, plus `boundsCoverFloor()` — a conservative per-axis-from-origin coverage check. A regression test (`rangeEnvironmentBounds.test.ts`) proves the default `±5` bounds do NOT cover the real floor (confirming the bug was real, not hypothetical), that `/v2/play`'s `±30` would still clip it, and that the chosen `±65/near1/far100` does cover it — so a future accidental shrink of either the floor or the bounds is caught by a failing test rather than silently regressing back to an invisible shadow. Light color, intensity, and position were deliberately left untouched — only the shadow-camera frustum changed.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-B: recovery-volume teleport is NOT live-verified — reported as an open item, not silently promoted to an empirical pass**

Decision: the brief's validation-matrix item "recovery-volume teleport: no stale shadow after teleport" stays **unverified in the browser** as of this step. What IS reported is a structural argument plus existing unit-test coverage — this is explicitly weaker evidence than every other validation-matrix item (which were all live-triggered and screenshotted) and must not be read or cited as if it were a live pass. Carried forward as open until either a map with a reachable kill-plane edge exists, or Step 8E-E revisits it directly.

Reason: `RangeEnvironment.tsx`'s test range is a fully sealed box — four fixed walls (`x=±18`, `z=-59`/`z=19`, all 8 units tall) enclose the entire floor on every side, so the `RANGE_KILL_Y=-20` recovery volume (the same defensive kill-plane pattern as `/v2/play`) is structurally unreachable by any legitimate WASD movement on this map; there is no edge to walk off. Reaching it would require either an out-of-scope physics-clipping exploit or directly poking Rapier internals via injected JS — neither is a legitimate validation methodology, and both risk violating the brief's own "do not change movement physics" constraint by proxy.

Chosen: report the structural guarantee instead — `KaelFirstPersonShadowBody.tsx` never reads or reacts to `respawnNonce` itself; every frame it reads `pose.worldPosition`/`worldYaw` directly off `getFirstPersonBodyWorldPose()`'s current snapshot with no interpolation, smoothing, or cached previous-frame value. Since a respawn is nothing more than the SAME snapshot's position field changing to a new value between two frames, and the shadow has no separate position-tracking state of its own to go stale, there is no code path by which the shadow COULD lag behind a teleport — the "staleness" failure mode this test targets doesn't have a mechanism to occur, and `shadowBodyTransform.test.ts`'s existing "respawn snap" test (a discontinuous world-position jump produces an immediate, exact, finite output with no interpolation artifact) covers the same transform math at the unit level. Documented as an honest scope note for whoever builds Step 8E-C, rather than a claimed-but-unverified pass.

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C correction: Step 8E-B's own report overclaimed a "temporary shadow ownership" wiring that was never actually implemented**

Decision: flagged transparently, then fixed as part of this step's own Section 9 work, rather than silently patched or left standing.

Reason: Step 8E-B's final report (and the changelog/decisions entries written from it) stated that "while `?shadow=1` is active, the visible FP-arms' `castShadow` is disabled ONLY for that debug session." Re-auditing the actual Step 8E-B commit (`a5967fe`) at the start of this step — via `git show a5967fe --stat -- src/components/three/weapons/KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx`, which returned an EMPTY diff — proved that file was never touched by that commit at all. The real code (`node.castShadow = true`, unconditional, no `useShadowDebugEnabled()` check anywhere in the file) had been casting a shadow the entire time, regardless of the debug flag. The claim was false from the moment it was written.

Rejected: quietly fixing the gate in this step without mentioning the discrepancy (would let a false claim stand uncorrected in already-committed docs, and would look, to a future reader diffing this step against the last one, like new work rather than a correction of a specific prior misstatement). Rejected: treating this as embarrassing enough to omit from the changelog (the project's own standing rule — code wins over docs for "what is," and a docs/code mismatch is itself logged as a bug, not hidden — applies here exactly, see `ai-rules.md`'s sync rule referenced at the top of `CLAUDE.md`'s doc-loading order).

Chosen: state the discrepancy plainly in this step's own changelog entry and here, then implement the real gate as originally described — `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` now reads `useShadowDebugEnabled()` and sets `node.castShadow = !shadowDebugActive` in its existing mesh-traverse effect (dependency array extended to react to the flag). Verified live: unchanged (`castShadow=true`) on plain `/v2/range` and always on `/v2/play`; off only under `/v2/range?shadow=1`.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C: a canonical weapon-world pose already existed — no second bridge was built**

Decision: `KaelFirstPersonShadowWeapon.tsx` reads `gripWorldPose.ts`'s existing `weaponWorldPosition`/`weaponWorldQuaternion` fields directly. The brief's own Section 7 anticipated needing to "add a generation-safe imperative bridge... when one does not exist" — the Section 1 audit this step opened with found one already did.

Reason: `gripWorldPose.ts`'s own module doc comment (present since Milestone 7, Phase F, Step 5) documents `weaponWorldPosition`/`weaponWorldQuaternion` as existing specifically for "a dev-only consumer... to resolve a CANDIDATE local anchor into world space using the exact same live weapon transform this frame's real hands used" — i.e. this exact "a future world-space consumer needs the real weapon transform" use case was anticipated and built for over a month before this step needed it. `VortexViewmodel.tsx` already writes both hands' AND the weapon's own world position/quaternion atomically, every frame, after its full hip/ADS/sway/bob/recoil/reload/inspect composition — reading it is zero-cost and carries the exact same generation-safety guarantees (`gripWorldPose.test.ts`'s existing stale-generation-rejection test) a new bridge would have needed to reprove from scratch.

Rejected: building a new `shadowWeaponWorldPose.ts` bridge as the brief's own suggested shape sketches (a plausible design if the canonical pose genuinely hadn't existed, but building it here would have been a needless duplicate of an already-correct, already-tested service, plus a second thing to keep in sync with `VortexViewmodel.tsx`'s own transform chain going forward).

Chosen: `KaelFirstPersonShadowWeapon.tsx` is a pure reader of `gripWorldPose.ts` — no independent transform computation anywhere in that file, satisfying the brief's own "no independent recoil/action computation" requirement by construction rather than by discipline.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C: the shadow arm rig reuses the visible rig's generic machinery but never its config or debug state**

Decision: `shadowUpperBodyRig.ts` imports and calls `resolveKaelArmBones`, `measureArmRestMetrics` (via `buildSideRuntimeState`), and `solveTwoBoneIk` directly from the existing `kaelArmRig.ts`/`kaelArmSolve.ts`/`twoBoneIk.ts` modules — but defines its own `SHADOW_ARM_IK_CONFIG` (`shadowArmIkConfig.ts`) and `ShadowArmDebugSide` (`shadowArmDebugState.ts`), never importing `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG` or `KaelArmDebugSide`.

Reason: the underlying bone-resolution/rest-metrics/IK-math functions are already generic — they take a skeleton root, a container, and plain vectors/quaternions, with zero dependency on which specific config or debug object a caller happens to use. Reusing them avoids duplicating already-tested, non-trivial logic (bone-name candidate matching, container-relative rest-quaternion measurement, the law-of-cosines two-bone solve). But the CALIBRATION VALUES (elbow poles, shoulder assists, hand-basis adjustments) are measured against a physically different rig — this rig's container is world-anchored (the shadow body's own root, positioned at the physical player location per `shadowBodyTransform.ts`) where the visible arms-only rig's container is camera-relative (follows `camera.position`/`camera.quaternion` plus a static view-space offset); the two rigs' shoulder-to-target geometry is not the same problem even though both ultimately reach for the same real grip anchors. The brief's own explicit instruction — "Do not assume the visible FP-arm basis corrections can be copied directly... Measure and calibrate separately" — was borne out directly by measurement: the visible rig's approved left-shoulder-assist reach deficit was 7.36cm (Step 6G); this rig's own measured deficit for the same grip anchor was ~30cm, an order of magnitude different, because the geometry the assist has to bridge is different.

Rejected: importing `FIRST_PERSON_ARM_IK_CONFIG` and overriding only the fields that needed to differ (would leave a live, silent coupling where a future edit to the visible rig's config could accidentally change the shadow rig's behavior, or vice versa, with no compiler error to catch it — exactly the kind of structural risk this whole milestone's architecture has consistently avoided elsewhere, e.g. the Step 8E-B locomotion-pose bridge existing specifically so two consumers can't independently drift).

Chosen: two separate config/debug-state modules, sharing only the underlying generic math — verified structurally (no shared config import in either direction, confirmed by reading both files) and functionally (the shadow rig's own measured shoulder-assist value is nothing like the visible rig's, as expected for a genuinely different rig).

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C: the left-hand reach deficit was measured from real shoulder/target world positions, not estimated**

Decision: `SHADOW_ARM_IK_CONFIG.leftShoulderAssistLocal = [0.187, -0.117, -0.188]` — derived by adding temporary shoulder-world-position/target-world-position/reach-deficit readouts to `KaelShadowDebugPanel.tsx`, reading the REAL vectors live in the browser (right shoulder `[0.078, 1.479, 10.101]`... left shoulder `[-0.273, 1.479, 10.101]`, left target `[0.229, 1.166, 9.596]`, raw shoulder→target distance 0.778m, deficit 30.1cm), computing the exact world-space unit direction from shoulder to target, converting it into container-local space via the container's own inverse world quaternion (container yaw ≈0 at measurement time, so this conversion was close to identity but performed exactly rather than assumed), and scaling to 0.29m (≈96% of the measured 0.301m deficit).

Reason: per this step's own explicit calibration-order instruction ("do not tune wrists to hide bad hand positions... do not tune shoulders to hide incorrect weapon alignment"), the shoulder assist had to be derived from the REAL geometry, not iterated by eye — an assist chosen by visual guess-and-check could easily end up compensating for the wrong axis or overshooting into a different failure mode (e.g. an assist that "looks right" at one pose but is actually pointing 20° off the true shoulder→target direction, which would only become obvious at a different pose/pitch). Measuring the exact vectors first, then computing (not guessing) the assist direction, made the result verifiable arithmetic rather than a fitted-by-eye number.

Chosen: after applying this assist, both hands measured 0.00-2cm position error across idle/walk/sprint/jump-takeoff/falling/landed/reload/inspect at the same ~30-35° downward pitch used for measurement — well within the brief's own ≤2cm acceptance target. No assist was needed or applied for the right side (its own measured deficit was consistently negative — i.e. comfortably within reach, never clamped — at every tested pose).

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C: reach quality is genuinely camera-pitch-dependent, characterized by a real pitch sweep rather than assumed constant**

Decision: report the calibrated shoulder assist as valid specifically for the ~30°+ downward-pitch range it was measured at, with a documented, measured degradation curve at shallower pitches, rather than claiming a single fixed assist solves reach at every possible camera angle.

Reason: a route-remount test unexpectedly showed persistently elevated position error (5-17cm) that did NOT resolve over several seconds — ruling out a transient remount/settling bug, a real pitch sweep (0°/10°/20°/30°/40°/50°) was run and found a smooth, monotonic relationship: right-hand error 5.5cm→2.0cm→0.0cm→0.0cm→0.0cm→0.0cm, left-hand error 17.2cm→11.4cm→5.6cm→0.8cm→0.0cm→0.0cm as pitch increases from 0° to 50°. This is architecturally expected, not a bug: the shadow body's shoulder position is yaw-only/world-anchored (correct per Step 8E-B — it must not track camera pitch, since it represents the character's own physical body, not a camera-relative viewmodel), while the weapon (and therefore its grip targets) is fully camera-attached and swings through an arc as pitch changes. A FIXED shoulder-assist offset — the only kind this step's architecture provides — cannot fully compensate for a target that moves relative to the shoulder as pitch changes; it can only be tuned to be exactly right at one reference pitch (chosen here as the natural ~30-35° downward angle a player uses to look at their own weapon) and progressively short of that at other pitches.

Rejected: fixing this by increasing the assist magnitude further (would overshoot at the calibrated 30°+ range to reduce shortfall at pitch=0, trading a solved case for a still-imperfect one — worse on net, since 30°+ is the more representative "looking at your own gun" range). Rejected: silently reporting only the calibrated-pitch numbers without mentioning the sweep (would misrepresent the shadow as pitch-robust when it measurably isn't).

Chosen: report both — the calibrated range (≤2cm from ~30° on) as the real, verified result, and the pitch-dependence curve as an honest, characterized, NOT-hidden limitation. Confirmed the degraded case (pitch=0) fails SAFELY — reach-clamped, finite, no exploded geometry, no inverted elbow, no crossed limbs, just a visibly imperfect hand-to-grip gap — via the same fail-soft guarantees `solveShadowArmSide`'s unit tests already prove structurally. A pitch-adaptive assist (scaling the offset by pitch angle) is a plausible future fix, explicitly out of this step's scope, flagged for Step 8E-D.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C.1: the Step 8E-C pitch-dependence limitation was rejected as a stopping point and required a real architectural fix, not a bigger table of caveats**

Decision: build a genuine chest-aim frame (cascading Spine→Spine1→Spine2 rotation, solved BEFORE the arms) plus a pitch-aware dynamic per-side assist, rather than accepting Step 8E-C's "solved from ~30° on" as the final state.

Reason: the human reviewer of Step 8E-C's report correctly identified that a 17cm hand-to-grip gap at level pitch is not a minor visual-tuning gap — it is a visible detachment the player could plausibly notice even from outside their own first-person view (the shadow is projected by the world light, independent of what the camera itself is currently framing). "Solved for the calibrated range, degrades outside it" is a real, honestly-reported result, but it is not the same thing as "solved" — the acceptance target explicitly covers 0°–70°, not "the range we happened to calibrate at."

Rejected: incrementally increasing the Step 8E-C static assist's magnitude (already flagged in that step's own decision entry as a rejected option, and confirmed rejected again here for the same reason — it only trades one imperfect pitch for another, since a FIXED assist cannot track a target that moves continuously with pitch).

Chosen: the two-mechanism design described in the following entries — a real, bone-driven chest lean (anatomically visible, bounded, restrained) as the primary correction, and a dynamic, pitch-aware, per-side, SMOOTHED assist (replacing the static constant) for whatever the chest lean's own geometry can't reach. Both mechanisms' response curves were measured, not assumed.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C.1: a real sign-inversion bug — the "corrective" chest lean initially made the left-hand error WORSE, caught by live measurement, not code review**

Decision: `applyChestAimFrame` negates its `totalPitchRad` input internally before building the axis-angle rotation quaternion, with a dedicated regression test proving the corrected direction.

Reason: the first implementation used the exact same `pitchQuat.setFromAxisAngle(rightAxisWorld, pitchRad)` technique `lowerBodyRig.ts`'s leg-swing code already established, with a POSITIVE pitch intended to mean "lean forward/down." After wiring it up and re-running the pitch sweep, the LEFT hand's error got WORSE (17.3cm, versus ~17cm with no chest lean at all) rather than better. Direct before/after shoulder-position measurement confirmed the shoulder was moving AWAY from the target (Z increasing, away from the target's lower Z), not toward it. Working through the rotation by hand: rotating a world -Z (forward) vector by a POSITIVE angle around world +X (right), by the right-hand rule, tips it toward +Y (up) — the OPPOSITE of the intended forward/down lean. This is a easy, non-obvious sign trap (the SAME technique, with the SAME "positive pitch" framing, is correct for camera pitch elsewhere in this codebase, e.g. `RangeController.tsx`'s own convention — but that convention is about the CAMERA's own rotation being applied directly, not a delta rotation composed via `setFromAxisAngle` around a fixed world axis, which is a different operation with a different effective sign).

Rejected: leaving the bug in place and "compensating" by further increasing the assist magnitude (would have hidden a real, findable bug behind a bigger number, and the chest lean would have been doing net-negative work — silently making the assist's job harder, not easier).

Chosen: fix the sign at the source (inside `applyChestAimFrame`, not at every call site), verified via: (1) direct empirical re-measurement after the fix (shoulder-to-target distance dropped, confirming the corrected direction actually helps), and (2) a new permanent unit test (`shadowUpperBodyRig.test.ts`) that constructs a known rotation and asserts the chest's forward-direction world-Y component decreases (tips down) for a positive input pitch — this exact class of bug can never silently reappear.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C.1: the final chest-lean-vs-dynamic-assist split was measured, not assumed — a 34° chest lean alone could not close the gap**

Decision: `chestAimMaxPitchRad: 0.436` (25°, a restrained, anatomically-plausible "leaning into the shot" amount) carries a modest share of the correction; `leftMaxShoulderAssistM: 0.4` / `rightMaxShoulderAssistM: 0.1` (asymmetric, matching the same right-fine/left-short pattern every prior calibration pass in this milestone has found) closes the rest.

Reason: after fixing the sign bug, a real A/B measurement isolated the chest lean's OWN marginal contribution: at 0° chest lean, left deficit was 20.1cm; at a restrained 14°, 17.3cm (only ~2.8cm of help); at an anatomically-EXTREME 34° (already visually excessive per the brief's own "no exaggerated torso twisting" constraint), only 13.3cm (another ~4cm). This confirmed a structural limit: the shoulder's own lever-arm distance from the chest pivot (spine2) is short, so a bone ROTATION can only ever displace the shoulder by a bounded amount, however large the angle — even a genuinely excessive lean could not have closed the full ~38cm raw gap (computed directly from the measured pre-assist shoulder/target vectors) for the left hand alone.

Rejected: closing the entire gap via chest lean alone (would require an implausible, brief-violating torso angle). Rejected: closing the entire gap via a single SHARED (both-sides-equal) assist magnitude (the right side needed dramatically less correction than the left at every measured pitch — a shared cap would either starve the left or way overshoot the right).

Chosen: a modest, capped chest lean as the anatomically-visible primary mechanism, with the residual (asymmetric, per-side, pitch-varying) gap closed by the dynamic assist — verified by the full pitch×yaw matrix converging to ≤0.34cm everywhere once both pieces were correctly sized. `rightMaxShoulderAssistM`/`leftMaxShoulderAssistM` remain genuinely BOUNDED caps (never "unlimited reach" — the brief's own explicit prohibition) — the dynamic system only ever applies up to what the measured deficit actually calls for, clamped at these ceilings.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C.1: an apparent matrix failure (3.2–3.5cm at a few pitch=0/yaw=±45°/±90° points) was a test-harness artifact, confirmed by a dedicated settling-time sweep before being accepted as such**

Decision: report the pitch×yaw matrix at a realistic 450ms per-point settle time (the version that shows ≤0.34cm everywhere) as the authoritative result, with the shorter-settle-time anomaly explicitly documented as a test-methodology finding, not a real gameplay defect.

Reason: an initial full-matrix run (220ms settle per point) showed 4 of 35 points between 2.19cm and 3.55cm — all at pitch=0° paired with a yaw the test driver had JUST snapped to (via a single large, instantaneous mouse-delta injection). A dedicated settling-time sweep at exactly those points (measuring error at +220ms/+500ms/+1000ms/+2000ms/+3000ms after the same instantaneous yaw+pitch snap) found the SAME points converge to ≤0.14cm by 500ms and ~0.00cm by 1000ms — proving this was the dynamic assist's own smoothing catching up after an unrealistically large, instantaneous target jump, not an unclosed gap. Real mouse input never produces this kind of discontinuity (continuous small movements, not one giant instantaneous delta), so this transient was never something a real player's camera motion would produce in the first place — confirmed independently by the REAL recoil-transient test (genuine sustained fire, not a synthetic snap), which peaked at only 0.78cm.

Rejected: increasing the smoothing RATE to converge faster (would risk visible snapping/jitter during genuine gameplay pitch changes, trading a test-harness artifact for a real player-facing one). Rejected: silently using the artificially-fast 220ms matrix and omitting the discrepancy (would either overstate a false failure or, worse, invite "fixing" a problem that doesn't exist in real play).

Chosen: settle time increased to 450ms for the matrix report (comfortably past the measured 500ms convergence point with margin), the 220ms-vs-500ms+ discrepancy documented as an explicit test-methodology finding, and the real-fire recoil-transient test kept as the independent, harness-artifact-free confirmation that the ACTUAL in-game smoothing behavior is fast enough.

---

**2026-07-28 — Step 8E-C.1: the wrist rotation-error metric was rebuilt to be a genuine measurement, because the old one could not have shown a bad calibration even if one existed**

Decision: `solveShadowArmSide`'s `rotationErrorRad` now composes the actual resulting hand-bone world orientation with the FIXED, measured (never `tuning`-adjusted) `handBasisCorrection`, and compares THAT against the raw `targetWorldQuat` directly — replacing a comparison against a value that was itself algebraically derived from the same inputs earlier in the same function call.

Reason: Step 8E-C's version computed `handWorldQuatScratch` from `targetWorldQuat * effectiveBasisCorrection^-1`, then (at `rotationWeight=1`) set the hand bone to exactly that value, then compared the bone's actual resulting orientation against `handWorldQuatScratch` — a tautology that could only ever read ~0°, structurally, regardless of whether `effectiveBasisCorrection` (the TUNABLE component) was any good. This exact "use the FIXED correction, not the tunable one, or a bad tuning value can never show up" reasoning was already established in this codebase for the visible rig's own `palmForwardWorldDir`/`thumbSideWorldDir`/`palmNormalWorldDir` diagnostics (`kaelArmSolve.ts`) — Step 8E-C's `rotationErrorRad` simply didn't follow the same rule its sibling diagnostics already did.

Rejected: leaving the metric as-is and relying solely on visual screenshot review for wrist correctness (screenshots are still necessary — the brief's own Section 6 explicitly asks for them — but a numeric diagnostic that cannot detect a real problem is worse than no diagnostic, since it can be mistaken for evidence).

Chosen: the corrected formula, verified by a new test that deliberately sets a wrong (90°) hand-basis adjustment and asserts the reported error is now large — proving the metric is live, not decorative. At the CURRENT shipped tuning (`[0,0,0]` both sides, untuned), the corrected metric still reads ~0°, which is an honest, non-tautological result now — not evidence of a real, human-eyeballed "this looks anatomically correct" pass, which has not been done this pass and remains open for the next visual-calibration session.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.2: the shadow weapon is now CHEST-ANCHORED, not camera-relative — the human reviewer's own diagnosis that the camera-relative target was "intentionally camera-friendly, not necessarily reachable by a physically proportioned world-space character" was correct, and the fix is architectural, not a tighter cap**

Decision: `shadowWeaponPresentationPose.ts` (new) defines the shadow weapon's world POSITION as a small, fixed offset from the chest bone's own live world transform (never `gripWorldPose.ts`'s camera-relative `weaponWorldPosition`), with ORIENTATION still derived from the chest's own (now genuinely look-direction-following) orientation via a fixed muzzle-alignment quaternion. Right/left grip anchors and reload/inspect action targets are resolved against this new transform via the already-tested `resolveRuntimeAnchorWorldPose` — no new anchor data.

Reason: Step 8E-C.1's numbers passed (≤0.34cm everywhere) but only by permitting up to 0.4m/40cm of shoulder translation — the human reviewer rejected this explicitly: "That is not restrained shoulder assistance; it can effectively pull the shoulder away from the torso to make an otherwise unreachable target pass numerically." The root cause was structural: the camera-relative target's distance from a world-anchored (yaw-only, non-pitching) shoulder depends on camera pitch in a way no bounded, anatomically-plausible shoulder motion can fully close, especially at level pitch. Chest-anchoring removes the dependency entirely — the target is defined relative to a bone the arm rig already treats as its own reference frame, so its distance from the shoulder stays small and roughly CONSTANT regardless of camera pitch, yaw, ADS state, or movement (recoil, sway, bob never touch it either, since none of those are read by this new transform at all).

Rejected: incrementally lowering `leftMaxShoulderAssistM` alone without changing the target (would have reintroduced Step 8E-C's original level-pitch reach failure — the target genuinely isn't reachable within a tight cap from a camera-relative position). Rejected: keeping the camera-relative target but adding a THIRD correction mechanism on top of chest lean + shoulder assist (would treat the symptom a third time rather than the cause, and the brief's own Section 3 explicitly asked to consider exactly this redesign).

Chosen: the chest-anchored redesign, verified by a full pitch(0-70°)×yaw(6 values)×ADS×action-state real-browser matrix (30 points) all measuring 0.00cm error, 0.00cm assist, `reachClamped: false` — not just "small," genuinely zero correction ever triggered — plus a scene-free unit test (`shadowWeaponPresentationPose.test.ts`) proving both grip anchors resolve within 0.5m of the chest for a spread of chest world positions/yaws, independent of any mounted skeleton.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.2: the chest-lean curve changed from a reach-driven INVERSE shape to a genuine monotonic look-direction follow — reach is no longer this curve's job**

Decision: `computeChestAimPitch` (new, pure, in `shadowAimFrame.ts`) computes `clamp(smoothedAimPitch * chestAimFollowFraction, -chestAimMaxPitchRad, chestAimMaxPitchRad)` — MORE lean as the player looks further down (or back, looking up), capped at a believable maximum (30°). This replaces Step 8E-C.1's `chestAimMaxPitchRad`-at-pitch-0-relaxing-to-zero-by-`chestAimFalloffPitchRad` curve.

Reason: the old curve's shape was chosen entirely to help REACH (worst reach deficit was at pitch≈0°, so the curve leaned hardest there) — it had no relationship to representing "the character is looking in the aim direction," which is exactly what the human reviewer flagged: "screenshots labelled 30° and 60° show derived aim values of only 2.4°/4.3°... it does not prove the chest silhouette follows the player's real look direction." Once the weapon became chest-anchored (previous entry), reach stopped depending on the chest-lean curve's shape at all — freeing it to represent the ACTUAL thing it should: a torso that visibly, monotonically leans further as the player looks further down, matching a real shooter's stance.

Rejected: keeping the inverse curve and just also asking it to "look better" (the two goals — maximize reach help at low pitch, vs. represent real look direction — are fundamentally in tension; a curve can't do both). Rejected: making the chest track the FULL raw camera pitch 1:1 (would risk "exaggerated torso twisting," the brief's own explicit ceiling) — a bounded, restrained follow fraction (0.55) with a hard cap (30°) is the middle ground.

Chosen: the monotonic curve, verified by a dedicated pure-function test suite (`shadowAimFrame.test.ts`) proving monotonicity across a pitch sweep, exact symmetry between up/down, correct clamping at the cap, and proportional scaling with the follow fraction — plus the real-browser matrix showing `chestPitch` growing smoothly from ~1° (level) through 30° (capped, from pitch≈60° onward), tracking `aimPitch` at roughly the configured 0.55 ratio until the cap engages.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.2: found and fixed a real, previously undiagnosed bug — the aim-pitch SOURCE (`extractAimPitchFromWeaponQuaternion` against the weapon's own quaternion) under-reports true camera pitch by roughly 16x at large angles, which is exactly the "2.4°/4.3°" numbers the human reviewer's own Step 8E-C.1 complaint cited**

Decision: the shadow's aim-pitch source changed from `extractAimPitchFromWeaponQuaternion(gripPose.weaponWorldQuaternion, containerWorldQuat)` to `convertRangeLocalPitchToAimPitch(rangeLocalPose.pitch)` — reading `src/lib/v2/range/localPose.ts`'s already-published-every-frame `pitch` field (the same value `RangeController.tsx` sets `camera.rotation.x` from) directly, instead of extracting it back out of a weapon quaternion that has additional rotation baked into it.

Reason: while building this pass's own real-browser validation driver, a systematic pitch sweep showed the shadow's own `aim pitch: raw` debug readout topping out around 5° even when the camera was driven to a genuine, independently-confirmed ~89° (cross-checked against `bodyDebugReadout.ts`'s direct `camera.rotation.x` readout, which correctly reached `-PLAYER.PITCH_LIMIT`).
