Root-caused with a standalone, no-browser Node script (`.scratch/verify-aimpitch-math.mjs`) that replicates `VortexViewmodel.tsx`'s exact quaternion composition: `group.quaternion.copy(camera.quaternion)` THEN `group.rotateX/Y/Z(HIP_POSE.rotation)`, where `HIP_POSE.rotation.y` includes `MODEL_FORWARD_CORRECTION_Y` (≈90°, correcting the raw GLB's own forward axis). `extractAimPitchFromWeaponQuaternion`'s own math is independently correct — verified by feeding it a RAW camera quaternion with no compound rotation, where it round-trips 30°/60°/85°(clamped) exactly — the problem is specifically what `weaponWorldQuaternion` IS: reading pitch from a "-Z" direction on a frame that's been additionally yawed ~90° in its own local space by the model-forward correction does not linearly track how far the camera itself has pitched. Feeding the SAME compound quaternion through the function reproduced the exact 2.35°/4.33°/5.14° suppression at 30°/60°/88.9° input — the precise numbers the human reviewer's Step 8E-C.1 review complaint cited as evidence the chest wasn't following real look direction. That complaint was therefore a real, measurable defect in the pitch SOURCE this whole time, not a subjective visual read that could be argued with.

Rejected: tuning `chestAimFollowFraction` higher to "compensate" for the suppressed input (would still leave the RELATIONSHIP between camera pitch and chest lean nonlinear/distorted — a bigger fraction applied to a nonlinear, saturating signal doesn't make it linear). Rejected: fixing `extractAimPitchFromWeaponQuaternion` itself to somehow "undo" the compound rotation (would require re-deriving the exact HIP/ADS pose rotation constants inside a supposedly-generic pitch-extraction utility, coupling it to weapon-specific presentation values it has no business knowing about).

Chosen: read `rangeLocalPose.pitch` directly. This is NOT "a second, independently-computed camera pitch" (the thing Step 8E-C.1's own brief warned against) — `rangeLocalPose` is the exact same general-purpose, already-published-every-frame bridge family as `gripWorldPose.ts`/`actionPoseState.ts`, already read by other non-debug gameplay systems (`VortexFireSystem.tsx`'s animation-state resolution), just for a value neither of those two bridges happens to publish. `extractAimPitchFromWeaponQuaternion` itself is left in the codebase (its own math is correct and tested) with an updated, prominent doc-comment warning future callers away from ever pointing it at a weapon's own presentation-composed quaternion again. New `convertRangeLocalPitchToAimPitch` (tested, including an exact round-trip across a realistic pitch-limit sweep) handles the sign conversion explicitly rather than inline, given this exact code family has already shipped one real sign-inversion bug this milestone (Step 8E-C.1's chest lean).

---

**2026-07-29 — Two real Playwright/Chromium Pointer Lock automation bugs found while building this pass's validation driver, both distinct from (and compounding) the aim-pitch-source bug above**

Decision: document both findings here for any future validation script in this codebase that needs to drive camera pitch/yaw under Pointer Lock via Playwright.

Reason: (1) CDP's `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` accepts optional `movementX`/`movementY` override fields, documented as letting a caller specify the movement delta directly — under active Pointer Lock, Chromium was found to IGNORE these entirely for `mouseMoved` events; a real DOM `mousemove` listener attached in-page observed `[0,0]` for every dispatched event regardless of the requested movement, confirmed via a dedicated diagnostic script. (2) Separately, and independent of (1): rapid consecutive `page.mouse.move(x,y)` calls with no real wall-clock gap between them are measurably dropped/coalesced by Chromium under active Pointer Lock — a cumulative 531px delta across 9 back-to-back moves (no delay) registered in-game as only ~5° of camera pitch, while the SAME cumulative delta with an explicit ~45ms `sleep()` between each individual move registered the full, correct ~89°. A calibration test with real DOM-listener instrumentation confirmed sum-requested == sum-observed exactly once the per-step delay was added, across a spread of 10-200px step sizes.

Rejected: relying on CDP's documented `movementX`/`movementY` override (tried first, since it seemed like the intended tool for exactly this use case — found broken empirically, not assumed). Rejected: a single large one-shot `page.mouse.move()` call for a big pitch delta (tried, also measurably dropped most of the delta — the coalescing bug affects large single jumps too, not just tight loops).

Chosen: position-based `page.mouse.move(x,y)` with a persistently-tracked virtual cursor position (never reset to an absolute coordinate mid-sequence — this is also the fix for the earlier, separately-documented 2026-07-28 yaw-corruption bug), broken into steps of at most ~60px each, with an explicit ~45ms `sleep()` between every individual dispatched step. This makes a full 0-70° pitch sweep call take longer in wall-clock terms than the original (buggy) version, but produces correct, verified results — confirmed by cross-checking the resulting `rangeLocalPose.pitch`-derived aim value against `bodyDebugReadout.ts`'s independent direct camera-pitch readout at every step of a full sweep.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.2: a systematic camera-angle hunt found no marker-free view of the actual ground-projected shadow exists from the current first-person-only camera — a structural, not a tuning, limitation**

Decision: this pass's visual evidence relies on diagnostic-visible partial-body/arm/weapon captures (mesh rendered with a visible material, viewed directly through the FP camera) rather than a literal, complete, third-person-style silhouette of the shadow cast on the ground — the specific gap is flagged explicitly rather than silently omitted or worked around.

Reason: a systematic 10-combination pitch/yaw sweep (30-88° pitch, ±90°/±40°/±20°/0° yaw) with all debug markers and diagnostic material disabled found every tested angle shows only the shadow character's OWN nearby geometry (torso, shoulder, head) filling the frame at close range — never a distant, readable ground shadow. Root cause, computed directly from the range's light geometry: the directional light sits at roughly 57° elevation above horizontal (`atan(22 / sqrt(12² + 8²))`), and for a ~1.8m character this produces a shadow only about `1.8 / tan(57°) ≈ 1.2m` long — short enough that ANY camera angle able to frame it necessarily also has the character's own body (which the FP camera is co-located with, at head height) dominating the near field of view. This is not fixable by camera angle alone; a genuine external/third-person or free-camera viewpoint would be required, and this milestone's own hard constraints explicitly forbid changing the camera.

Rejected: silently omitting the "complete marker-free silhouette" requirement without comment (would look like an oversight rather than an identified, reasoned limitation). Rejected: building a minimal spectator/free-camera dev tool to satisfy the requirement (would itself violate the explicit "do not change camera" constraint this same brief states, and is a large enough addition to warrant its own explicitly-authorized pass rather than being smuggled in as a side effect of visual validation).

Chosen: document the finding plainly (this entry, and the human-review artifact's own notes), rely on diagnostic-visible framing for what visual evidence IS achievable this pass, and leave the "complete ground-shadow silhouette" requirement explicitly open pending either a human accepting the diagnostic-visible evidence as sufficient, or a future, separately-authorized pass adding dev-only spectator tooling.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3: the human reviewer explicitly authorized a dev-only external review camera, reversing the previous pass's "no camera changes" conclusion — built via R3F's manual-render mechanism so it never touches the real gameplay camera at all**

Decision: `KaelShadowReviewCamera.tsx` owns an entirely separate `THREE.PerspectiveCamera`, never assigned to `useThree().camera` (the one `RangeController.tsx` reads/writes every frame). It uses `useFrame(callback, 1)` — R3F's documented "manual render" trigger, where any priority>0 callback makes R3F stop calling its own default `gl.render()` for that Canvas and defer to the priority>0 callback instead.

Reason: the previous pass's own finding (Step 8E-C.2 entry above) was that no marker-free view of the shadow exists from the first-person camera, and that building a spectator camera to fix this would violate the "do not change camera" constraint. The human reviewer's Step 8E-C.3 brief explicitly lifted that restriction for an ISOLATED validation camera that never ships: "The earlier 'do not change camera' restriction protects the gameplay camera; it should not prevent an isolated validation camera that never ships." The engineering question this decision answers is HOW to add that camera without risking the real one.

Rejected: swapping `state.camera` to the review camera via drei's `<PerspectiveCamera makeDefault>` pattern (would make `RangeController.tsx`'s own `camera` reference (via `useThree((state) => state.camera)`) silently start pointing at the review camera too, since that hook is reactive — meaning the SAME mouse-look/movement code would begin writing pitch/yaw onto the review camera instead of the real one, exactly the kind of gameplay-camera risk the constraint was protecting against). Rejected: modifying `RangeController.tsx` itself to conditionally branch on review mode (would touch the one file this whole design goal was to leave untouched).

Chosen: the manual-render approach — `RangeController.tsx` keeps updating the REAL camera from real input every frame, completely unaware the review camera exists; the review camera only decides which one actually gets drawn to the screen this frame. Verified live: canvas keeps rendering correctly the instant review mode is disabled (component unmounts, priority>0 callback disappears, R3F auto-reverts) — no explicit restore step was written, and none was needed.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3: a real forward-direction sign bug — the chest-anchored weapon was positioned BEHIND the character, not in front, and Step 8E-C.2's own reach numbers structurally could not have caught it**

Decision: `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT` (`shadowWeaponPresentationPose.ts`) changed from a +90° to a -90° rotation around the shared +Y axis, and every `positionLocal` Z value in `SHADOW_WEAPON_PRESENTATION` had its sign negated.

Reason: building this pass's external review camera, calibration screenshots consistently showed the character's hands reaching toward empty space with no weapon visible. A direct diagnostic (a temporary bounding-box + world-position console log) found the weapon mesh's world Z sat CONSISTENTLY GREATER than the character root's own Z — i.e., on the spawn-wall side, not the range-interior side the player actually faces and walks into (confirmed via `RANGE_SPAWN=[0,3,10]` facing into the range toward the far wall at Z=-59, the established "forward = -Z" convention this whole codebase already relies on). The original `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT`/position-offset Z convention was based on an ASSUMPTION — that the chest BONE's own local -Z axis matches the shadow container's verified world -Z forward — which was only ever empirically checked for the PITCH axis (`applyChestAimFrame`'s rotation-around-"right" math works correctly regardless of what "forward" means, since tipping something up/down doesn't depend on which way it's facing). It was never checked for the chest bone's actual YAW-plane forward direction, because Step 8E-C.2's own validation only ever measured hand-to-grip REACH (distance) — which an IK solver satisfies identically regardless of which direction the target sits from the shoulder. A target 30cm behind the chest is exactly as "reachable" to a two-bone solver as one 30cm in front; the 0.00cm error numbers were real and correct, they just never could have revealed this specific defect.

Rejected: re-deriving the "correct" sign by pure trigonometric reasoning about the chest bone's rest-pose rotation chain (this exact category of manual sign-reasoning has already produced two real bugs this milestone — the Step 8E-C.1 chest-lean inversion and this one — both only caught by direct empirical measurement, not by re-checking the math more carefully on paper).

Chosen: negate the sign, verify EMPIRICALLY (not just re-derive) via the same bounding-box diagnostic — confirmed the weapon now sits on the correct, range-interior side of the character root — then re-run the full reach matrix to confirm the sign flip didn't regress anything (it didn't: 0.00cm error/assist across all 15 states, same as before, since IK reach is direction-agnostic — only the SEMANTIC correctness changed, not the numeric proof). `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT`'s own doc comment now records this trace so a future reader doesn't have to rediscover it.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3: the shadow weapon's own "diagnostic visible" material renders black — fixed with a dedicated material rather than reusing the body's own "restore original material" technique**

Decision: `KaelFirstPersonShadowWeapon.tsx`'s diagnostic toggle applies a purpose-built `WEAPON_DIAGNOSTIC_MATERIAL` (light gray, `MeshStandardMaterial`) instead of calling `applyOperatorRenderMode(instance, 'full')` (which restores the mesh's own authentic, originally-loaded material — exactly what the shadow BODY's own diagnostic toggle does successfully).

Reason: after fixing the forward-direction bug above, the weapon was STILL invisible in diagnostic-visible calibration screenshots. A direct test — forcing every weapon mesh to a bright, impossible-to-miss magenta `MeshBasicMaterial` — proved the geometry, position, and mounting were all completely correct (a small but clearly visible magenta sliver appeared exactly at the grip). The weapon's OWN authentic material, once restored via `'full'` mode, simply renders too dark to distinguish from this scene's background lighting at calibration viewing distance — the same underlying reason the BODY's own "full" mode reads as a flat pale mannequin rather than its true dark-clothed appearance (Step 8E-C's art bible describes "dark charcoal gloves... titanium/gold accents," not a uniform pale white), just more severe for the weapon's own darker material.

Rejected: keeping the magenta test material permanently (a jarring, obviously-fake color, not appropriate for calibration screenshots meant to read as a coherent figure). Rejected: trying to fix the underlying texture/lighting issue in the real material (out of scope — this dev-only diagnostic toggle's whole purpose is visibility for calibration, not authentic material preview, and the body's own toggle already sets the precedent that diagnostic mode need not show the "real" look).

Chosen: a dedicated, purpose-built diagnostic material for the weapon (light gray, distinct from the body's own near-white, so the two read as separate but clearly-associated parts of one figure) — established via `applyOperatorRenderMode(instance, 'shadowOnly')` first (reusing that call's existing visible/castShadow-setup logic) with the diagnostic material layered on top only when diagnostic mode is active, rather than duplicating the visible/castShadow logic by hand.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3: the review camera's initial framing showed only legs — the Rapier capsule's CENTER, not the character's feet/ground level, was being used as the camera's height reference**

Decision: `KaelShadowReviewCamera.tsx` applies `SHADOW_BODY_PHYSICAL_LOCAL_OFFSET`'s own Y correction (the same constant `KaelFirstPersonShadowBody.tsx` uses to position its own root) to `firstPersonBodyPose.ts`'s published `worldPosition` before passing it to the camera-preset math, rather than using that raw value directly.

Reason: the first working screenshot from the new review camera showed only the character's legs filling most of the frame, with nothing above chest height. `firstPersonBodyPose.ts`'s `worldPosition` is the Rapier `CapsuleCollider`'s own published world position — its CENTER, not the character's feet (the same reconciliation `shadowBodyTransform.ts`'s own doc comment documents at length for the shadow body's own root positioning). The camera-preset math (`shadowReviewCameraPresets.ts`) was written assuming a ground-level reference point; feeding it the raw capsule-center value effectively aimed the camera roughly a meter too high — at a point above the character's actual head, cropping the frame to show mostly the lower body.

Rejected: hand-tuning the preset heights to compensate empirically without fixing the root cause (would leave the SAME latent bug for any future preset addition, and conflates a units/reference-frame bug with an aesthetic framing choice). Rejected: duplicating the offset math as a new, separately-tracked constant (would create a second value that could drift from `shadowBodyTransform.ts`'s own, if that offset is ever revisited).

Chosen: reuse `SHADOW_BODY_PHYSICAL_LOCAL_OFFSET` directly at the point the review camera reads the player's position — verified visually (a subsequent screenshot showed the full body, head to boots, correctly framed) before also widening the preset radii/heights slightly for a more comfortable margin.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3: a repeated-navigation non-regression check surfaced 4 console messages never seen in any single-session validation run — recorded as a probable test-harness artifact, not silently dropped**

Decision: report this finding here rather than omitting it, while not treating it as a code regression without further evidence.

Reason: a dedicated non-regression script that repeatedly `page.goto()`-navigates between `/v2/range` and `/v2/play` within one browser session (checking for shadow-panel absence, clone-count stability across a remount, etc.) logged 4 console messages: one Next.js dev-server Fast-Refresh RSC-fetch warning (a known dev-only HMR artifact of navigating while the Fast Refresh WebSocket is active — does not exist in production, where Fast Refresh is absent) and three `THREE.GLTFLoader: Couldn't load texture` errors referencing blob URLs. Every OTHER validation script this pass and every prior pass — all of which drive real gameplay input within a SINGLE page load, never repeatedly navigating across routes — reported zero console errors, including runs executed both before and after this navigation test in the same session.

Rejected: silently omitting the finding (would look like an oversight, and a future session hunting for a "phantom" texture-loading bug would have no record that this was already investigated). Rejected: treating it as a confirmed regression and attempting a fix without being able to reproduce it outside the repeated-navigation pattern (risks fixing a test-harness artifact instead of a real defect, or masking a genuine one under an incorrect diagnosis).

Chosen: document the finding, its narrow reproduction conditions (only appears under repeated cross-route navigation in dev mode, never in normal single-session play), and the circumstantial case for it being a blob-URL-revocation-vs-in-flight-texture-load race inherent to aggressive dev-mode route switching — flagged for a future session to investigate further only if it's ever observed during actual normal play, which it has not been.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3.1: `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT` nearly got re-flipped to +90° on a direction-blind visual check, caught before shipping by a direction-sensitive one — recorded in full because the failure mode generalizes**

Decision: keep `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT` at -90° (the Step 8E-C.3 value, unchanged) and record the near-miss explicitly, not just the final answer.

Reason: this pass's close review cameras showed the rifle poorly readable from several angles even after a legitimate diagnostic-material contrast fix, and that readability problem was (wrongly) diagnosed as an orientation bug. The first check used to pick between +90°/-90° was a side-view visibility comparison — with the review camera positioned perpendicular to the character's fore-aft axis, the weapon read as a long, clear bar at +90° and was nearly invisible at -90°, which looked like decisive evidence for +90°. It is not: a camera perpendicular to an axis sees an object's full length regardless of which way along that axis the object points — this check can tell "on-axis" from "off-axis" but cannot tell "forward" from "180° backward." A SEPARATE check — a world-space dot product of the muzzle direction against the established spawn-forward axis (world -Z, per `RANGE_SPAWN`/`RangeController.tsx`) — actually did favor -90° (mapped the muzzle to ≈world -Z) and was set aside as contradicting the "clearer" visual evidence, which was backwards: the dot-product check was the correct one, the visual comparison was not. Before shipping +90°, a third, genuinely direction-sensitive check settled it: the muzzle TIP's world position (weapon pivot + `muzzleLocal` rotated by the candidate quaternion, `muzzleLocal.x=+0.47` from `VORTEX_RUNTIME_ANCHORS`) compared against the pivot's and the chest's own world Z. A correctly forward-pointing rifle must satisfy chestZ > pivotZ > muzzleTipZ (monotonically further toward -Z/forward). Measured live at the player's spawn transform: at -90°, 10.070 > 9.787 > 9.589 (muzzle tip sits ~0.2m further forward than the grip pivot — correct). At +90°, 10.070 > 9.787 but muzzleTipZ=9.982 sits BETWEEN the chest and the pivot — the "muzzle" end pointed backward, into the character's own chest, not away from it. This directly corroborates the earlier dot-product check and directly contradicts the side-view visibility read.

Rejected: shipping +90° (the near-miss) — would have reintroduced a backward-pointing weapon, this time passing casual review because it happened to look MORE visible from certain angles (a backward-pointing rifle sticking out away from the body reads just as "present" in silhouette as a correctly forward one; direction is invisible to a pure-visibility check). Rejected: silently reverting to -90° without recording why the wrong value was ever seriously considered — a future pass hitting the same readability complaint could easily rediscover the same flawed "make it more visible from the side" heuristic and ship the actual regression this time.

Chosen: -90° confirmed and left unchanged; the doc comment on `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT` (`shadowWeaponPresentationPose.ts`) now records the full four-step sequence (misdiagnosis → flawed visual check → correct-but-dismissed dot-product check → decisive muzzle-tip check) so the direction-blind-vs-direction-sensitive distinction is available to whoever next touches this constant. The REAL, legitimate readability fix from this pass — darkening the weapon's diagnostic material for contrast against the body's own pale diagnostic material — was kept. The residual finding that weapon readability is still angle-dependent (clear from oblique/elevated cameras, occluded by the character's own forearms from a pure side profile, faint during the deliberately-tucked-in sprint carry) is disclosed in the published review artifact and `changelog.md` rather than further chased this pass — it is a real characteristic of external third-person viewing of a body-anchored weapon, not something either the orientation or the material fix could address, and pursuing it further would mean tuning `positionLocal` (pushing the weapon further from the body), which risks drifting into the pose/architecture redesign this pass's brief explicitly excluded.

---

**2026-07-29 — Step 8E-C.3.1: found (not fixed) that several shadow review camera presets show the character's back when their name says "front," and vice versa**

Decision: document the finding in `docs/known-bugs.md` rather than fixing it this pass.

Reason: while cross-referencing the new close camera presets against the character's own visible facing (hands/face direction in captured screenshots) to build the weapon-readability evidence set, `bodyCloseThreeQuarter`/`threeQuarterFront`/`handsCloseRight` (positive `yawOffsetDeg`) consistently showed the character's BACK, and `threeQuarterRear`/`bodyCloseSide`/`handsCloseLeft` (negative offset or the rear preset) consistently showed the FRONT — backwards from what the names claim. This has presumably been true since these presets were introduced in Step 8E-C.3, but was never independently checked against the character's own facing (the prior pass's own screenshots were dominated by the lower-body contamination this pass fixed, which likely made it harder to notice).

Rejected: fixing it inline as a quick yaw-sign flip — the root cause (why the preset math's assumed "forward" doesn't match the character rig's own facing) is not yet diagnosed, and this milestone has a documented pattern of exactly this category of bug (chest-bone forward-axis assumptions, weapon muzzle-axis assumptions) turning out to need real empirical verification rather than a guessed sign flip. Fixing the SYMPTOM (swap which labels point which way) without understanding the CAUSE risks masking a convention mismatch that affects other code reading the same yaw math.

Chosen: documented as a known, low-risk (dev-only diagnostic label) issue in `docs/known-bugs.md`, out of this pass's explicit scope (review-harness isolation and calibration, not further camera-convention changes). This pass's own curated evidence artifact uses whichever preset actually shows the needed angle, captioned honestly, rather than trusting the button label.

---

**2026-07-30 — Step 8E-C.3.2: root-caused and fixed the camera-preset front/rear naming bug flagged (not fixed) in Step 8E-C.3.1 — and, in the process of fixing it correctly, found that the obvious one-line fix would have silently broken left/right**

Decision: negate only the Z (front/back) component of the yaw-relative camera offset, not the full offset vector.

Reason: `computeShadowReviewCameraTransform`'s yaw-relative branch computed `dir = new THREE.Vector3(0, 0, 1).applyAxisAngle(UP, playerYawRad + offsetDeg)`. At `offsetDeg=0`, this base vector points toward world +Z. But the player/character actually faces world -Z at yaw=0 — confirmed two independent ways: `RangeController.tsx` sets `camera.rotation.y = yaw.current` directly (three.js's standard "camera looks down -Z at rotation 0" convention), and Step 8E-C.3.1's own weapon-orientation investigation independently confirmed the same forward axis via a live muzzle-tip-vs-chest world-position measurement. So every preset's `yawOffsetDeg` was being measured from BEHIND the player instead of in front of them, exactly matching the found symptom (`threeQuarterFront` showing the back, `threeQuarterRear` showing the front).

The naive fix — negate the whole `dir` vector, or equivalently add 180° to every effective angle — was checked BEFORE implementing, not after: this is a full point-reflection through the player's position, which flips BOTH the front/back axis AND the left/right axis simultaneously (think of a clock face rotated 180°: 12↔6 AND 3↔9 both swap). Whether that second flip was safe depended on whether left/right was ALREADY correct in the shipped code, which had never been independently checked (the original bug report was specifically about front/back). Measured directly rather than assumed: the debug panel's real R/L shoulder world-position readout at yaw=0 gives `R pre-assist shoulder` x=+0.082, `L pre-assist shoulder` x=-0.271 — confirming character-right = world +X. Checking the EXISTING (pre-fix) code's `rightSide` preset (`yawOffsetDeg=+90`) against this: it already resolved to a camera position on world +X — i.e., already correctly on the character's real right side. A full vector negation would have moved it to -X, silently swapping left and right for the first time, immediately after "fixing" front and back.

Rejected: negating the full `dir` vector (the obvious, symmetric-looking fix) — would have fixed the reported symptom while introducing a new, unreported one, in a dev-only diagnostic tool whose entire purpose is showing reviewers the correct side of the character; a left/right regression here is exactly as dangerous as the front/back bug it would have replaced. Rejected: fixing only by renaming labels — the underlying math would still be wrong for whichever future preset gets added, reintroducing the same class of bug (this milestone's own repeated history: chest-bone forward-axis, weapon muzzle-axis, and now camera-preset forward-axis, all the same "assumed convention never independently checked" pattern).

Chosen: `dir.z = -dir.z` after the existing rotation math — negates only the front/back component, leaves left/right untouched. Verified three ways before considering it done: (1) geometry — solved for the exact correction algebraically before writing code, not by trial-and-error; (2) 4 new unit tests pin the corrected absolute geometry down numerically per axis (front=-Z, rear=+Z, right=+X, left=-X), added to `shadowReviewCameraPresets.test.ts` alongside the 14 pre-existing tests, all of which still pass unmodified (they're relational — constant radius, opposite sides, above-player — not tied to the specific absolute convention that changed); (3) live-browser capture of all four cardinal presets, confirming visually that each now shows the side its label claims. `docs/known-bugs.md`'s entry for this bug is resolved and removed. This is the smallest change that fixes the actual, verified defect — no other camera, pose, or architecture code touched.

---

**2026-07-31 — Step 8E-D: bias, normalBias, and shadow-map resolution all tested empirically and found to make NO visible difference — shipped as an honest negative result, not papered over with a plausible-sounding "calibrated" number**

Decision: keep `bias=0`, `normalBias=0`, `mapSize=1024` — identical to the pre-8E-D production defaults — as the canonical Step 8E-D calibration, rather than shipping a non-zero value chosen for how "reasonable" it looked rather than for any demonstrated visual effect.

Reason: this step's brief called for tuning shadow-bias/normalBias against the review harness and documenting the final values and why they were selected. The natural failure mode here is exactly the one Step 8E-C.3.1 already found and reverted once this same milestone (the `WEAPON_CHEST_ALIGN_QUAT` near-miss): picking a plausible-looking number, observing that nothing looks obviously broken, and shipping it as "calibrated" without ever confirming the number is actually doing anything. To avoid repeating that mistake, three candidates were tested against the SAME angles/poses with the SAME receiver: bias=0 (production default), bias=-0.0008/normalBias=0.012 (a conservative first candidate), and bias=-0.008 (an extreme, 10x candidate, specifically chosen to be large enough that a real bias-related artifact — either fixed acne or introduced peter-panning — should become visible if the test methodology can detect ANYTHING in this range at all). All three were visually indistinguishable at every camera distance tested (`shadowClose`, `shadowWide`, close body-framing presets). The test methodology itself was independently confirmed sound in the same session: switching `receiverMode` between `production` and `readable` DOES produce an obvious, easily-screenshotted difference, so the "no difference" result for bias is not an artifact of an insensitive test — it's a genuine finding that bias/normalBias, across a 10x range, do not visibly affect this specific scene's shadow quality at the resolutions and distances the review harness currently uses. The same reasoning applies to shadow-map resolution: 1024² and 2048² produced visually indistinguishable results, and inspecting `rangeEnvironmentBounds.ts`'s `RANGE_SHADOW_CAMERA_BOUNDS` explains why — the shadow camera's orthographic frustum is sized to cover the ENTIRE range floor (tens of meters), so even at 2048² each shadow-map texel still covers a large world-space area relative to the character's own scale; doubling texel count doesn't meaningfully shrink the per-texel world footprint when the frustum itself is the bottleneck.

Rejected: shipping the -0.0008/0.012 candidate anyway, on the reasoning that "small conservative values can't hurt and might help in cases not covered by this pass's specific test angles." This is precisely the unverified-but-plausible reasoning this project's own history warns against — a value that LOOKS like careful tuning but is actually just an unverified guess is worse than an honestly-reported default, because it invites a future session to trust it without re-checking. Rejected: raising resolution to 2048² "to be safe" despite no measured visual benefit — this has a real, non-zero GPU memory/bandwidth cost (unmeasured precisely due to the headless test environment's software-rendering limitation, but non-zero on real hardware) that this pass cannot justify without evidence of a corresponding benefit.

Chosen: canonical values stay at the production defaults for bias/normalBias/resolution; the REAL, demonstrated finding from this pass is the receiver-mode work (production vs. readable, a measured, visible difference) and the architectural observation about the shadow frustum being the actual resolution bottleneck — both are real contributions even though the bias/resolution tuning task itself concluded "no change justified." Self-shadowing (`receiveShadow` on the prototype body/weapon) was tested the same evidence-gated way and reached the same kind of honest negative result — no visible weapon-on-arm or arm-on-torso shadow at the tested close-range angles — and was left disabled for the identical reason: no evidence, no ship. If a future pass wants to pursue shadow quality further, the frustum-sizing finding is the actionable lead, not another bias sweep.

---

**2026-07-31 — Step 8E-D.1A/8E-D.1: player-centered shadow frustum sized from real deformed-mesh measurement (3.5m×6m rectangle), tracked via continuous light-space texel snapping with no dead zone, ground-anchored at a fixed Y**

Decision: replace the full-floor (130m×130m) static shadow frustum, while in player-centered mode, with a tight 3.5m-wide × 6.0m-tall rectangle (near 20/far 27), continuously re-centered on the player's own ground position and snapped to the shadow map's per-axis texel grid every frame — no dead zone, no smoothing/interpolation of the snap itself.

Reason: Step 8E-D's own finding was that `RANGE_SHADOW_CAMERA_BOUNDS`' floor-spanning frustum makes texel density (world-space size per shadow-map pixel) the actual quality bottleneck, not bias or resolution. Fixing that requires shrinking the frustum to the player's own scale — but by how much, and what shape, needed real measurement rather than a guess. The 8E-D.1A measurement pass sampled REAL deformed mesh (via `SkinnedMesh.applyBoneTransform`, not rest-pose bounds) across 13 action states and found a worst-case light-space footprint of 0.759m width (sprint) / 1.356m height (inspect_hold) / 3.755m combined depth span (landing, driven by the receiver's fixed height against the player's airborne Y, not the body's own pose). A square frustum sized to the larger of the two axes (8m/10m/12m candidates were all measured safe) wastes 3-5m of margin on the narrower axis for no benefit — a rectangle matched to each axis's own real requirement gets more effective texel density per shadow-map pixel for the same 1024² resolution. The final 3.5m×6m choice carries roughly 1.0m/1.9m of margin over the measured worst cases, accounting for one frame of dash-speed movement (18 m/s, the fastest real per-frame displacement in this codebase) plus a 15% buffer for untested pose combinations. A small (1-2 texel) dead zone was considered and rejected: at this frustum's own scale, one texel is 3-6mm — a dead zone that size provides no perceptible stabilization benefit while adding real clipping risk on an already-tight rectangle, so continuous snapped tracking (the standard technique, not a novel invention) was kept instead. The ground anchor's fixed Y (0, the canonical light target's own pre-8E-D.1 Y) was chosen over tracking the player's live capsule Y specifically so a jump/fall/landing arc translates the frustum center only in the horizontal (light-space X/Y) plane, never drags the whole light rig vertically — confirmed live across a full jump arc (ground-anchor Y and light-world Y both held exactly constant while the player was airborne).

Rejected: a square frustum (simpler, one texel-size scalar) — measured to waste 3-5m of margin per side versus the tighter rectangle, for no offsetting benefit. Rejected: a 1-2 texel dead zone before snapping — negligible benefit (sub-centimeter, below normal player-visible thresholds) against real clipping-margin cost on a frustum with under 1m of slack on its narrow axis. Rejected: tracking the player's live (airborne) Y as the ground anchor — would have made every jump visibly translate the entire light rig, a much larger and more distracting artifact than the texel-density problem this pass exists to fix.

Chosen: `PLAYER_CENTERED_SHADOW_FRUSTUM_CONFIG` (`playerCenteredShadowFrustum.ts`) — width 3.5m, height 6.0m, map 1024², near 20/far 27 — tracked via `snapToTexelGrid()`'s continuous, no-dead-zone, independent-per-axis snapping, applied to a ground anchor fixed at Y=0. `STATIC_FULL_FLOOR_SHADOW_FRUSTUM_CONFIG` (restating `RANGE_SHADOW_CAMERA_BOUNDS`) remains selectable for direct A/B comparison and is what every real player (`shadowReviewEnabled` false) always sees, unchanged.

---

**2026-07-31 — Step 8E-D.1: the fixed light-space reference frame must be built from a `THREE.Camera`, not a plain `Object3D` — a real bug caught by its own round-trip unit test before it shipped**

Decision: `buildFixedLightSpaceBasis()` (`playerCenteredShadowFrustumBasis.ts`) constructs its detached, never-scene-parented reference frame using `new THREE.Camera()`, not `new THREE.Object3D()`.

Reason: `THREE.Object3D.prototype.lookAt()` special-cases `this.isCamera || this.isLight`: a camera or light object orients using the standard -Z-forward convention (`Matrix4.lookAt(eye, target, up)`), while a plain `Object3D` gets the OPPOSITE, swapped-argument convention (`Matrix4.lookAt(target, eye, up)` — designed for e.g. an arrow mesh whose +Z should point AT a target, not away from it). This is a real, easy-to-miss three.js API behavior, not a typo-class bug. THREE's actual `DirectionalLightShadow.camera` is a genuine `THREE.OrthographicCamera` (`isCamera === true`), so it always takes the first branch. A reference frame built from a plain `Object3D` would silently take the SECOND branch, producing a basis with the OPPOSITE rotation from the real, live shadow camera — every "light-space" coordinate this pass computes would then be measured in a mirrored frame, and texel snapping computed in that mirrored frame would not align with the real shadow map's actual texel grid at all, defeating the entire purpose of this pass while still *appearing* to work (the tracking math is self-consistent under a wrong-but-fixed basis, so nothing would visibly break in a quick look — only a genuinely independent geometric check catches it). This was caught, not guessed at: a unit test asserted that the canonical target position `(0,0,0)`, projected into the basis built from light position `(12,22,8)`, should land at light-space `(0, 0, -26.31)` (26.31 = the real distance from light to target, negative because of the -Z-forward convention) — the test failed with `z=+26.31` (the mirrored result) under the original `Object3D`-based implementation, immediately identifying the exact defect before any real-browser validation was run.

Rejected: catching this only via visual/real-browser inspection — a mirrored-but-self-consistent basis produces plausible-LOOKING tracking (the frustum still follows the player, still translates smoothly) with no obvious visual symptom; only a basis constructed and checked against ground-truth geometry (not "does it look like it's working") would reliably catch this class of bug, which is exactly why this was caught by a unit test asserting exact numeric geometry rather than by screenshot review.

Chosen: `buildFixedLightSpaceBasis()` uses `THREE.Camera` and is covered by 9 unit tests (`playerCenteredShadowFrustumBasis.test.ts`) including the round-trip proof above, a proof that translation purely along the light's forward axis changes only light-space Z (never X/Y — the same fact 8E-D.1A's own measurement pass relied on), and a non-canonical (non-hardcoded) basis check.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8E-E: production shadow-caster ownership implemented as one typed, source-controlled policy read by a single pure decision function, not scattered inline booleans**

Decision: `shadowCasterPolicy.ts`'s `RANGE_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY` (`'fp-arms' | 'full-body'`) is the sole switch; `resolveRangeShadowCasterDecision()` is the one place that turns `(shadowDebugEnabled, shadowReviewEnabled, policy)` into the three booleans `RangeScene.tsx` actually mounts from.

Reason: the Step 8E-E audit's own explicit preference was "minimal branches in existing components, one typed caster-ownership policy module" — writing the exclusivity logic three separate times (once for the FP-arms `castShadow` gate, once for the full-body mount condition, once for the controller's mount condition) would have created three independent places that could drift out of sync under a future edit, exactly the kind of bug class this milestone's own predecessor steps (8E-C's weapon-forward-axis bug, 8E-C.3.2's camera-preset bug) kept finding. A pure function is also the only way to get REAL unit-test coverage of the exclusivity invariant in a codebase with no React/R3F rendering test harness (established since Step 8E-C.3's own scoping note) — `resolveRangeShadowCasterDecision()` is exhaustively tested across all 8 input combinations, proving `fpArmsCastShadow` and `fullBodyCasterActive` are never both true and never both false, which a component-level integration test could not have proven at all in this codebase.

Rejected: inlining `shadowDebugEnabled || policy === 'full-body'`-style expressions directly at each of the three JSX/effect call sites — works today, but has no single point of truth and no way to unit-test the CROSS-cutting invariant (that the three sites' conditions actually compose into "exactly one active caster") short of reading all three sites side by side on every future change.

Chosen: one pure decision function, called once per `RangeScene.tsx` render, destructured into the three consumers.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8E-E: `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` gained an optional `casterPolicy` prop rather than reading the policy module directly, to keep the production switch provably scoped to `/v2/range`**

Decision: `KaelFirstPersonArms` accepts `casterPolicy?: RangeShadowCasterPolicy = 'fp-arms'` as a prop; it does not import or read `shadowCasterPolicy.ts` internally.

Reason: this component is SHARED between `/v2/range` (`RangeScene.tsx`) and `/v2/play` (`V2PlayScene.tsx`) — the exact same component instance type, mounted in two different scene trees. If the component read the policy module's exported constant directly (module-level, evaluated identically regardless of which scene imported it), the production caster switch would silently apply to `/v2/play` too, violating this milestone's own explicit "route-scoped to `/v2/range`" requirement — `/v2/play` has death/respawn/pause/restart lifecycle states the full-body shadow system has never been validated against (Step 8E-E's own audit, Section 5/6). Making the prop OPTIONAL with a default equal to the historical unconditional behavior means `V2PlayScene.tsx`, which never passes this prop at all, is provably unaffected — not by a runtime route check (which could be gotten wrong or bypassed), but by the simple absence of a prop value anywhere in its render tree. A new source-text regression test (`shadowCasterPolicy.test.ts`) asserts neither `V2PlayScene.tsx`, the `/v2/play` `PlayerController.tsx`, nor V1's `PlayerController.tsx` reference `shadowCasterPolicy` at all, so a future accidental import would fail a test immediately rather than silently widening scope.

Rejected: reading `EFFECTIVE_RANGE_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY` directly inside `KaelFirstPersonArms.tsx` — simpler at the call site, but ties a shared, route-agnostic component to a route-specific policy, and would have required an explicit `if (route === '/v2/range')` runtime check to un-apply it in `/v2/play` — strictly worse than never being able to apply it there in the first place.

Chosen: an optional prop, default-safe, threaded through the existing `castShadow` `useEffect`'s dependency array — no other change to the component's own logic.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8E-E: the player-centered controller must ignore `shadowReviewStore`'s `frustumMode` entirely when activated by the production policy, not just default it to player-centered**

Decision: `KaelPlayerCenteredShadowController` gained an `allowDevModeOverride` prop. When `true` (the dev review harness is active), it reads `shadowReviewStore.getState().frustumMode` every frame, exactly as Step 8E-D.1 always did. When `false` (production `'full-body'` policy, no query flags), the tracking mode is hardcoded to `'player-centered'`, and the store is never consulted at all.

Reason: found during implementation, before any browser testing — `shadowReviewStore` is a Zustand module singleton that, by Step 8E-D's own established convention, deliberately PERSISTS across route navigation within one browser session (so a dev reviewer's calibration settings survive a route leave/re-entry during one work session). If a developer opened the review harness, switched to `'static-full-floor'` for an A/B comparison, then navigated to a plain `/v2/range` URL in the SAME browser tab, the store's `frustumMode` field would still read `'static-full-floor'` — and since the production controller (now active without any flags, per this milestone's own requirement) was, before this fix, unconditionally reading that same store field, a real player's session could have silently rendered the STATIC full-floor frustum instead of the intended player-centered one, with no flag or code change that caused it — a classic dev-tooling-state-leaking-into-production bug. This was reasoned out from the store's own documented persistence behavior, not observed as a live symptom — exactly the kind of thing a careful pre-implementation read of the existing code should catch before it ships, rather than after a bug report.

Rejected: calling `resetCalibration()` automatically whenever the review flags turn off — fragile (depends on catching every possible flag-off transition, including a raw URL navigation with no in-app "disable" action to hook), and still leaves a window where a mid-session production activation (if that were ever possible without a route change) would read a stale value. Rejected: leaving the risk undocumented on the theory that "a real developer would remember to reset" — this milestone's own instruction explicitly warned "review-panel changes must not leak into normal production range sessions," and an assumption about developer memory is not a guarantee.

Chosen: `allowDevModeOverride` — the production path never touches the dev-session store at all, so there is no state for a stale session to leak through.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8F: `shadowCasterPolicy.ts` generalized to a route-agnostic resolver rather than writing a second, near-duplicate policy module for `/v2/play`**

Decision: `resolveRangeShadowCasterDecision()` became `resolveShadowCasterDecision()` — the exact same exclusivity math, with no route name anywhere inside it — and a second policy constant (`PLAY_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY`) was added alongside `RANGE_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY`, both consumed through the one shared function.

Reason: Step 8F's own audit and this milestone's brief both explicitly called for "one route-agnostic pure resolver," and the alternative — copy `shadowCasterPolicy.ts` into a `play`-scoped module with `PLAY_`-prefixed names — would have reproduced exactly the "same logic in two places, provably able to drift apart" problem Step 8E-E's own original design (one pure function instead of three inline booleans) existed to avoid, just one level up. The exclusivity invariant (never both casters active, never both inactive) is identical regardless of which route is asking; only the INPUTS (which policy, which debug flags) differ per route, and those were already parameters, not hardcoded, in the pre-8F version.

Rejected: a second `playShadowCasterPolicy.ts` module duplicating `resolveRangeShadowCasterDecision()`'s body under a new name — the two copies would pass their own respective test suites independently, but nothing would catch them drifting apart if a future edit fixed a bug in one copy and not the other (this is precisely the failure mode Step 8E-E's own decision entry above warned against for inline booleans, and duplicating a whole module is the same problem at a larger scale). Rejected: keeping `resolveRangeShadowCasterDecision()`'s exact old parameter names (`shadowDebugEnabled`, `shadowReviewEnabled`) and just calling it from play with those same range-flavored names — technically works (the function never inspected the names, only the values), but reads as though play has a `shadowReviewEnabled` concept it does not; renamed to `debugFullBodyRequested`/`debugControllerRequested` to describe what each flag actually does, not which route historically needed it.

Chosen: one resolver, two independent policy constants (`RANGE_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY`, `PLAY_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY`), each route's own scene file supplying its own debug-flag values (play's are always `false`, since this milestone deliberately ships no play review UI — see the next entry). A route-independence test asserts identical inputs from either route's policy produce identical decisions, i.e. the resolver itself cannot be told which route is calling it.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8F: no shadow-review/debug UI shipped for `/v2/play` this milestone — the player-centered controller's `allowDevModeOverride` is unconditionally `false` on that route**

Decision: `/v2/play` never mounts anything resembling `KaelShadowReviewPanel`/`useShadowReviewEnabled`/`shadowReviewStore` — the full-body caster's activation there is a pure function of the compile-time `PLAY_SHADOW_CASTER_POLICY` constant alone, with no query-flag path at all.

Reason: the Step 8F brief was explicit that a play review UI was out of scope for this pass, and range's own review harness (camera presets, calibration sliders, frustum helper, receiver toggle) represents real, non-trivial UI surface built up over Steps 8E-C through 8E-D.1 specifically for range's own tuning needs — reusing it as-is for play would mean either a second copy of that whole harness (rejected for the same duplication reason as the policy resolver above) or awkwardly parameterizing every one of its camera presets/receiver assumptions for a completely different arena, neither of which this milestone's brief asked for or budgeted time against. Since play's approved frustum configuration was already fully measured and finalized by Step 8F.0 before any implementation began, there was no open tuning question a review UI would need to answer live.

Rejected: building a play-specific review harness "for completeness" alongside range's — unbudgeted scope, and the exact configuration values it would exist to tune were already locked in by Step 8F.0's measurement pass, leaving no real tuning task to perform with it. Rejected: reusing `shadowReviewStore` directly on `/v2/play` (e.g. gating on the same `?shadow=1&shadowReview=1` query flags) — that store's `frustumMode`/calibration fields are a module singleton shared across the whole browser tab; letting `/v2/play` read it would reopen exactly the cross-session-leakage risk the `allowDevModeOverride` prop (see the Step 8E-E entry above) was built to prevent, for a UI surface play doesn't even expose a way to change.

Chosen: `V2PlayScene.tsx` always passes `debugFullBodyRequested: false`, `debugControllerRequested: false` to the shared resolver and `allowDevModeOverride={false}` to the shared controller — the decision reduces to a pure function of the policy constant, and the controller can never read `shadowReviewStore` on this route at all.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8F: `/v2/play`'s player-centered frustum is a genuinely different size from `/v2/range`'s (3.5×12 vs 3.5×6, near 24/far 44 vs near 20/far 27) — not a copy-paste inconsistency**

Decision: `PLAY_PLAYER_CENTERED_SHADOW_CONFIG` (`playShadowFrustumConfig.ts`) uses its own Step 8F.0-measured values, deliberately never reusing `PLAYER_CENTERED_SHADOW_FRUSTUM_CONFIG`'s range numbers.

Reason: Step 8F's own audit flagged this as the single most important risk of a naive rollout — range's near/far were measured against range's own light-to-target distance (~26.3m) and its own (Wind-Lift-free) movement envelope; play's light sits at a measurably different distance (~32.1m to the deck) and, critically, Wind Lift can carry the player's body mesh to roughly 10.6m of world-space elevation, an outlier with no range equivalent. Step 8F.0's real per-frame measurement (140 captured frames, real deformed-mesh sampling, real light-space projection) found play's single-frame worst case at 2.557m width / 9.427m height / near-floor 25.916 / far-ceiling 42.043 — reusing range's 6m-tall frustum against a 9.427m real requirement would have clipped the shadow at Wind Lift's apex on every single ascent, silently, the first time a real player used it.

Rejected: reusing range's config outright "since they're the same character and similar light angle" — this is exactly the assumption Step 8F's audit existed to catch before it shipped; the two routes' light geometry and movement envelopes are close enough to look interchangeable at a glance but are not, in fact, interchangeable at the precision a fixed-size shadow frustum requires. Rejected: widening range's own frustum to cover both routes' worst cases in one shared config — would have made range's own texel density permanently worse (its objects/shadows are already validated and human-approved at the current size) to accommodate an outlier (Wind Lift) that range doesn't even have.

Chosen: two independently-measured, independently-configured `ShadowFrustumConfig` values, one per route, each pinned by its own tests against its own measured worst case — a future change to either route's numbers cannot silently affect the other.

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8F: play's shadow-map resolution is derived per-policy (2048² static / 1024² player-centered) — a genuine new per-route difference, not an oversight versus range's fixed 1024²**

Decision: `V2PlayScene.tsx` computes its `shadow-mapSize` JSX prop from `playShadowCasterDecision.fullBodyCasterActive` (`PLAY_PLAYER_CENTERED_SHADOW_CONFIG.mapWidth` vs `PLAY_STATIC_SHADOW_CONFIG.mapWidth`), rather than a single hardcoded map-size constant the way `RangeScene.tsx` effectively has today.

Reason: range's own static and player-centered configs happen to BOTH use 1024² (`STATIC_FULL_FLOOR_SHADOW_FRUSTUM_CONFIG.mapWidth === PLAYER_CENTERED_SHADOW_FRUSTUM_CONFIG.mapWidth === 1024`), which is why `RangeScene.tsx`'s existing `shadowMapSize` variable never needed to depend on caster policy at all — it was already correct for both modes by coincidence. Play's pre-existing static fallback ships at 2048² (the original, unmodified `V2PlayScene.tsx` literal, now named `PLAY_STATIC_SHADOW_CONFIG`), while the approved player-centered candidate is 1024² (matching range's own resolution) — genuinely different values this time, so the JSX prop has to actually branch on which mode is in effect. No live A/B toggle exists on `/v2/play` (see the review-UI entry above), so this is a per-mount-constant derived from the compile-time policy, never a value that changes while the scene is mounted — the same "resolution requires route re-entry to apply live" limitation range's own review panel already documents.

Rejected: picking one resolution for both play modes (e.g. always 1024², dropping the static fallback's map size) — would have silently changed the ALREADY-SHIPPED static fallback's visual behavior as a side effect of this rollout, which the brief's own "static rollback restores ±30/1-80/2048²" requirement explicitly forbids.

Chosen: policy-derived map size, matching the two pre-existing/approved values exactly, with the derivation living at the one JSX call site rather than inside the shared controller (which never touches renderer-level map size at all, on either route — see the controller's own doc comment).

---

**2026-08-01 — Step 8F: a naive same-endpoint ping-pong detector flagged 5 "unexplained" texel-snap transitions during rapid direction reversal — investigated and found to be correctly-explained real motion, not a defect, rather than either suppressed or reported as a false alarm**

Decision: the flagged transitions are documented here as an investigated-and-cleared methodology finding, not treated as evidence of frustum instability requiring hysteresis.

Reason: the temporary texel-stability probe's detector compared only the FIRST and THIRD sample of every 3-sample window, flagging a transition when the snapped light-space position returned to its earlier value with the endpoints' own straight-line distance below a 1cm epsilon. During a rapid-reversal sequence (alternating 'a'/'d' every 150ms), 5 such windows were flagged. Manually inspecting the underlying ground-anchor data for each flag showed the INTERMEDIATE sample (the middle of the three) sat roughly 14-15cm away from both its neighbors — a real, substantial strafe displacement in one direction, immediately reversed by the next input flip, netting a small displacement between the outer two samples while the middle one legitimately reflects a different position. The detector's own endpoint-only comparison could not distinguish "two real, opposite 15cm movements that happen to cancel out" from "spurious jitter with no real movement at all" — only reading the actual anchor positions (not just the pass/fail flag) revealed which one this was.

Rejected: silently omitting the flags from the report because they turned out to be explainable — the milestone's own instruction is that shimmer findings must be disclosed, and a reader independently re-running this same detector would reproduce the same 5 flags; documenting the investigation is what makes that reproducible result not look like a missed defect. Rejected: reporting the flags at face value as "5 instances of texel-snap ping-pong" without the underlying-data investigation — would have overstated the finding (stationary and mouse-look-while-stationary sequences, the actual "shimmer" concern this gate exists to catch, both showed zero flags and a byte-identical snapped position across every sample) and could have triggered unnecessary hysteresis work against a detector artifact rather than a real problem.

Chosen: disclosed with the full investigation; no hysteresis added (matches this milestone's own "do not add hysteresis unless the test reproduces a real failure" instruction — this test reproduced a detector limitation, not a frustum failure).

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B: the two fixed-step accumulators (DroneSquad, DroneBoltPool) stay separate this phase, deliberately not merged for "conceptual neatness"**

Decision: `DroneSquad.tsx` and `DroneBoltPool.tsx` keep their own independent `createStepAccumulator()` instances, unchanged from before Step 9B.

Reason: the Step 9A audit noted this as a minor observation, not a defect — both accumulators are pure functions of the same `rawDelta` each receives from the same R3F frame loop, so they stay in lockstep in practice; there was never a demonstrated bug here. The user's own explicit instruction before implementation began was to keep them separate specifically because merging would touch `DroneBoltPool.tsx` — the protected projectile path — for a purely cosmetic consolidation, which cuts directly against this phase's own "behaviour-neutral extraction" mandate: any touch to the protected file widens the diff of a phase whose entire point is to be provably inert.

Rejected: merging them into one shared accumulator passed to both components — would have been a real, if small, architectural improvement, but at the cost of modifying `DroneBoltPool.tsx` (explicitly protected, "remains untouched unless a compilation issue proves an absolutely unavoidable type-only adjustment is necessary" — no such issue existed) during a phase whose acceptance gate requires zero protected-file changes.

Chosen: left separate, recorded here as a deliberate, revisit-later decision — the user's own instruction was to reconsider only in 9D or later, if movement-architecture work or profiling gives a concrete reason.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B: models exactly the five states the runtime actually produces today, not the seven the public `DroneAiState` type declares**

Decision: the new pure core's `LegacyDroneRuntimeState` union is `'spawning' | 'searching' | 'engaging' | 'attacking' | 'destroyed'` — five values. `lib/v2/play/types.ts`'s existing `DroneAiState` (seven values, also declaring `'inactive'` and `'stunned'`) is untouched.

Reason: Step 9A's audit found, by direct source inspection, that `'inactive'` is never assigned anywhere and `'stunned'` is never assigned as a state value either — it is a timed boolean overlay (`now < stunnedUntil`) layered on top of whichever of the five real states is active. Modelling all seven in the new pure core would mean either inventing behaviour for two values the legacy code never produces (a real, if small, behaviour change — exactly what this phase forbids) or adding dead code paths that could never execute, neither of which serves "byte-identical to production." The user's own instruction, given before implementation began, was explicit on this point: do not introduce the future seven-state union yet, and do not rename the live states to `patrol`/`engage`/`attack`/`dead` (the 9C-onward vocabulary) prematurely.

Rejected: modelling the full seven-state `DroneAiState` union in the pure core "for forward compatibility" — would have made the new code's own type surface lie about what states are actually reachable, and `docs/decisions.md`'s own established pattern (see the Step 8E-E/8F entries) is to fix a doc/code mismatch by recording it and correcting it deliberately, not by quietly widening the implementation to match a stale-looking declaration without evidence the wider declaration was ever intentional. Rejected: silently migrating `lib/v2/play/types.ts`'s `DroneAiState` down to five values as part of this same commit — that file is a wider public type Step 9A didn't audit for other consumers, and narrowing a shared type is exactly the kind of change a "behaviour-neutral extraction" phase should not bundle in.

Chosen: a new, narrower, 9B-scoped type (`LegacyDroneRuntimeState`) lives entirely inside `lib/v2/ai/droneAiTypes.ts`; `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s `getState(): DroneAiState` still typechecks correctly against it via ordinary structural subtyping (five literals are a valid subset of seven), so no consumer-facing signature needed to change. The `DroneAiState`/documentation mismatch itself is now explicitly recorded (this entry, plus the doc comments in `droneAiTypes.ts`/`DroneEnemy.tsx`) rather than left as a silent discrepancy — full migration is deferred to Step 9C, per the user's own explicit instruction.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B: two confirmed legacy quirks — aborted-windup staleness and reset's phase/strafe carry-over — preserved exactly as found, contradicting the phase brief's own assumption about one of them**

Decision: an aborted attack windup leaves `windupUntilMs` at its stale pre-abort value (never reset to 0); `resetLegacyDroneRuntime` reseeds only `lastFireAtMs` and leaves `strafeDirection`/`strafeFlipAtMs` (and, in the adapter, `phase`) completely untouched.

Reason: both are directly confirmed by two independent kinds of evidence — reading `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s actual source (the abort branch reassigns only `state.state`; `resetInternal()`'s body never references `strafeDir`/`strafeFlipAt`/`phase` at all) and the captured pre-refactor browser trace (a sample taken immediately after a forced mid-windup hit showed `state==='engaging'` with `windupUntil` still holding its pre-abort value; deck-a's `strafeDir`/`strafeFlipAt` fields were unchanged across the captured restart). The phase 9B brief's own Section 5 test-list text assumed the opposite for the first one ("aborted windup clears its runtime field") — this is a genuine discrepancy between the brief's assumption and the verified real behaviour, not a matter of interpretation. Per this project's own established precedent (code is ground truth for "what is," per `CLAUDE.md`'s documentation-hierarchy rule, and per this same milestone's own repeated instruction not to "clean up" legacy quirks), the VERIFIED behaviour wins over the brief's unverified assumption.

Rejected: silently implementing the brief's assumed "clears windupUntil on abort" behaviour without flagging the conflict — would have been an actual, if small, behaviour change smuggled into a phase whose entire acceptance gate is zero behaviour change, and would have contradicted the phase's own captured trace evidence. Rejected: "fixing" the reset's phase/strafe carry-over to also reseed those fields "for consistency" — tempting, since it looks like an oversight, but the brief is explicit that even known-awkward legacy quirks stay exactly as found in 9B, with actual cleanup deferred to a later, deliberately-scoped phase.

Chosen: both quirks preserved exactly, each with a dedicated passing unit test (`droneAiStateMachine.test.ts`'s "aborted windup does NOT clear windupUntilMs" and "reset does NOT re-roll strafeDirection or strafeFlipAtMs" tests) and a `droneAiLegacyParity.test.ts` sequence-parity test tying the abort case directly back to the captured trace sample. The discrepancy with the brief's own assumption is disclosed here and in the Step 9B final report, not silently corrected.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B: drone random seeding uses a fixed source-controlled namespace, not a `matchStore.ts`-sourced per-match seed**

Decision: `deriveDroneSeed({ matchSeed, droneId, lifeGeneration })` is fed a fixed constant (`DRONE_AI_SEED_NAMESPACE` in `DroneEnemy.tsx`) as its `matchSeed` input this phase, not a real value drawn from `matchStore.ts`.

Reason: the phase brief explicitly rules out modifying `matchStore.ts` "merely to add a match seed" — that file is on this phase's own protected list. A genuine per-match seed (a fresh value each session, so two different matches produce two different decision traces even for the same drone/generation) is a reasonable future enhancement, but the reproducibility contract THIS phase actually needs — a given drone's Nth life always produces the same trace given the same inputs, so a captured baseline can be replayed and diffed — is already fully satisfied by namespace + drone ID + life generation alone. Each drone still gets its own independent, uncorrelated stream (verified: `deriveDroneSeed` with 8 different drone IDs produces 8 distinct seeds), and restarts still avoid replaying a prior life's sub-sequence (life generation increments on every reset).

Rejected: adding a `matchSeed` field to `matchStore.ts` anyway, reasoning that it's a small, additive, low-risk change — rejected specifically because the brief's protected-systems list exists precisely to keep a "pure extraction, zero behaviour change" phase from quietly growing scope into an adjacent system, however small the addition looks in isolation.

Chosen: a fixed namespace constant now; a real per-match seed is left as an explicit, disclosed future enhancement (not a blocker) for whichever later phase actually needs match-to-match trace variation.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B.1: the Step 9B final report's test count was arithmetically wrong — root cause, and why the reconciliation is trustworthy**

Decision: the Step 9B report's "107 new tests (63 state-machine, 15 legacy-parity, 17 random, 27 import guards)" and "617/617" claims are corrected here. Reconciled, runner-verified counts: `droneAiRandom.test.ts` 17, `droneAiStateMachine.test.ts` 46, `droneAiLegacyParity.test.ts` 15 (at the time of the original Step 9B report), `droneAiImportGuards.test.ts` 27, `playShadowFrustumConfig.test.ts` 12 (not 13). 500 (baseline) + 12 + 105 (17+46+15+27) = 617, which is what `npm test` actually reported — the total itself was right, only the per-file breakdown text was wrong.

Reason: an earlier combined test run (`npx tsx --test droneAiRandom.test.ts droneAiStateMachine.test.ts`) reported "tests 63" as the two files' COMBINED total (17 + 46 = 63). The Step 9B report mistakenly attributed that 63 to `droneAiStateMachine.test.ts` alone, while ALSO separately listing "17 random" as its own line — silently double-counting `droneAiRandom.test.ts`. `playShadowFrustumConfig.test.ts`'s count was independently off by one from a similar manual-tallying slip. Caught only because the user did the arithmetic on the reported numbers and found they didn't sum to the reported total (500 + 122 + 13 ≠ 635, and 63+15+17+27=122≠107) — a direct example of why a reported count must be re-derived from the runner, not summarized from a prior summarization.

Rejected: trusting the original per-file breakdown and only patching the arithmetic to make it "add up" — would have fixed the presentation without fixing the underlying error (an actual double-count), and could have hidden a similar mistake in the NEXT report. Rejected: silently editing the historical Step 9B changelog entry in place with no note — the correction is disclosed as a correction (both here and inline in the changelog) so a reader who saw the original wrong numbers can find out why they changed, rather than the history quietly rewriting itself.

Chosen: every file's count re-verified individually against the real test runner (`npx tsx --test <file>`), cross-checked with a source-level `describe`/`it` counting script (which itself needed a regex fix mid-verification — an early version undercounted `droneAiStateMachine.test.ts` at 44 due to mixed quote styles), and the "500 baseline" independently re-derived by extracting the EXACT pre-9B `package.json` test-file list via `git show d7c22ed:package.json` and running exactly those 32 files against current file contents. `droneAiImportGuards.test.ts`'s own count (9 literal `it(` call sites, 27 actual runtime tests) is explained explicitly rather than left as an apparent discrepancy — its first `describe` block uses nested `for` loops over `PURE_CORE_FILES × FORBIDDEN_IMPORTS`, generating `3 × 7 = 21` tests at runtime plus 3+3 from two static blocks.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B.1: sequence-parity test switched from a live seeded PRNG to a literal random tape, after the seeded version silently failed to exercise its own scenario**

Decision: the full deterministic sequence-parity test in `droneAiLegacyParity.test.ts` feeds the legacy oracle and the pure core an identical literal tape (`createTapeRandomSource`), not two `createSeededRandomSource` instances built from the same numeric seed.

Reason: the first version of this test used `createSeededRandomSource(SAME_SEED)` for both the oracle and the core, reasoning that "same seed, two objects" was the more realistic analogue of two independent per-drone RNG streams. It compiled and the tick-by-tick oracle-vs-core assertions all passed — but a final sanity check (`coreRng.count >= 8`, guarding against the whole test vacuously passing because neither implementation did anything) FAILED. Root cause: the seeded generator's actual float outputs are opaque at test-authoring time, so whether a fire-cooldown had elapsed by a given hand-picked synthetic timestamp depended on an unobserved jitter draw — in the run that failed, the fire-desync jitter happened to push the cooldown past the end of the 6.1-second synthetic timeline entirely, so no shot ever fired and the "meaningful test" floor wasn't met. The tick-by-tick equality assertions were never wrong (oracle and core always agreed, since they consumed the identical stream) — the test just wasn't exercising what it was built to exercise.

Rejected: deleting or loosening the `>= 8` sanity floor to make the failure go away — that floor exists specifically to prevent a sequence-parity test from passing vacuously; removing it would have hidden exactly the kind of silent under-testing this closure pass exists to close. Rejected: hand-computing the seed's actual float outputs in advance to pick a "lucky" seed that happens to trigger the right events — fragile (any future change to `mulberry32`'s internals, however unlikely, would silently break the test with no explanation) and defeats the purpose of a readable, self-documenting timeline.

Chosen: a literal tape (the same technique already used by every formula-parity test in this file) with hand-chosen values that deterministically guarantee the fire/flip events occur at the labelled ticks (e.g. a `0.0` fire-jitter draw so `lastFireAtMs` starts at the spawn timestamp, making the cooldown-elapsed timing fully predictable). The correctness assertions themselves remain purely relative (oracle output === core output at every tick) — never a hand-computed absolute expected value — so this change only affects WHICH events the timeline is guaranteed to exercise, not what property the test proves.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9B.1: live-browser fire-event counts (38 vs 20) are lifecycle evidence, not parity evidence — and are not "fixed" to match**

Decision: the differing fire-event counts between Step 9B's two independently-timed live-browser captures (38 in the pre-refactor baseline, 20 in the post-refactor validation run) are left as-is and explicitly reframed as qualitative/lifecycle evidence, not exact-timing parity evidence — no attempt was made to force them to match.

Reason: two live browser sessions, each driven by real wall-clock `performance.now()` timestamps and real frame-timing jitter, cannot be expected to reach the same fire-cooldown boundaries at the same real-world instant even with an identical deterministic random tape underneath — the SEQUENCE of decisions is deterministic given identical inputs, but the actual wall-clock arrival at each decision point is not, because the two sessions' frame delivery timing itself differs. Treating the 38-vs-20 gap as something to explain away or force into agreement would have been chasing an artifact of session timing, not a logic defect. The property that actually matters — does the new pure core make the same decisions, in the same order, consuming the same random values, as the verified legacy behaviour — is proven exactly by the deterministic synthetic-trace test instead (see the sequence-parity entry above), which needs no real-time browser capture at all.

Rejected: re-capturing more browser sessions hoping for a lucky match, or adding artificial timing synchronization to the Playwright harness to force two sessions to reach identical wall-clock fire counts — both would have produced a fragile, misleading "parity" number without actually strengthening the real evidence. Rejected: removing the browser captures from the evidence package entirely — they still prove real things (zero console errors, correct drone-count bookkeeping across restarts/remounts, the same qualitative behaviour shape) that the deterministic unit tests, running with no renderer at all, cannot prove.

Chosen: both kinds of evidence are kept, with their roles now made explicit in `docs/changelog.md`'s Step 9B.1 entry — live-browser captures for qualitative/lifecycle confidence, the deterministic synthetic trace for exact-timing parity proof.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9C: `LegacyDroneRuntimeState` renamed to `DroneAiRuntimeState` (the one required rename), but `LegacyDroneAiRuntime`/`LegacyDroneAiObservation`/`LegacyDroneAiDecision`/`decideLegacyDroneAi`/`createLegacyDroneRuntime`/`resetLegacyDroneRuntime` deliberately kept as-is**

Decision: only the state UNION type is renamed (`LegacyDroneRuntimeState` → `DroneAiRuntimeState`, gaining `investigating`). Every other "Legacy"-prefixed type and function name in `droneAiTypes.ts`/`droneAiStateMachine.ts` is left unchanged.

Reason: the phase brief explicitly required resolving the state-union mismatch ("Resolve the existing wider public DroneAiState mismatch deliberately... Do not leave two conflicting state definitions") but never asked for a broader rename sweep. The brief's own illustrative code sketches used non-"Legacy" names throughout (`DroneAiRuntime`, `DroneAiObservation`, etc.), which could be read as an implicit ask — but a full rename would touch every call site across `DroneEnemy.tsx` and all six drone-AI test files for zero behavioural benefit, which cuts against this project's own established "don't refactor beyond what's asked" discipline (CLAUDE.md). The runtime/observation/decision types and functions still legitimately describe "the pure extraction of the verified legacy decision logic" even with 9C's additions layered on top (the additions are new behaviour bolted onto a still-legacy-rooted core, not a wholesale replacement of it) — so keeping the names is accurate, not just conservative.

Rejected: a full rename sweep (`LegacyDroneAiRuntime`→`DroneAiRuntime`, `decideLegacyDroneAi`→`decideDroneAi`, etc.) — would have produced a much larger diff for a phase whose own scope fence repeatedly emphasizes minimalism ("the smallest adapter extension," "do not create the full future DroneMovementIntent architecture yet"), and increases the risk of a careless miss during a mechanical find-and-replace across six files.

Chosen: `DroneAiRuntimeState` is the one authoritative six-state union (`droneAiTypes.ts`), and `lib/v2/play/types.ts`'s `DroneAiState` now aliases it directly. Every other identifier keeps its 9B name. If a future phase (9D+) ever sheds enough "legacy-preserved" character that the prefix becomes actively misleading, that rename can be revisited on its own, separately justified merits.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9C: player-generation invalidation collapses `investigating`/`engaging`/`attacking` directly to `searching`, not to `engaging`**

Decision: when `observation.playerGeneration` differs from `runtime.observedPlayerGeneration`, any of `investigating`/`engaging`/`attacking` is forced straight to `searching` (never merely to `engaging`), and all memory fields are cleared in the same step.

Reason: the memory that would have made `engaging` meaningful (a specific remembered/confirmed position) belongs to the OLD player life and is being cleared in this exact same step — collapsing to `engaging` would leave the drone in a state whose own name implies active tracking of a target it no longer has any information about.
