Collapsing directly to `searching` (the state that means "no current target information, ambient patrol") is the accurate description of what the drone actually knows the instant a generation change is detected. If the new life happens to already be visible on this same tick, the EXISTING `searching`→`engaging` same-tick cascade (unchanged since 9B) picks it back up immediately anyway — so this costs nothing in the common case (respawn into view) and is more honest in the uncommon one (respawn where the new spawn point isn't immediately visible).

Rejected: collapsing to `engaging` unconditionally — would have been a "hopeful" state that doesn't reflect the drone's actual (now-empty) knowledge, and would have required either (a) a special one-tick grace period before the normal LOS-loss-confirmation machinery could apply (extra complexity for no proven benefit), or (b) risking the drone briefly reporting `engaging` with a `movementTarget`/facing direction pointed at nothing meaningful.

Chosen: direct collapse to `searching`, verified by a dedicated test proving the two realistic sub-cases separately: the new life not yet visible on the invalidation tick (lands cleanly in `searching`, no attack, no fire) and the new life already visible on that same tick (legitimately cascades straight into a fresh `engaging`/possibly-`attacking` acquisition — a genuine new acquisition of the new life, not a bug, since the old life's attack was still correctly aborted first).

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9C: the LOS-loss confirmation timer starts the instant visibility is lost while EITHER `engaging` OR `attacking`, not only from `engaging`**

Decision: `losLostStartedAtMs` is set on the first tick `canSeePlayer` is false while `runtime.state` is `engaging` OR `attacking`. The actual transition TO `investigating`, however, still only fires from `engaging` — an `attacking` drone must abort to `engaging` first, via the existing, already-verified 9B abort branch in the attack block.

Reason: the phase brief itself offered two valid designs — either an `attacking`→`investigating` shortcut, or the "abort immediately, then confirm" flow it explicitly recommended, requiring the choice to be documented. The recommended flow was chosen because it reuses the abort path 9B already proved correct (windup-field staleness, no-fire guarantee) rather than adding a second, parallel abort mechanism. But a naive implementation of that flow (starting the timer only once state is ALREADY `engaging`, i.e. one tick after the abort) would measure the 250ms window from the wrong origin — the abort itself happens later in the SAME function call as the timer-start check, so if the timer only started once `state==='engaging'`, it would silently begin one tick late every time an `attacking` drone loses LOS, very slightly (one tick, ~16ms at 60fps) understating how long the drone had actually been unable to see the player before the window started. Broadening the timer-START condition to include `attacking` fixes this precisely, without changing which state can transition INTO `investigating`.

Rejected: measuring the confirmation window only from the moment state reads as `engaging` — technically simpler (one less condition to reason about) but measurably (if trivially) less accurate, and would have made "the confirmation window is measured from the moment LOS is truly lost" a claim the code didn't quite keep.

Chosen: broadened timer-start condition (`engaging` OR `attacking`), narrow transition condition (`engaging` only) — verified by a dedicated test asserting `losLostStartedAtMs` equals the exact tick LOS was lost even while the runtime was still nominally `attacking` at that instant.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9C: `observedPlayerGeneration` starts at a `-1` sentinel rather than threading the real starting generation through `createLegacyDroneRuntime`/`resetLegacyDroneRuntime` as a new parameter**

Decision: both construction functions initialize `observedPlayerGeneration` to `-1` — a value no real `matchStore.respawnNonce` can ever equal, since that counter only ever increments from 0. Neither function's signature gained a new parameter for this.

Reason: a fresh or just-reset drone always starts in `spawning` with no memory — the generation-invalidation branch's state-forcing logic only ever affects `investigating`/`engaging`/`attacking`, so a guaranteed-mismatching sentinel on the very first decision tick after construction/reset triggers a completely harmless no-op "invalidation" (nothing to clear, state stays `spawning`) that simply syncs the field to whatever the real current generation is. This is simpler and touches less call-site code than passing the caller's actual current generation into both functions (which would require `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s mount-time `useMemo` and `resetInternal()` to both read `targetSnapshot`/`matchStore` at a point where neither currently does).

Rejected: adding an `initialPlayerGeneration`/`currentPlayerGeneration` parameter to `createLegacyDroneRuntime`/`resetLegacyDroneRuntime` — would have kept the runtime's starting value "correct" from tick zero instead of "harmlessly wrong for exactly one tick," but the value of that correctness is zero (nothing reads `observedPlayerGeneration` before the first decision tick anyway) against a real cost (wider function signatures, more call-site churn, one more thing every future caller must remember to supply correctly).

Chosen: the `-1` sentinel, documented directly on the field itself (`droneAiTypes.ts`) and verified by a dedicated test.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9C: `droneAiPerception.ts` imports `segmentHitsBox`/`ArenaBox` from `lib/v2/play/spawnConfig.ts`/`types.ts` — a new, deliberate cross-directory dependency for the pure AI core**

Decision: rather than re-implementing the AABB slab-intersection test inside `lib/v2/ai/`, the new perception module imports the existing `segmentHitsBox` function directly from `spawnConfig.ts` (a `lib/v2/play/` file), plus the plain `ArenaBox` type from `types.ts`.

Reason: the phase brief explicitly required reuse ("do not duplicate the slab-intersection algorithm"), and `spawnConfig.ts` itself has zero React/R3F/Three.js/Rapier/Zustand import (verified: it's plain arithmetic and data) — so importing its one pure geometry function does not compromise the "no React/R3F/Three.js/Rapier/Zustand/system-clock/built-in-random" guarantee `droneAiImportGuards.test.ts` enforces, which is the actual property that matters for this module's renderer-independence and testability. The OCCLUDER DATA itself (the real arena's cover/wall boxes) is never hardcoded into the perception module — it's supplied by the caller (`occluders: readonly ArenaBox[]`), keeping the module free of any specific arena's geometry.

Rejected: duplicating a second copy of the slab-intersection algorithm inside `lib/v2/ai/` to keep that directory importing nothing outside itself — explicitly forbidden by the brief, and would have created exactly the kind of "two implementations of the same geometry test, now free to drift apart" risk the "reuse, don't duplicate" instruction exists to prevent.

Chosen: the one cross-directory import, scoped to a single pure function and a single plain data type, both independently verified dependency-free — a slightly wider "pure" than 9B's own core had, but not a compromise of the actual guarantee that guard test enforces.

---

**2026-08-02 — Step 9C: live-browser scenario validation uses distance-based (not precise cover-geometry) invisibility, after a real-latency root-cause investigation**

Decision: the Playwright validation scenarios move the (camera-position-overridden) player far outside `DETECT_RADIUS` to force `targetVisible=false`, rather than positioning it behind a specific `COVERS` box.

Reason: an early version of the harness pre-computed a visible/occluded coordinate pair relative to each drone's SPAWN position (`DRONE_SPAWNS`), but drones actively move (approach/retreat/strafe) once engaging — by the time the scenario ran, drones had already drifted meters away from their spawn coordinates (they'd been engaging the player's default spawn position from the moment the match went active, well before the harness took over), silently invalidating the precomputed geometry. A second attempt recomputed the pair dynamically against each drone's LIVE position at the start of the scenario — closer, but drones kept moving throughout the multi-step scenario (steering toward wherever the harness last placed the player), so even a freshly-computed pair could go stale a few seconds later. Root-caused by adding a temporary `lastPerception` field (the real `evaluateDronePerception()` output, not a guess) directly to the debug snapshot and reading it as ground truth — this showed a "visible" candidate the harness's own hand-copied geometry constants had gotten wrong, confirming the issue was drone movement plus a real risk of transcription error in a hand-copied constant set, not a production defect. Since `targetVisible` folds `withinDetectionRadius` and `lineOfSightClear` into the identical boolean the pure core consumes either way — and cover-specific occlusion (multiple occluders, vertical offsets) is already proven directly and thoroughly by `droneAiPerception.test.ts`'s own deterministic suite — using plain distance for the LIVE scenario is not a loss of real coverage, only a loss of "looks like hiding behind a wall" visual authenticity in the screenshots.

Rejected: continuing to fight cover-geometry precision (e.g. re-deriving the pair on every single scenario step, or constraining the drone's own movement during the test) — would have added meaningful harness complexity to re-prove a fact (cover occlusion works) the deterministic suite already proves exactly, for a live-scenario goal (prove the STATE MACHINE integration works end-to-end in a real browser) that doesn't actually need cover specifically to be demonstrated.

Chosen: distance-based visible/invisible toggling for all live scenarios (A/B/C/D/E), documented here and in the Step 9C changelog entry; the deterministic `droneAiPerception.test.ts` suite remains the authoritative, exact proof of cover-geometry correctness.

---

**2026-08-03 — Step 9D: the 9C overlap baseline uses a moving (orbiting) player, not a stationary one**

Decision: the official 9C-vs-9D quantitative overlap comparison (Section 19 of the phase brief) uses a player orbiting a 3m radius on a 6s period, not a player standing still.

Reason: an initial stationary-player capture (player at arena center, 8 drones converging to engage, sampled over 160+ real seconds / ~19 simulated seconds) showed essentially zero visual overlap under 9C's UNMODIFIED movement — drones naturally spread across the engagement ring at different compass angles from their spread-out spawn points and stayed there. That result is real, but it would have made the quantitative gate vacuous (0%→0%, "material reduction" trivially true but meaningless) and doesn't match how the problem was actually described (drones reactively repositioning as the player moves, several converging toward the same new angle at once). A second capture with the player continuously orbiting DID reproduce genuine, if modest, transient overlap under unmodified 9C movement (min XZ 1.06m, 0.24% of samples below the measured visual-overlap threshold, longest sustained overlap 473ms, a max simultaneous cluster of 2) — a real, meaningful baseline to improve against.

Rejected: keeping the stationary-player capture as the official baseline (would have produced a meaningless 0%→0% comparison); inventing an artificially adversarial scenario (e.g. teleporting the player instantly between extremes) that wouldn't correspond to anything a real player does.

Chosen: the moving-player methodology (3m orbit radius, 6s period, player at y=2 near arena center) as the one official baseline/candidate comparison, run with identical parameters (drone count, difficulty, viewport, DPR, sample cadence, capture duration) for both 9C and 9D, documented in `docs/changelog.md`'s Step 9D entry.

---

**2026-08-03 — Step 9D: `resolveDroneMovementIntent()` consumes the already-decided `LegacyDroneMovementMode`, not raw `state`/`stunned`**

Decision: the new pure movement module's input type carries `legacyMovementMode: LegacyDroneMovementMode` (the actual value `decideLegacyDroneAi()` already computed this tick) as the field that drives all branching, rather than re-deriving a movement mode from `state`+`stunned` booleans independently inside the new module.

Reason: the phase brief's own suggested `DroneMovementInput` shape lists `state`/`stunned` as separate fields, which could be read as "re-derive the priority chain here" — but `droneAiStateMachine.ts` already computes exactly this priority chain (stunned > spawning > searching > investigating > engage/attack) as part of `decideLegacyDroneAi()`'s own output. A second, independently-written implementation of that same priority logic inside the new module would be a real duplication risk: the two chains could silently drift apart under a future edit to either file, with nothing forcing them to agree. `state` is still accepted as an informational passthrough field (for caller/test readability), but branching runs on `legacyMovementMode` alone.

Rejected: re-deriving movement mode from `state`+`stunned` inside the new module, matching the brief's suggested shape literally — the brief itself explicitly permits deviating ("the exact representation may differ based on current architecture," "adapt to the real code"), and a second priority-chain implementation is exactly the kind of duplicate-state-union risk the project's own established conventions (see the 9C entries above) consistently reject.

Chosen: `legacyMovementMode` as the sole branching input, `state` kept only as documentation-oriented passthrough — verified by the "movement-mode selection" test block asserting the mapping from every `LegacyDroneMovementMode` value to the new module's own `DroneMovementMode`.

---

**2026-08-03 — Step 9D: separation's reference speed for scaling is the drone's own `strafeSpeedMps`, not the base vector's own magnitude**

Decision: when blending the separation correction into the final direction, its magnitude is scaled by `effectiveStrength * input.strafeSpeedMps` — a fixed, always-nonzero existing speed constant — rather than by the magnitude of the base tactical vector being blended into (`||base||`).

Reason: `||base||` was the first design considered (it has an appealing "always proportionate to whatever the drone was already doing" property), but it fails exactly where separation is needed most: an investigating drone that has ARRIVED at its arrival radius, or a searching drone in the middle of its ambient wander formula, both legitimately produce a near-zero or genuinely-tiny base vector — using that magnitude as separation's own reference would silently disable (or nearly disable) separation exactly when two idle/arrived/wandering drones are most likely to be sitting on top of each other (the investigating-cluster and search-cluster cases the phase brief explicitly calls out in its own Sections 4 and 12). `strafeSpeedMps` is always a real, nonzero, existing (never a new invented) speed value, giving separation a consistent, always-meaningful "how fast should a drone sidestep a neighbour" reference regardless of what its base movement is otherwise doing.

Rejected: `||base||` as the separation reference speed — elegant in the common case, broken in exactly the idle/arrived cases separation exists to fix. Rejected: a brand-new separation-only speed constant — would have been the "new speed tier" the brief's own Section 11 explicitly asks to avoid.

Chosen: `strafeSpeedMps` (an existing, difficulty-scaled, always-nonzero constant already threaded through every drone) as the fixed reference, documented directly in `droneAiMovementIntent.ts`'s own doc comment and exercised by the "search remains home-relative" / "investigate continues approaching memory" dominance tests (both explicitly constructed with a nearby neighbour to prove separation still visibly acts even when the base vector is small).

---

**2026-08-03 — Step 9D: retreat/advance dominance uses BOTH a lower blend-strength cap AND a hard radial-projection clamp, not just one**

Decision: when a drone is retreating or advancing, separation's effective blend strength is first reduced to 50% of its normal cap (`OUT_OF_BAND_BLEND_FACTOR`), AND, after blending, the final vector's radial component (projected onto the drone-to-player axis) is hard-clamped so it can never net-reverse the retreat/advance direction — both mechanisms active together, not just one.

Reason: the phase brief's own Section 10 offered these as alternatives ("either a lower out-of-band cap OR a minimum dot-product constraint"), either of which would independently satisfy the stated requirement under NORMAL conditions (a single nearby neighbour). But the lower cap alone is a probabilistic mitigation, not a guarantee — a sufficiently adversarial multi-neighbour configuration (several drones simultaneously pushing the same drone toward the player) could in principle still produce a net-positive radial component even at a reduced blend strength, depending on exact neighbour geometry. The hard clamp alone would work as an absolute guarantee, but combining it with the lower cap keeps the TYPICAL (non-adversarial) case smoother/less abrupt — the clamp only actually engages in the rare case the reduced-strength blend still would have crossed zero, rather than being the sole mechanism doing all the work on every single tick.

Rejected: implementing only ONE of the two mechanisms — both satisfy the brief's letter, but relying solely on the probabilistic lower cap leaves a theoretical (if narrow) multi-neighbour edge case unguarded, and relying solely on the hard clamp produces a slightly more abrupt correction on every tick where separation is active at all, rather than only when it would otherwise have actually reversed the direction.

Chosen: both mechanisms together, verified by dedicated dominance tests using an ADVERSARIALLY-placed single neighbour (positioned specifically to try to flip the net radial direction) and confirmed live in the browser (Scenario D, both retreat and advance) with an even more direct adjacent-neighbour placement than the unit tests use.

---

**2026-08-03 — Step 9D.1: three independent zero-inflation blend designs were measured live and rejected before reaching a bounded, close-proximity COLLISION-RECOVERY allowance**

Decision: the final Step 9D.1 blend combines a directional-lerp rotation (unbounded by `separation.strength`'s own ceiling — see the next entry) with a small, proximity-scaled CLOSE-PROXIMITY COLLISION-RECOVERY magnitude allowance (`EMERGENCY_SPEED_BOOST_MAX`), rather than staying strictly at `final.length <= base.length` at all times. This is deliberately framed and documented as collision recovery, not a movement-speed buff: it governs only how fast a drone may separate from a neighbour it is already (or nearly) touching, is exactly 0 for any drone with no neighbour inside `neighbourRadiusM`, and never applies to ordinary combat positioning at normal engagement distances.

Reason: the human reviewer's own closure brief required "no speed inflation... Required: 0 apart from floating-point tolerance," with a suggested implementation (add separation, then rescale down to `||base||` if it overshoots). That exact design was implemented first and unit-tested cleanly (60→76 tests, all passing, including a 200-sample randomized sweep proving zero measured increase) — but a REAL, extended (200s, 8-drone, moving-player) capture, the same methodology already used for the 9C-vs-9D quantitative gate, showed it was measurably WORSE than doing nothing: 14.1% of samples below the overlap threshold and 12.1s of continuous sustained overlap, against 9C's own unmodified 0.24%/473ms and 9D's own (rejected) uncapped result of 0%/0ms. Two further zero-inflation redesigns (directional interpolation instead of additive-then-clamp; then decoupling the interpolation factor from `separation.strength`'s own 0.5 ceiling so direction could fully commit under genuine urgency) were each ALSO measured live and each got WORSE, not better (22.6s/18.5%, then 13.2s/21.6%). The consistent geometric cause, confirmed by inspecting the actual closest-pair samples at the moment of worst overlap in each run: two drones strafing in OPPOSITE rotational senses around a continuously-moving player cross paths at a closing speed no same-speed redirection can out-run, regardless of how decisively the direction rotates — a kinematic limit, not a insufficiently-clever direction formula. The phase brief's own acceptance gate anticipated exactly this possibility in its closing paragraph: "The brief allowed a bounded emergency multiplier only if genuinely necessary and measured; it did not automatically approve permanent additive speed during ordinary separation" — three independent, real, live-measured failures of the strict interpretation constitute exactly that "genuinely necessary and measured" bar.

Rejected: persisting with a fourth or fifth strictly-zero-inflation redesign on the theory that the RIGHT direction-only formula must exist somewhere — after three structurally different attempts (additive-clamp, direction-lerp-capped, direction-lerp-decoupled) all failed the same way for the same underlying kinematic reason, continuing to search that same design space was judged very unlikely to succeed and expensive to keep re-measuring (each live verification costs ~200 real seconds). Rejected: reverting to the ORIGINAL (pre-9D.1, also-rejected) uncapped additive design wholesale — that design's "permanent additive speed during ordinary separation" is exactly what the closure brief singled out as NOT automatically approved, even though it happened to work.

Chosen: a narrow, bounded, proximity-scaled emergency allowance (zero at ordinary range, capped even at maximum urgency) layered on top of the direction-first design, with the exact final report disclosing all three failed attempts and their measurements rather than presenting only the successful configuration — see the changelog's Step 9D.1 entry for the full measured trace.

---

**2026-08-03 — Step 9D.1: the emergency speed-boost ceiling is scaled by `urgency` (separation.strength renormalized against its own known ceiling), not by `separation.strength` directly**

Decision: both the directional-lerp rotation factor and the emergency magnitude ceiling use `urgency = min(1, separation.strength / DRONE_LOCAL_SEPARATION.maxBlendStrength)` — a value that reaches a genuine 1.0 exactly at `hardSeparationRadiusM` (already visually touching) — rather than using `separation.strength` (which never exceeds `maxBlendStrength`, 0.5 by construction) as that scaling factor directly.

Reason: `separation.strength`'s own 0.5 ceiling exists to bound the RAW separation-direction signal `computeLocalSeparation()` returns (a general-purpose value also consumed for other purposes) — it was never meant to also cap how strongly the FINAL blend can commit to escaping. Using it directly as the blend/urgency factor was the second of the three failed 9D.1 attempts: even when a neighbour was already touching (the most urgent case there is), direction could only ever rotate halfway toward pure separation and magnitude could only ever reach a modest boost, because the raw signal itself never climbs above 0.5. Renormalizing against the SAME ceiling `computeLocalSeparation()` already uses internally to reach 1.0 (weight=1.0) inside `hardSeparationRadiusM` lets both the direction rotation and the magnitude allowance reach their own full, separately-defined ceilings (100% direction commitment, `1+EMERGENCY_SPEED_BOOST_MAX`× magnitude) exactly when genuinely warranted, without changing `separation.strength`'s own meaning or its other consumers.

Rejected: raising `maxBlendStrength` itself to 1.0 so `separation.strength` could reach a genuine ceiling directly — would have changed the meaning and range of an already-established, tested field (also read by `DroneMovementIntent.separationStrength`, exposed for introspection/telemetry) for a concern (blend-factor renormalization) that only concerns the movement blend internally.

Chosen: a separate, locally-scoped `urgency` value computed once per tick from `separation.strength`'s existing, unchanged range, used only inside the blend calculation — `separation.strength` itself keeps its original 0..0.5 meaning everywhere else.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E: Medium's `targetMemoryDurationMs` reuses `DRONE_PERCEPTION_MEMORY.investigateDurationMs` verbatim, rather than restating `4500` as a second literal**

Decision: `droneAiDifficulty.ts`'s per-difficulty memory-duration table sources Medium's own entry directly from `DRONE_PERCEPTION_MEMORY.investigateDurationMs` (imported from `droneAiPerception.ts`), not a fresh `4500` literal.

Reason: this codebase's own established convention (see `difficulty.ts`'s "SINGLE SOURCE OF TRUTH... never duplicates, never restates" doc comment, and `droneAiDifficulty.ts`'s own `attackWindupMs` field doing the identical thing with `DRONE.WINDUP_MS`) treats a second copy of the same numeric fact as a live drift risk, not a harmless convenience — a future edit to one 4500 with the other left behind would silently desync Medium's actual behaviour from what the 9C module's own doc comment (arena-spacing/approach-speed reasoning) still claims backs it. Reusing the existing constant costs nothing (both modules already sit in `lib/v2/ai/`) and makes "Medium is byte-identical to pre-9E" a structural guarantee, not just an asserted one.

Rejected: a fresh `4500` literal in `droneAiDifficulty.ts`, matching Low/Max's own pattern of a new per-tier literal — simpler to read in isolation, but reintroduces exactly the "two 4500s that could drift" risk the codebase's own conventions exist to avoid, for a value where reuse was trivially available.

Chosen: `medium: DRONE_PERCEPTION_MEMORY.investigateDurationMs`, verified by a dedicated test asserting strict equality between the two, not just that both happen to read `4500`.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E: `acquireReactionDelayMs` is 0 for both Medium and Max, not a smaller-but-nonzero value for Max**

Decision: Max's resolved profile carries `acquireReactionDelayMs: 0`, identical to Medium's — Max's own differentiation from Medium comes entirely from `targetMemoryDurationMs` (5250ms vs. 4500ms), not from any further reaction-speed advantage.

Reason: the brief's own constraint was "no slower than Medium, optionally faster/immediate" — but Medium is already seeded to react on the exact tick of acquisition (0ms delay), and there is no meaningful way to react FASTER than the current tick without introducing a negative delay (nonsensical — it would require acting before the observation that triggers it). Treating "immediate" as the floor both tiers already share is the correct reading of "optionally faster," not a missed opportunity to differentiate Max further. The player-facing goal ("the highest tier remains more persistent without becoming unfair") is already satisfied by Max's longer memory alone — stacking an ALSO-tighter reaction on top would risk reading as unfair twitchiness rather than persistence.

Rejected: inventing a negative or sub-zero reaction concept (e.g. "pre-emptive" reaction) to give Max a nominally distinct number from Medium — would have no real mechanical meaning (nothing can happen before the tick that triggers it) and would only exist to make two numbers look different on paper.

Chosen: `medium: 0, max: 0` — verified by a unit test asserting `max <= medium` (satisfied, trivially, by equality) rather than asserting strict inequality, since the brief's own constraint was never "strictly faster."

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E: the reaction-gate deadline (`reactionReadyAtMs`) lives on the pure runtime; the presentation-cue deadlines (`acquirePulseUntilMs`/`fireFlashUntilMs`) live on the adapter, not the runtime**

Decision: `reactionReadyAtMs` was added as a genuine field on `LegacyDroneAiRuntime` (the pure core), while the telegraph's own acquire-pulse and fire-flash deadlines are plain `useRef` values owned by `DroneEnemy.tsx` (the adapter), re-supplied to `resolveDroneTelegraph()` fresh every tick rather than also living on the pure runtime.

Reason: `reactionReadyAtMs` is COMBAT TRUTH — it directly gates whether a windup is allowed to start, so it must be part of the deterministic, testable pure-core contract (`droneAiStateMachine.ts`/`droneAiTypes.ts`), exactly like `windupUntilMs`/`stunnedUntilMs` already are. The acquire-pulse/fire-flash deadlines are PRESENTATION ONLY — nothing in `droneAiStateMachine.ts` ever reads them, and Rule 4 ("presentation must never drive combat truth") is easiest to guarantee structurally by never letting a presentation-only value anywhere near the pure runtime's own type at all, rather than adding it there and then trusting every future edit to remember not to read it back into a decision. This mirrors the codebase's own existing precedent: `phase` (hover-bob angle) has stayed adapter-owned since 9B for the identical reason.

Rejected: adding `acquirePulseUntilMs`/`fireFlashUntilMs` to `LegacyDroneAiRuntime` alongside `reactionReadyAtMs`, for a cleaner "everything's in one place" contract — would have required either the pure core silently ignoring fields it never reads (confusing) or `droneAiStateMachine.ts` growing a presentation concept it structurally shouldn't have, exactly what Rule 4 forbids.

Chosen: pure combat-gating state stays in the runtime; presentation-only deadlines stay adapter-owned refs, re-fed into the pure `resolveDroneTelegraph()` every tick — verified structurally by an import-guard test asserting zero coupling between `droneAiTelegraph.ts` and `droneAiStateMachine.ts` in either direction, not merely by doc-comment claim.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E: catching a one-shot decision event (`targetAcquired`) for live-browser evidence required an in-page event log, not external polling**

Decision: the browser-validation harness for the reaction-gate timing recorded every `targetAcquired`/`startWindup`/`fireExactlyOnce` occurrence into an append-only array written SYNCHRONOUSLY inside the same tick the pure core's decision fires (`window.__drone9EEvents__.push(...)`, temporary instrumentation, fully removed afterward), rather than polling a single latest-snapshot object from outside the page via Playwright.

Reason: an initial approach polled a per-drone snapshot object (overwritten every `update()` call) from OUTSIDE the page at a fixed JS-side interval. Two independent attempts (a broad 14s multi-difficulty sweep, then a tighter restart-triggered re-acquisition capture) both caught zero live acquisitions despite real combat clearly happening (telegraph phases cycling, drones firing) — root-caused to this environment's headless rendering running at roughly 1-2fps under an active 8-drone scene (a previously-documented characteristic of this environment, first identified during Step 9D/9D.1's own overlap-capture work): a one-shot flag true for exactly one substep can be overwritten by a LATER substep within the same slow rendered frame before an external poll ever reads it, and/or the acquisition can complete entirely between two widely-spaced external polls. This is the identical class of problem 9D/9D.1 solved for long-duration captures by running the ENTIRE sampling loop inside one `page.evaluate()` — the fix here is the same idea applied to point-in-time EVENTS rather than continuous SAMPLES: record every occurrence as it happens, in-page, so external polling frequency can no longer race past it.

Rejected: increasing external polling frequency to compensate — the fundamental issue is real rendered-frame cadence, not polling cadence; polling faster than the page itself updates cannot catch a value between two updates that never happened. Rejected: accepting "zero captured live acquisitions" as sufficient (relying solely on the 95 deterministic unit tests) — the unit tests already exhaustively prove the pure LOGIC is correct in isolation; the browser pass exists specifically to prove the WIRING between `DroneSquad.tsx` → `DroneEnemy.tsx` → the pure core is connected correctly end-to-end in the real running scene, which an unpolled event genuinely could not demonstrate.

Chosen: an in-page, synchronous, append-only event log, read once at the end of a capture window — successfully caught exact-millisecond reaction-gate timing (`reactionReadyAtMs === acquiredAt + 350` for Low, `=== acquiredAt` for Medium/Max) on the very first run after the change, across all three difficulties. Documented here so a future phase facing the same "external polling misses a one-shot event in this slow-headless environment" problem does not have to re-discover the fix.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E.1: an unverified reference to a "diagnostic task" was not acted on until independently checked against actual repo/tool state**

Decision: when asked to "check on" a named background task investigating a described regression, the task ID was queried directly (found not to exist) and the described regression numbers were checked against the actual committed source (`EMERGENCY_SPEED_BOOST_MAX`, `HEAD`) before any further action — rather than treating the description as established fact and proceeding to "fix" `droneAiMovementIntent.ts` based on it.

Reason: the described regression (14.1% samples below threshold / 12.1s sustained overlap) matched, exactly, the FIRST of five iterations from the already-resolved, already-approved, already-committed Step 9D.1 speed-cap saga — a rejected intermediate attempt, not the shipped state. The actual shipped/committed code (`EMERGENCY_SPEED_BOOST_MAX = 1.0`, confirmed live in the file) measures 0%/0ms/2.33m minimum distance — the opposite of a regression. Proceeding on the unverified premise would have meant reopening and rewriting already-approved, human-signed-off, committed code based on a claim that didn't survive a two-command check (`TaskOutput` for the task ID, `git log`/`grep` for the code state). This is the same category of discipline as the codebase's own established "verify from source before implementing, do not trust approximate/secondhand values" convention (see the Step 9E entry above), applied to a claim about PROJECT STATE rather than a claim about SOURCE VALUES.

Rejected: treating the task reference as presumptively real and either fabricating a plausible-sounding diagnostic summary, or beginning a "fix" to `droneAiMovementIntent.ts` on the strength of the description alone — both would have violated this session's own repeated, explicit "do not fabricate results for work that was not actually done" and "verify actual repo state before acting" discipline.

Chosen: report the discrepancy directly (task doesn't exist; the cited numbers belong to a superseded, rejected iteration; the shipped code already measures the opposite result) and wait for the user's own read on the situation rather than guessing. The user independently confirmed the same finding and explicitly reconfirmed 9D/9D.1 stay closed — validating the check-first approach.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E.1: the muzzle-flash mesh moved to local +Z, but the pre-existing eye mesh (also likely affected by the same `lookAt()` quirk) was deliberately left untouched**

Decision: `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s new (9E) muzzle-flash mesh was repositioned from local `(0, 0, -0.42)` to `(0, 0, +0.42)` after live measurement proved it rendered on the drone's far side from the player (`THREE.Object3D.lookAt()` orients a plain object's local -Z axis AWAY from its target, confirmed directly against this repo's installed `three` build). The pre-existing eye mesh, authored at local `(0, 0, -0.3)` since Milestone 6 and commented "faces forward (-Z)," was NOT touched, despite almost certainly suffering the identical misorientation.

Reason: the muzzle flash is 9E's own new code, directly required by THIS phase's own explicit acceptance gate ("no visibly detached flash," bolt/muzzle origin alignment) — fixing it is squarely in scope, mechanical (a one-line sign flip), and low-risk (a mesh that was `visible=false` 99%+ of the time in every prior validation pass, so no prior human visual sign-off is being silently invalidated). The eye is a FAR larger-blast-radius change: it predates Milestone 9 entirely, has been visually present (and implicitly accepted — no prior phase's own extensive real-browser screenshot evidence ever flagged it) across every one of this project's own milestones 6 through 9E, and "fix the eye's facing direction" is the kind of change that needs its own dedicated, explicitly-scoped pass with real human visual comparison (before/after), not a silent side effect of an unrelated validation-closure pass whose own brief explicitly said "no production-code changes unless a real defect is found" (singular, scoped to what THIS pass's own required checks actually gate on).

Rejected: fixing both meshes together for consistency — would have doubled the size and risk of an already-adjacent, disclosed finding, and specifically violates the brief's own "no production-code changes unless a real defect is found during the missing browser validation" scoping (the eye's own facing was not one of the checks this pass's brief required). Rejected: fixing neither and just disclosing both as known issues — would have left Section 5 (bolt/muzzle origin alignment), one of this pass's OWN explicit required gates, genuinely failing.

Chosen: fix the in-scope, newly-added muzzle mesh only; disclose the eye's likely-identical issue prominently (code comment, changelog, this entry) as a separate, out-of-scope finding for the user to schedule as its own pass. Re-measured after the fix: bolt-direction and muzzle-offset-direction vectors went from anti-parallel (dot product ≈ -0.9999) to parallel (≈ +0.9999-1.0), origin gap dropped from ~0.9-1.0m to ~0.29-0.32m (consistent with the pre-existing hover-bob amplitude plus the intentional 0.5-vs-0.42 magnitude difference).

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E.1: proving "zero stale state after a restart" required a temporary unconditional-per-frame runtime read, decoupled from `update()`'s own phase gating**

Decision: a new temporary handle method (`debugRuntimeSnapshot9E1`, read `runtimeRef.current` directly) was added and called from `DroneSquad.tsx`'s `useFrame` UNCONDITIONALLY — before the `if (match.phase !== 'active') return` gate — specifically to observe a drone's true post-reset runtime state during the 3-second `'countdown'` window immediately following a restart.

Reason: the existing per-drone debug snapshot (and, in production, the ENTIRE drone AI decision pipeline) only updates inside `update()`, which is itself gated to `phase === 'active'` by design (drones "hold their spawn-in" during countdown, per this component's own doc comment). Reading that gated snapshot immediately after calling `restart()` — even though the underlying `runtimeRef.current` had ALREADY been synchronously reset via `resetInternal()`, which runs BEFORE the phase gate — showed STALE pre-restart values for the entire ~3s countdown, because nothing had called `update()` yet to refresh the snapshot. An initial attempt to verify "zero stale reaction deadlines/windups after restart" this way produced a false negative: every restart cycle appeared to retain full pre-restart combat state, when the real runtime had already, correctly, cleared it.

Rejected: sampling the existing update()-gated snapshot with a longer wait — this would only ever show state from AFTER the first real `'active'` tick (by which point, on Medium's 0ms reaction delay, a fresh acquisition may have ALREADY re-populated `reactionReadyAtMs` with a NEW, legitimate value, making "was it ever actually null" unprovable from that snapshot alone). Rejected: trusting the existing unit tests alone (`resetLegacyDroneRuntime` returning `reactionReadyAtMs: null` is already exhaustively proven in isolation) — this pass's own purpose was proving the WIRING (that `DroneSquad.tsx` really does call `reset()` for every drone, synchronously, before any phase gate) works correctly end-to-end in the real running scene, which the pure-function unit tests cannot demonstrate by themselves.

Chosen: a temporary, phase-independent runtime read, added and used only for this validation pass, then fully removed — confirmed all 8 drones showing `reactionReadyAtMs === null`, `windupUntilMs === 0`, `investigateUntilMs === null`, state `'spawning'`, `lifeGeneration` incremented by exactly 1, on every one of 5 restart cycles.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E.2: the muzzle-facing regression test uses the REAL `three` package directly, a deliberate exception to `lib/v2/ai/`'s own "no Three.js" convention**

Decision: `droneVisualConfig.test.ts` imports `three` and constructs real `THREE.Object3D` instances (calling the actual `.lookAt()` implementation this project ships) to prove `DRONE_MUZZLE_LOCAL_OFFSET`'s facing direction, rather than hand-deriving the expected geometry or asserting only the constant's raw sign.

Reason: the ROOT CAUSE of the original defect (Step 9E.1) was a specific, easy-to-get-wrong fact about `THREE.Object3D.lookAt()`'s own behavior for non-Camera/non-Light objects — a fact this project only discovered by writing a small isolated script against the real library, not from documentation or intuition. A test that only asserts `DRONE_MUZZLE_LOCAL_OFFSET[2] > 0` would catch a sign flip but would NOT catch a future regression where someone "fixes" the sign back to match a WRONG mental model of `lookAt()`'s convention, or where a future Three.js version changes that convention. Exercising the real library directly is the only way to make the test's own claim ("this offset ends up on the facing side") independently verifiable against the actual dependency, not against a possibly-incorrect assumption baked into the test itself.

Rejected: a pure-math test reimplementing `lookAt()`'s rotation construction by hand (matches this codebase's usual "no Three.js in tested modules" preference) — would require transcribing the exact same algorithm the test is trying to verify, making the test tautological (it would pass even if BOTH the hand-transcription and the real implementation shared the same wrong assumption, exactly the failure mode that let the original bug ship undetected). Rejected: skipping the test entirely and relying on the existing live-measured dot-product evidence (9E.1's own browser trace) — a one-time trace proves the code was correct AT THAT MOMENT, not that a later edit can't silently reintroduce the defect, which was the reviewer's own explicit concern.

Chosen: a small, explicitly-scoped, disclosed exception — real `three`, real `Object3D`/`lookAt()`, asserted via `.getWorldPosition()` and a dot-product check — confined to this one presentation-orientation regression file; `lib/v2/ai/`'s own pure-core modules remain untouched by Three.js, unaffected by this precedent. The suite includes a dedicated "would this test have caught the original bug" assertion (the old negative-Z offset fails the identical check), which is the actual evidence the test has teeth, not just that it passes today.

---

**2026-08-04 — Step 9E.2: reaction/cooldown live hit-priority proof was not achieved, after exhausting the same technique that proved acquire — a directly-measured environment frame-rate limit, not a methodology gap**

Decision: after applying the SAME synchronous, zero-CDP-latency in-component trigger technique that successfully proved the `acquire` case deterministically (on the first attempt), `reaction` and `cooldown` were each attempted with escalating rigor — a 40s wait, a 100s wait, then a 20-restart loop yielding roughly 100 independent fresh-acquisition trials across 5 drones — and all failed. Rather than continue retrying indefinitely or claim a false PASS, the actual constraint was directly measured: an in-page `requestAnimationFrame` sampler run for 3 real seconds with zero external polling overhead captured only 5 distinct simulation ticks, meaning this environment's real inter-frame gap under this scene's headless rendering load is approximately 600-780ms.

Reason: `acquire` and `fire` share a property `reaction`/`cooldown` do not: both begin on EXACTLY the same tick as a one-shot pure-core decision event (`targetAcquired`/`fireExactlyOnce`). Since the trigger is armed BEFORE that event and checked synchronously inside the SAME `update()` call that computes it, catching them requires zero luck — the triggering tick and the observing tick are IDENTICAL. `reaction` (a window that opens only after the 180ms acquire pulse fades) and `cooldown` (a window that opens only after the 80ms fire-flash ends) both require a SEPARATE, LATER tick to land inside a sub-200ms span — and the direct rAF measurement proves this environment's own real tick spacing (~600-780ms) is itself WIDER than either target window, meaning most of the time NO tick lands inside them at all, independent of how quickly any instrumentation reacts once one does. This is a rendering-pipeline-level constraint (the browser's actual `requestAnimationFrame` callback rate under this specific 3D scene's load), not a defect in the reasoning or a limitation of the trigger mechanism, which is provably latency-free (it already caught `acquire` on the first try).

Rejected: continuing indefinite retries on the theory that a long-enough wait must eventually succeed — the direct frame-rate measurement shows the target windows are narrower than the TYPICAL gap between observable states, not just occasionally narrower, making success a low-probability event per trial rather than a near-certainty given enough trials, and this project's own "no silent caps" convention favors a bounded, disclosed stopping point over open-ended cost with no better guarantee of success. Rejected: modifying `DRONE_COMBAT_TELEGRAPH`'s `acquirePulseMs`/`cooldownSettleMs` values (or `acquireReactionDelayMs`) to widen the windows for testing purposes — explicitly forbidden ("do not modify combat timing," "do not modify difficulty values") and would mean testing against artificially-widened windows rather than the real, shipped values. Rejected: claiming a live PASS for reaction/cooldown based on the acquire case's success by analogy — the underlying pure-function priority resolution (`resolveDroneTelegraph()`'s `hit` check, unconditional and second-only-to-`destroyed`, with zero phase-specific special-casing) is the same code path for all six non-hit phases and is already exhaustively unit-tested per-phase, but the LIVE-OBSERVATION claim specifically is not established for these two phases and should not be reported as if it were.

Chosen: report `acquire` as a genuine, deterministic live PASS; report `reaction`/`cooldown` as NOT independently live-observed, with the exact measured root cause (not "environment seems slow" but "measured 5 distinct ticks across 3 real seconds, ~600-780ms inter-frame gap") documented here and in the changelog, backed by the already-exhaustive deterministic unit-test coverage of the identical, uniform priority-resolution code path.

---

**2026-08-04 -- Milestone 9F: stuck-recovery attack-blocking required a small, disclosed edit to droneAiStateMachine.ts/droneAiTypes.ts -- the first change to either file since Step 9E**

Decision: implement attack-suppression during an active stuck-recovery episode as ONE new optional observation field, `recoveryBlocksAttack?: boolean` (droneAiTypes.ts), consulted by TWO added conditions on droneAiStateMachine.ts's existing windup-start/abort if/else-if pair -- nothing else in either file changes. The field defaults falsy and is byte-identical to its prior absence when omitted, so every pre-9F call site and test is unaffected.

Reason: 9F's own brief requires recovery to "temporarily block attack initiation" and abort an in-progress windup "through the existing legitimate abort path" -- both of which are inherently attack-block concerns, and the attack block lives in droneAiStateMachine.ts. Unlike 9D/9E (which explicitly protected this file), 9F's own protected-systems list does NOT name droneAiStateMachine.ts or droneAiTypes.ts, and the brief's own "Critical Architectural Decision" section anticipates exactly this need ("recovery must temporarily block attack initiation... Stop and report before adding a new AI state if repository structure genuinely makes the overlay impossible" -- implying a small, non-state-adding edit to enable the overlay IS in scope, only a new state value is not).

Rejected: faking `observation.canSeePlayer = false` from the adapter while recovery is active, to trigger the EXISTING LOS-loss abort path without touching droneAiStateMachine.ts at all -- rejected because it would fabricate a false perception fact (the brief's own "recovery must not fabricate searching/investigating/engaging" and "underlying perception... must remain truthful" rules), and a fake LOS loss lasting longer than `losLossConfirmMs` (250ms, and recovery phases run 900ms+) would incorrectly drive the drone into a truthful-looking but actually-fabricated `investigating` transition. Rejected: letting the adapter simply skip calling `bolts.spawn()` while recovery is active but otherwise leaving the pure decision unmodified -- rejected because the pure core would still internally complete the windup/fire cycle and advance `lastFireAtMs` as if a real shot had fired, silently shortening the drone's next REAL cooldown once recovery ends (an actual, if subtle, "free" cooldown reduction) -- exactly the kind of combat-integrity leak the brief's "no free shot" requirement exists to prevent.

Chosen: the two-condition, optional-field extension described above. `DroneAiRuntimeState` remains exactly six values (re-verified by a dedicated test in `droneAiImportGuards.test.ts`); `droneAiStuckRecovery.ts` never imports `droneAiStateMachine.ts` (verified by a dedicated import-guard test) -- the coupling runs in exactly one direction, and only through one plain boolean the adapter derives. All 201 pre-existing droneAiStateMachine/droneAiLegacyParity/droneAiMovementIntent tests pass unmodified; 7 new tests cover the gate itself (byte-identical when omitted, blocks new windups, aborts in-progress ones with zero fire/spread-RNG/lastFireAtMs change, resumes normally afterward with no free shot, and leaves truthful state transitions like investigating unaffected).

**2026-08-04 -- Milestone 9F: several browser-validation scenarios (G, H, M, full stun/death, instrumented perf comparison) were left to unit-test coverage only, disclosed rather than silently skipped**

Decision: given this milestone's already-large scope (three new pure modules, 137 new tests, ten-plus live scenarios), Scenario G (two/three-drone boundary+separation interaction), Scenario H (investigating near a boundary), Scenario M (real Wind Lift player-arc interaction with drone altitude), a full live stun/death exercise beyond the incidental combat-abort evidence Scenario F's own trace already provides, and an instrumented p50/p95/p99 frame-time comparison were not independently live-validated. Each is instead backed by its own dedicated pure-module unit coverage.

Reason: the acceptance-gate-critical scenarios -- normal-play false-positive rate (Scenario A), the constraint math itself live (B/C/D), the detector actually firing on a genuine stuck condition and escaping (E), full escalation to a real teleport (F), the attack-blocking wiring (I, confirmed incidentally within the same F trace), player-generation interaction (K), and lifecycle stability (L, 5 restarts + 5 remounts + difficulty 8->5->8) -- represent the specific claims most likely to hide a real defect if fabricated or assumed rather than observed, and all ten were achieved with genuine, real, unmanufactured browser evidence (see changelog). The remaining scenarios each test an INTERACTION between 9F and an already-independently-proven system (9D separation is untested by 9F itself and unmodified; stun/destroy handling in the pure recovery module has its own direct unit tests; Wind Lift's real physics are owned entirely by the protected, untouched PlayerController.tsx) rather than a novel claim about 9F's own logic.

Rejected: fabricating or inferring a live result for these scenarios to present a complete checklist -- this project's own established discipline (see the Step 9E.2 entries above) is to report exactly what was observed, not round up partial coverage to a full pass. Rejected: spending further, unbounded time attempting full live coverage of every lettered scenario at the cost of the milestone's overall correctness/report quality, given the already-substantial real evidence already gathered for the highest-risk claims.

Chosen: report the achieved scenarios as genuine live PASSes with their real measured numbers, and name the unachieved ones explicitly, with the specific unit-test suite that backs each one instead -- consistent with the "no silent caps" principle (dropped coverage is disclosed, not hidden as an implied "everything was checked").

**2026-08-04 -- Milestone 9F.1: the real Wind Lift player arc could not be produced live in this session, root-caused to two verified, environment-specific facts -- disclosed rather than approximated or worked around with a protected-file edit**

Decision: after confirming synthetic Playwright keyboard/mouse input produces no real player movement (camera position remains frozen at spawn despite 3+ seconds of held WASD input), the root cause was directly measured rather than assumed: `document.pointerLockElement` stays `null` after a canvas click in this headless session, and `PlayerController.tsx`'s own `canMove` gate requires `document.pointerLockElement !== null`. A second candidate workaround -- directly overriding `camera.position` (the brief's own "teleport-assisted entry is acceptable" allowance) -- was also directly tested and confirmed non-viable: the override is reverted within about one real second by the protected `PlayerController.tsx`'s own authoritative Rapier-body-driven position publish, which runs every frame regardless of what last touched the camera object directly.

Reason: both of the two realistic paths to a real player-driven Wind Lift rise were exhausted and precisely diagnosed, not merely assumed to fail. Modifying `PlayerController.tsx` to accept an external position override would violate the brief's own explicit protected-file list. Chasing a Playwright/Chromium flag combination that reliably grants synthetic pointer lock was judged out of proportion to this one sub-scenario given the closure pass's already-large scope and the fact that six other, higher-priority live scenarios (multi-drone boundary+separation, investigating-near-boundary, stun-during-recovery, death-during-recovery, the exact latency reconciliation, and the performance benchmark) all needed to complete first.

Rejected: fabricating or approximating a "real Wind Lift arc" result from the partial evidence that WAS available (drones near the lift showing 0 exclusion violations against a stationary player) -- that evidence is real but is not the same claim as "drones correctly track a REAL vertically-moving player," and reporting it as equivalent would misrepresent what was actually observed. Rejected: editing the protected `PlayerController.tsx` to add a debug-only position-override escape hatch -- explicitly forbidden by 9F.1's own protected-file list, and the established project discipline treats "the brief said not to touch this file" as final regardless of how small the requested carve-out would be.

Chosen: report the partial (near-lift, stationary-player) result honestly, name it explicitly as partial, and disclose the exact two-part root cause (pointer-lock gesture requirement + physics-body position ownership) so a future pass has a precise, verified starting point rather than "environment seems unreliable."

**2026-08-04 -- Milestone 9F.1: a real test-harness bug (position-override ordering) was found and fixed during this closure pass -- disclosed as harness-only, not a production defect**

Decision: the Section-25/9F-style one-shot debug position-override hook, reintroduced for this closure pass's live boundary/investigating scenarios, was originally placed AFTER `resolveDroneMovementIntent()` had already computed that tick's movement from the pre-override (stale) position. The same tick's own normal commit then overwrote the override with a result still based on the old position on every single application, making the override silently inert beyond one immediately-discarded write -- confirmed directly by inspecting the in-page trace (a forced target position never once appeared in any recorded sample). Fixed by relocating the override to the very start of `update()`, before any position-dependent computation (perception, movement intent, proposed position) runs.

Reason: this was purely an ordering bug in TEMPORARY, harness-only debug code -- it never shipped, never reached the committed 9F diff, and could not have affected any real player-facing behavior, since the real production movement/constraint/recovery pipeline this hook was merely trying to OBSERVE was never itself modified or bypassed by the bug (the bug only prevented the SETUP step of placing a drone at a specific test position from taking effect; once fixed, the exact same unmodified production code correctly picked up the new starting position and responded to it as designed).

Rejected: silently re-running the affected scenarios without disclosing the bug -- this project's own established discipline (see the bipp5de5f incident's own decisions.md entry) treats verifying and disclosing exactly what went wrong, even in throwaway instrumentation, as required, not optional.

Chosen: fix the ordering, re-run every scenario that had been affected (Sections 3 and 4 of this closure pass) with the corrected hook, and disclose the bug and fix explicitly here and in the changelog rather than quietly presenting only the corrected results.

---

**2026-08-05 -- Milestone 9G: squad attack-permit coordination uses an event-driven prepare/coordinate/commit pipeline, never a per-drone "first to call wins" loop**

Decision: `DroneSquad.tsx`'s fixed-step substep callback gains a THIRD pass between the existing PASS 1 (`writeSpatialSnapshot`) and PASS 2 (`update`): every drone reports a side-effect-free attack-permit REQUEST (`prepareAttackRequest`, a new `DroneHandle` method mirroring `writeSpatialSnapshot`'s own "reused output object, no allocation, no mutation of stored refs" convention) into a shared preallocated array; ONE new pure module, `droneAiSquad.ts`, resolves every request into a permit in a single deterministic pass (`resolveDroneSquadCoordination()`) BEFORE any drone's real `update()`/`decideLegacyDroneAi()` call runs this substep. PASS 2 then consumes the already-resolved permit.

Reason: the brief's own explicit requirement ("do not choose an order-dependent 'first drone to call wins' design") rules out the simplest alternative -- iterating `droneRefs.current` and granting/denying each drone's request as its own `update()` call happens to run, in whatever order the array holds that frame. Any such design would make the outcome depend on array iteration order (itself an implementation detail of React ref-callback registration, not a gameplay-meaningful property), and would require either a second pass anyway (to release stale leases before the first live drone can be evaluated) or accepting a systematic bias toward drones earlier in the array.

Rejected: granting permits inline during each drone's own `update()` call (checking/mutating a shared coordinator ref directly from inside `DroneEnemy.tsx`) -- rejected for the order-dependence reason above, and because it would require `DroneEnemy.tsx` to import and mutate squad-level shared state directly, breaking the established "adapter owns its own per-drone refs, squad-level state lives in DroneSquad.tsx alone" boundary this codebase has held since 9D's `spatialSnapshots`. Rejected: computing every drone's request AND resolving coordination inside `droneAiStateMachine.ts` itself (folding the two concerns into one call) -- rejected because it would need the pure state machine to know about ALL drones simultaneously, a fundamentally different (and untestable, given no React harness) function signature, and would break the file's own single-drone-pure-function contract every phase since 9B has preserved.

Chosen: the three-phase pipeline above. `droneAiSquad.ts`'s own test suite includes an explicit order-independence test (`grant order is INDEPENDENT of the requests array order — reversing the input never changes the result`) as the actual evidence this requirement holds, not just an assertion that it does.

---

**2026-08-05 -- Milestone 9G: `prepareAttackRequest` deliberately DUPLICATES `droneAiStateMachine.ts`'s own attack-block gate predicate, as a disclosed, safety-proven read-only preview**

Decision: `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s new `prepareAttackRequest()` recomputes the same state/stunned/canSeePlayer/recoveryBlocksAttack/reactionReady/cooldown-elapsed conditions the real attack block in `droneAiStateMachine.ts` already checks, ONE tick early (before the coordinator has decided this tick's permit, and therefore before the real `decideLegacyDroneAi()` call that will actually act on it) -- rather than restructuring the pipeline so only one canonical evaluation ever occurs.

Reason: the coordinator MUST know, before granting scarce permits, whether a given drone would even want one this tick -- but the real gate can only be evaluated authoritatively as part of the real per-tick decision, which itself needs to already know the permit result (a permit is one of the abort conditions). Breaking this circularity requires SOME form of early, read-only preview. A key structural property was proven and is what makes the duplication safe rather than a drift risk: for an ALREADY-`attacking` drone, `wantsAttack` (the preview predicate) can only flip false via stun, LOS loss, or recovery becoming active -- because `reactionReadyAtMs`, once passed, is documented (`droneAiTypes.ts`) to never un-satisfy itself, and the `(state === 'attacking' || cooldownElapsed)` clause is trivially true while already attacking. Those three conditions are EXACTLY the same three conditions that already, independently, trigger `droneAiStateMachine.ts`'s own pre-existing abort branch -- so a coordination-driven lease release for an in-progress attack can never happen except co-incident with an already-happening real abort, never as a standalone new abort reason. A wrongly-granted permit (the preview says "wants attack" but the real, live-evaluated gate says no, e.g. a same-tick stun) is similarly harmless: no windup starts, the wasted grant self-corrects because the real state didn't change, and the drone simply re-requests next tick.

Rejected: restructuring `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s `update()` into two synchronous passes per drone (a "decide-without-side-effects" dry run, then a real commit) to avoid duplicating the predicate -- rejected as a much larger, riskier refactor of an already-stable, extensively-tested `update()` method for a benefit (avoiding one small, disclosed, provably-safe duplication) that does not justify the risk to 9B-9F's own established parity guarantees. Rejected: having the coordinator simply grant permits speculatively to EVERY requesting drone up to the cap without any eligibility pre-check, and letting the real per-tick gate silently discard unusable grants -- rejected because it would let a stunned/LOS-lost/recovering drone consume a scarce concurrent-attacker slot and a sector reservation for a tick it could never have used anyway, actively worse for the "controlled rotation of attackers" goal than a slightly-imprecise but generally-accurate preview.

Chosen: the disclosed duplication, with the safety property proven above documented in three places (`droneAiSquad.ts`'s own doc comment, `DroneEnemy.tsx`'s `prepareAttackRequest` doc comment, and here) and confirmed empirically by the Scenario A browser run (0 sticky-lease violations, 0 sector collisions across 50 real samples of live Max-difficulty combat).

---

**2026-08-05 -- Milestone 9G: firing-permit sectors are derived from a drone's CURRENT position at grant time and are NEVER fed back into movement -- confirmed structurally, not just by convention**

Decision: `resolveTacticalSectorIndex()` (`droneAiSquad.ts`) is called ONLY at the moment a NEW lease is granted, reading the candidate's current tactical position (already resolved by 9D's separation/range-band movement, untouched by this phase) -- never recomputed while a lease is held, and never consumed by `DroneEnemy.tsx` for anything beyond the one `coordinationBlocksAttack` boolean folded into the observation. No code path anywhere in `lib/v2/ai/` or `DroneEnemy.tsx` reads `DroneAttackPermit.sector` for any steering/facing/movement purpose.

Reason: this was the brief's own named "CRITICAL SCOPE DECISION" -- a coordinator that repositioned drones toward assigned sectors would be a combat-node/formation system, explicitly out of scope, and would risk fighting 9D's own already-tuned separation/range-banding math. Deriving sectors from wherever combat positioning ALREADY put a drone (rather than assigning drones TO sectors) means the "controlled rotation of attackers" effect emerges entirely from refusing a same-sector permit while one is already held, with zero new movement code.

Rejected: having the coordinator publish a "preferred sector" a drone could optionally steer toward for better spread -- rejected outright per the brief's own explicit prohibition, and because it would reopen exactly the "second implementation of movement priority" risk `droneAiMovementIntent.ts`'s own doc comment already warns against for a DIFFERENT reason (consuming `LegacyDroneMovementMode` rather than re-deriving it).

Chosen: sector-as-metadata-only, enforced structurally (a dedicated `droneAiImportGuards.test.ts` check confirms `droneAiMovementIntent.ts` never references sector/permit/lease/coordination at all, and that `droneAiSquad.ts` declares no sector-to-position/direction/steering-conversion identifier) rather than relying on doc-comment discipline alone.

---

**2026-08-05 -- Milestone 9G: per-difficulty concurrent-attacker caps (Low 2 / Medium 3 / Max 4) chosen to keep every tier a controlled rotation, never every drone at once**

Decision: `MAX_CONCURRENT_ATTACKERS` in `droneAiSquad.ts` is `{ low: 2, medium: 3, max: 4 }` against roster sizes of 5/8/8 respectively.

Reason: the milestone's own closing framing is "a controlled rotation of attackers -- without touching movement, perception memory, windups, damage, or recovery," explicitly contrasted against the pre-9G baseline of "eight independent turrets spotted you at once." A cap of 1 at every tier would remove simultaneous pressure entirely (a real behavior change beyond what the brief asked for -- it does not request nerfing difficulty, only spreading WHEN attacks happen); an unbounded/high cap would fail to meaningfully change the pre-9G behavior at all once enough drones are engaged simultaneously. Low's cap of 2 (against 5 drones) keeps its "learn the arena, more forgiving" identity consistent with `difficulty.ts`'s own existing slower/less-accurate Low tuning. Max's cap of 4 (against 8 drones, half the roster) matches its own existing faster-cooldown/tighter-aim identity without ever approaching "every drone fires at once."

Rejected: a per-difficulty SECTOR count in addition to the attacker cap -- rejected as an unnecessary second difficulty lever; `sectorCount` (fixed at 8 for every tier) is a purely geometric/visual-spread constant, not a combat-difficulty concern, and keeping it invariant avoids a second axis of per-tier tuning with no clear gameplay reason to vary it. Rejected: scaling the cap directly proportional to roster size (e.g. a fixed 50%) -- rejected because Low's 5-drone roster at 50% would round to 2 anyway (no difference), but the REASONING should be "how much simultaneous pressure is appropriate for this difficulty's own identity," not a mechanical roster-size ratio.

Chosen: the table above, verified live via `scenario-f-difficulty-threading.mjs` (three fresh page loads, one per difficulty, confirming the browser-observed `maxConcurrentAttackers` in the running coordinator profile matches exactly: 2/3/4) and via `droneAiSquad.test.ts`'s own profile tests (caps strictly increase with difficulty, and even Max never reaches the full roster).

---

**2026-08-05 -- Milestone 9G: two real test-harness artifacts found during browser validation, both disclosed and fixed -- neither is a production defect**

Decision: two issues surfaced while gathering live evidence for this milestone, both isolated to temporary validation-only code:

1. The phase-gated debug snapshot (`window.__d9gSnap__`, written only while `match.phase === 'active'`, mirroring real production gating exactly) legitimately holds its LAST pre-restart value during the brief `'restarting'`/`'countdown'` window after calling `restart()` -- an initial restart-clears-everything check that polled ONLY this snapshot immediately after `restart()` therefore showed a false "leases NOT cleared" result. Root-caused directly (the real coordinator ref, read via a second, phase-INDEPENDENT temporary accessor added specifically to settle the question, showed `leaseCount`/`grantHistoryCount` both reset to exactly `0` on the very next read after `restart()`, immediately) and confirmed as a polling-target artifact, not a reset-logic bug.
2. A difficulty-threading check that called `restart()` between difficulty switches within one page session produced identical (`Low`-equivalent) caps for all three difficulties, because `matchStore.ts`'s own `selectDifficulty()` is a no-op unless `phase` is `'ready'`/`'booting'`, and `restart()` deliberately does NOT return to `'ready'` (it "keeps" the current selected difficulty by design, per that function's own existing comment) -- so the second and third loop iterations' `selectDifficulty()` calls silently did nothing, and the test was unknowingly re-measuring `'low'` three times.

Reason: both are exactly the kind of throwaway-instrumentation issue this project's own established discipline (see the 9F.1 entries above) requires disclosing rather than silently working around or omitting from the record, even though neither ever touched production code.

Rejected: silently switching to the phase-independent live-ref check or the fresh-page-per-difficulty approach without noting why the first attempt gave a misleading result -- would have hidden a genuine (if harness-only) reasoning error from the record. Rejected: treating either as a real regression requiring a production-code fix -- both root causes are properties of the test harness's OWN polling target/control flow, not of `DroneSquad.tsx`'s real restart branch (independently confirmed correct by the live-ref read) or `matchStore.ts`'s real `selectDifficulty()`/`restart()` behavior (both working exactly as already documented in that file's own comments).

Chosen: fix both test scripts (a phase-independent live-ref accessor for the restart check; three fresh page loads instead of a restart-then-reselect loop for the difficulty check), re-run, and disclose both here rather than only presenting the corrected results.

---

**2026-08-05 -- Milestone 9G.1: the reported 9G test-count arithmetic (1021+50=1071 vs reported 1078) was wrong by exactly 7 -- reconciled precisely, not re-estimated**

Decision: the 9G report's own "+50" breakdown (25 `droneAiSquad.test.ts` + 8 state-machine gate tests + 17 import-guard scope-fence tests) undercounted `droneAiImportGuards.test.ts`'s real delta by exactly 7. Root cause, confirmed by running each changed file individually (both at the committed `9372923` baseline via `git stash` and at the current working tree) rather than re-deriving the number analytically: adding `droneAiSquad.ts` to the file's existing `PURE_CORE_FILES` array (a single array-literal edit) caused the PRE-EXISTING top-of-file loop (`for (const file of PURE_CORE_FILES) { for (label,pattern) {...} it(...) it(...) }`, present since Milestone 9B) to generate 7 ADDITIONAL runtime test instances for the new 10th array entry (5 forbidden-import checks + 1 `performance.now()` check + 1 `Math.random()` check) -- tests generated by code that already existed, invisible to a manual count of `it(` literals written inside the NEW "Milestone 9G scope fence" describe block specifically.

Reason: the original report's methodology (`sed`-anchoring on the new describe block's own boundary, then `grep -c "  it("`) only sees `it(` calls textually present within that anchor -- it cannot see tests a pre-existing loop generates dynamically from a data change made elsewhere in the same file. This is a real, disclosed methodology gap, not a fabricated number: 1021 (committed baseline, re-confirmed via `git stash` + fresh `npm test` run, exactly matching 9F.1's own reported 1021) + 25 + 8 + 7 + 17 = 1078, exactly matching the measured total.

Rejected: re-stating the original "+50" claim with a footnote -- the brief's own instruction was explicit ("Identify the additional seven tests precisely, or correct the inaccurate claim. Do not proceed using unreconciled numbers"), and an approximate explanation would not satisfy "precisely." Rejected: removing `droneAiSquad.ts` from the shared `PURE_CORE_FILES` loop to avoid the count "surprise" -- rejected because that loop's own guarantee (no React/R3F/Three.js/Rapier/Zustand import, no `performance.now()`/`Math.random()` call) is exactly the correct, real property `droneAiSquad.ts` must hold as a pure module, and removing it from that check would weaken real coverage purely to make a test-count arithmetic story simpler.

Chosen: report the precise per-file breakdown (droneAiSquad.test.ts +25, droneAiStateMachine.test.ts +8, droneAiImportGuards.test.ts +24 = 7 from the pre-existing loop + 17 from the new describe block), verified by directly running each file individually at both the committed baseline and the current tree via `git stash`/`git stash pop`, never by re-deriving the number analytically a second time.
